Stochastic Persistence in Infinite Dimensions Juraj Földes was partly supported under the grants: NSF DMS-1816408, Simons foundation SFI-MPS-TSM-00013942, U.S. DOE DE-NA0004269
Juraj Földes, Declan Stacy

Abstract
. Motivated by infinite-dimensional ecological and biological models such as reaction-diffusion SPDEs and stochastic functional differential equations, we develop a general criteria for stochastic persistence (coexistence) in terms of an average lyapunov function, which was previously known only in finite dimensions. To apply our results to SPDEs we analyze the projective process, and we employ a combination of mild (stochastic convolution) and variational (lyapunov function) techniques. Our analysis also requires some nontrivial well-posedness and nonnegativity results for reaction-diffusion SPDEs, which we state and prove in great generality, extending the known results in the literature. Finally, we present several examples including ecological models (Lotka-Volterra), an epidemic model (SIR), and a model for turbulence. Notably we show that, as in the SDE case, coexistence in the Lotka-Volterra model is determined by the invasion rates. Keywords: Stochastic persistence, stochastic reaction-diffusion, stochastic Lotka-Volterra, instability, invariant subsets, Lyapunov function, Lyapunov exponent, average Lyapunov function, invariant measures, SPDEs, functional differential equations, invasion rates 2020 MSC: 37H30, 60H15, 60H30, 60J25, 60J70 60F15, 92D25, 92D30, 37H10, 35R60, 37C75, 92C60.



1.
Introduction

A prominent feature of many dynamical systems is the existence of invariant subsets of the state or phase space. For instance, in physical systems, level sets of energy or momentum are invariant under the dynamics.
Moreover, often in practice and theoretical analysis, the system is affected by random influences which requires mathematical modeling via stochastic dynamical systems. As in the deterministic case, there can be a stochastically invariant set, which we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. For example, in biology
\mathcal{M}_{0}
might represent the subset where a species is extinct, and therefore we refer to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
as an extinction set.

To assess the relevance of such an extinction set
\mathcal{M}_{0}
based on its occurrence in observations, experiments, or numerical computation, one investigates the asymoptotic stability or instability of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. The former means that if the the initial condition is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, then the system converges, as
t\to\infty
, to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. The latter, which is the focus of our paper, captures the idea that trajectories remain bounded away from the extinction set
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

Stochastic persistence (instability) is a central concept in the study of long-term behavior of random dynamical systems. Originally developed in the context of finite-dimensional systems, the theory (and its complement, stochastic extinction) has proven to be a powerful tool for studying population dynamics, epidemiology, turbulence, and chemostat models (
[
Ben23
,
FS24
]
). However, many models of interest in biology, physics, and other applied sciences are naturally infinite-dimensional.
As articulated in
[
AKR+21
]
, “the spread of disease may be faster in one part of the population and slower in the other part … the spatial effect cannot be ignored.” Thus, models incorporating spatial dependency (SPDEs) can be more realistic than classical SDE models. The same applies for models where future behavior depends on (part of) the past, not just on the present state. As articulated in
[
NNY21
]
, “the delays or past dependence are unavoidable in natural phenomena
and dynamical systems; the framework of stochastic functional differential equations is more realistic, more effective, and more general for the population dynamics in real life than a stochastic differential equation counterpart.”

In the deterministic setting, there is a vast literature analyzing the persistence and stability of reaction-diffusion PDEs (see for example
[
KKB13
,
WSW19
,
LL14
,
VP25
,
Lee24
,
TM21
,
HX20
,
LL22
]
). Although recently there has been some treatment of the stochastic case for various examples, see
[
KKMS23
,
SKJ25
,
HH20
,
BF20
,
NNY20
,
LZ23
,
NY21
]
, there is no unified theory.
The main goal of our paper is to
develop a general framework for infinite dimensional stochastic dynamical systems that is readily applicable to
stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs), which incorporate spatial dependency, and stochastic functional differential equations, which account for delayed effects.
We stress that
several crucial arguments used in the finite-dimensional case no longer apply in infinite dimensions, and therefore
we propose a new approach to investigate infinite dimensional stochastic persistence.

More specifically, we prove under general conditions that infinite-dimensional stochastic systems are persistent if an “average Lyapunov exponent” is negative and give its formula
(see below for precise definitions). For stochastic systems, the average Lyapunov exponent is often hard to compute explicitly, so we also show the following perturbation result: if the average Lyapunov exponent of the corresponding deterministic system is negative, then the stochastic system is persistent if the random forcing is sufficiently weak.
Afterwards we apply our main abstract result to stochastic functional (delayed) differential equations, where our results considerably simplify arguments in
[
NNY21
]
and allow for deeper understanding of the results.

The analysis of SPDEs is quite challenging due to different functional frameworks, notions or solutions, and where the change of one sign can lead to blow-up solutions.
We devote our attention to SPDEs with
nonnegative solutions, which are the most relevant for biological applications. We combine the Lyapunov function method with powerful tools from semigroup theory to prove
persistence results for a general class of SPDEs under moderate assumptions. Then, in many examples, we provide criteria that are sharp at the heuristic level; their sharpness will be established rigorously through a complementary extinction theory developed in a forthcoming paper.
Additionally, our conditions on SPDEs allow for noise which is much closer to white noise compared to the usual Hilbert-Schmidt assumption. However, in order to treat such stochastic forcing, we have to prove well-posedness, non-negativity, and certain Itô formulas for mild solutions, which extend the works
[
Cer03
,
NY19
,
KvN12
]
and are
interesting in their own right. We remark that proofs of many technical and abstract results (especially in
Section

6.2
) can be omitted on the first reading. The reader who is less concerned with the dynamics and primarily interested in general SPDE theory can find the contemporary analysis of stochastic reaction-diffusion equations by reading
Section

6.2
and the preceding subsections in
Section

6
.

At the conceptual level,
the contribution of the present manuscript is twofold. First, we introduce new techniques for obtaining asymptotic stability of infinite-dimensional systems, which are necessary for accurate analysis of models, for example in epidemics.
Second, we further the development of the average Lyapunov function approach, which has proven to be optimal for analyzing the stability of fixed points and, more generally, invariant subsets of (random) dynamical systems (see the discussion in the introduction of
[
FS24
]
). From a broad perspective, the average Lyaupov function method allows us to linearize the problem about the invariant subset
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. The linearized problems are easier to analyze, as their behavior depends only upon a single quantity: the average Lyapunov exponent. Despite its abstract definition as
\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H
(see
4
(i)
), the average Lyapunov exponent is a familiar concept in concrete contexts. For example, in spatially homogeneous (finite dimensional) ecological models the average Lyapunov exponent has a natural interpretation as an “invasion rate” (
[
Ben23
,
NNY21
,
HN18
,
BS19
,
HNC21
]
). Our method allows us to extend the notion of the invasion rate to spatially dependent (infinite-dimensional) contexts, which is higly non-trivial (see
7.20
). For deterministic PDEs with a fixed point, the average Lyapunov exponent is the principal eigenvalue which we express as the maximum of a Rayleigh quotient. Thus our results in
Section

6.5
can be viewed as a generalization of the classical notion of the principal eigenvalue to the stochastic setting (see
7.4
). Overall, our results allow us to leverage the power of linearization to analyze the long-term behavior of more realistic applied models compared to the known procedures in the literature.

Compared to the traditional Lyapunov function approach, which require certain conditions on the generator of the Markov process to hold at every point, average Lyapunov functions only look at averages with respect to invariant measures on the subset
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. In applications, average Lyapunov functions are thus much easier to construct and are less dependent on the specific form of the system. On the other hand, establishing properties of
stochastic dynamical systems under assumptions on averaged rather than traditional Lyapunov functions is much more challenging and it is the purpose of the present paper. The essential step
to prove stochastic persistence is to formulate conditions which guarantee that a certain family of random measures
\mu_{t}
(the empirical occupation measures) are tight almost surely, and that a certain unbounded function
H
satisfies
\int Hd\mu_{t}\to\int Hd\mu
if
\mu_{t}\to\mu
(see precise definitions below). The key conditions in
[
Ben23
,
FS24
]
require the existence of functions
W,W^{\prime}
and a constant
K>0
which satisfy, among other assumptions:

(i)

\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the infinitesimal generator.

(ii)

W^{\prime}
has compact sublevel sets.

(iii)

W^{\prime}
is a lot bigger than
|H|
.

For finite-dimensional systems, finding such
W,W^{\prime}
in applications is usually quite natural, however, it is impossible in infinite-dimensional Banach spaces, where continuous functions with compact sublevel sets do not exist. In addition, SPDEs and SDEs with delay present their own additional challenges for proving the tightness and convergence results (for
\mu_{t}
). In particular, (i) is very hard to establish for SPDEs defined on the unit sphere in a Banach space (Lyapunov exponent methods naturally introduce a polar change of coordinates, which leads to equations on sphere), while (ii)-(iii) are difficult to prove for SDEs with delay.

To track the history for SDEs with delayed effects up to
r>0
units of time into the past, the natural state space is
C([-r,0],\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the set of all continuous functions from
[-r,0]
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. One criterion for compactness in
C([-r,0],\mathbb{R}^{n})
, see for example Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, is a uniform bound on the Hölder continuity of
[T-r,T]\ni t\mapsto X_{t}
. Inspired by
[
NNY21
]
, instead of requiring (ii), we require the process to belong a compact set on the event that
W(X_{t})
is uniformly bounded for a suitably long period of time, a condition, which is easily verified via Kolmogorov continuity criterion. We also replace (iii) with the assumption that
\int_{0}^{r}|H|
is a lot smaller than
\int_{0}^{r}W^{\prime}
. Then, the proof of tightness of
\mu_{t}
and convergence of
\int Hd\mu_{t}
resembles
[
NNY21
, Section 3]
, but
by adapting the arguments in
[
Ben23
]

we avoid the 13-14 pages of arguments in
[
NNY21
, Section 4]
. Also, our results are in some sense more fundamental, because they also apply to SPDEs.

For parabolic SPDEs we consider systems of
m
equations such that the nonlinearity is dissipative. For these equations, one can often use energy estimates to verify (i); for example, take
W
to be some norm (for example,
W(u)=\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
) and
W^{\prime}
to be a stronger norm (like
\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
).
For our applications in physics or biology (and to avoid complicated geometrical and topological arguments) we
take
\mathcal{M}_{0}
to be a subspace where some components
1,\ldots,d
, with
d<n
are equal to zero. Then polar coordinates in the first
d
components are natural as they keep track of the distance of the process to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.
Specifically, if we denote
u_{t}
the projection of our process on the first
d
coordinates, we define the norm of our process
u_{t}
given by
r_{t}=\|u_{t}\|
and the angle
v_{t}=u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|
. However, since the polar transformation is degenerate at the origin (if
r=0
, then the angle can be arbitrary), we must also “blow up”
\mathcal{M}_{0}
so that
\{u=0\}
is replaced by the sphere
\{r=0\}
(
v
arbitrary) – otherwise, it is not possible to define a certain function (
H
, the generator applied to our average Lyapunov function) continuously. After enlarging our state space in this way, the energy estimates no longer give us (i).
Indeed, since
\|v_{t}\|
is constant, it does not satisfy a dissipative problem.

The problem of obtaining tightness of empirical occupation measures for the projective process
(r_{t},v_{t})
is quite difficult
[
HR23
,
HPSRY24
]
, in particular measures supported on
r=0
. For example, consider the heat equation
u_{t}=\Delta u
. Since there is an invariant measure (for
v_{t}
) supported on any eigenfunction direction,
it is easy to see that the set of all invariant measures is not compact. On the other hand, the assumptions for finite dimensional problems in
[
Ben23
,
FS24
]
(especially assumption (i) above) imply that the set of invariant measures is compact. Thus, it is not possible to use the same techniques as in the finite-dimensional case to handle these projective processes. Even in this manuscript, our assumptions imply compactness of the set of all invariant measures for the projective process on
\{r=0\}
, which for a general SPDE would require some non-degeneracy assumption on the noise.

We avoid these issues by looking only at the cone of nonnegative functions. By recalling our example of the heat equation from above, we note that for nonnegative solutions there is only one invariant measure for the projective process (corresponding to the largest eigenvalue of the laplacian), because eigenfunctions corresponding to higher frequencies are not nonnegative. Since non-negativity is a natural assumption in biological models (negative population sizes are usually not considered), this restriction covers many important applications.

Even with this positivity assumption, however, showing compactness is quite hard and involves the combination of several Lyapunov functions (a variational method) with novel techniques involving the mild formulation of SPDEs (semigroup methods). Our results improve upon those in
[
KKMS23
,
SKJ25
]
because in addition to proving the required tightness, we also provide an explicit formula for the Lyapunov exponent which guarantees persistence, as opposed to just bounds.

Also, our general Lyapunov functions apply to variety of SPDEs and in this paper we present details for the following problems:

•

A logistic growth model with harvesting on a domain (see
Section

7.1
) with multiplicative noise, which is a stochastic version of the deterministic model analyzed in
[
KKB13
]
. We formulate criteria for the survival of all species.

•

A model for turbulence with (multiplicative) space-time white noise on a torus
[
KKMS23
,
ZTPSM00
]
(see
Section

7.2
). We show that when the noise is small enough, there are multiple invariant measures.

•

A SIR epidemic model on a domain with multiplicative noise
[
AYI+23
,
SKJ25
,
NNY20
,
NY19
]
(see
Section

7.3
). We formulate criteria that guarantee that the disease is endemic. Whereas the extinction set in the previous examples was the fixed point
0
, this extinction set is more complicated, further illustrating the power of our theory. Additionally, unlike in the cited papers, we obtain almost-sure results about persistence of the disease, as opposed to bounds on certain expectations.

•

Competitive Lotka-Volterra models on a domain with a variety of noise options. Whereas
[
NY21
]
focuses on extinction, we are able to prove coexistence results. We encourage the reader to read
[
NY21
, 7.3. Discussion]
, which outlines the difficulties in applying the “growth rate method” or the “eigenvalue method” to SPDEs. In this paper, we are able to perform both.

We do not provide examples for stochastic functional differential equations, as there are many in
[
NNY21
, Section 5]
and although our method provides a shorter proof, it does not improve known results in these cases.

We remark that under the positivity preserving assumption one can introduce the Cayley-Hilbert metric on a simplex
and show contractivity for Markov semigroups corresponding to linear or homogeneous problems
as in
[
BLSS25
,
Bus86
,
CF24
]
. Thus, one could try to prove
\mu_{t}\to\mu
by generalizing these methods, although it is not clear how to establish the contractivity for the non-homegeneus, non-linear problem in infinite dimensions that we analyze here. We opted for a different approach which is more in line with
[
Ben23
]
and generalizes also to the stochastic functional differential equations case.

To summarize, the present manuscript pushes the boundary of the current theory of stochastic persistence by providing a unified framework for treating a wide variety of infinite-dimensional systems. After formulating our setup and
1
–
6
in
Section

2
, we state and prove our general results in
Section

3
and
Section

4
. In
Section

5
we show how to verify
1
–
6
for stochastic functional differential equations. In
Section

6
, we consider a general stochastic partial differential equation with nonnegative solutions and formulate assumptions which guarantee that
1
–
6
hold, as well as prove novel results relating to well-posedness and the weak maximum principle. This is where we construct our newly developed Lyapunov functions and “upgrade” the control that we obtain using the semigroup (mild) approach. Finally, in
Section

7
we illustrate how our general results apply to SPDE models for population dynamics, turbulence, and epidemics.
The reader primarily interested in examples can go straight to
Section

7
after reading the introduction of
Section

6
.

2.
Definitions and Assumptions

Let
(\mathcal{M},d)
be a Polish (complete and separable) metric space which is not necessarily locally compact,
\mathcal{M}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}
be a closed set, and
\mathcal{M}_{+}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
(where
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
). The set
\mathcal{M}_{0}
can be viewed as an “extinction” set and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
as a “persistence” set.
We endow
\mathcal{M}
with the Borel
\sigma
- algebra. For each
x\in\mathcal{M}
, let
X_{t}^{x}
be a homogeneous Markov process defined on
\mathcal{M}
which has cadlag sample paths. By this we mean that there is a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
(which we assume is complete and right-continuous) and a family of
\mathcal{M}-
valued random variables
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
such that:

•

X_{0}^{x}=x
and
t\mapsto X_{t}^{x}
is cadlag (right continuous with left limits) a.s.

•

X^{x}_{\cdot}
is adapted to
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, meaning
X_{t}^{x}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
measurable for each
t\geq 0
.

•

For all bounded measurable functions
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
, the map

[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{M}\ni(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{t}f(x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t}^{x})]

is measurable and for any
s,t\geq 0
we assume (homogeneity)
that

\mathcal{P}_{s}f(X_{t}^{x})=\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t+s}^{x})|\mathcal{F}_{t}]\,.

It is standard to prove that
(\mathcal{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defines
a semigroup: for any
s,t\geq 0
it holds that
\mathcal{P}_{s+t}f=\mathcal{P}_{s}\mathcal{P}_{t}f
. Also, the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{t}f
makes sense as long as
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable and bounded from below (or above), possibly attaining the value
\infty
(or
-\infty
).

2.1.
Invariant Sets and the Feller Property

Next, we list two basic assumptions on our process
X_{t}^{x}
and the sets
\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+}
. First, we suppose that the sets
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
are invariant, that is,
if an initial condition is in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
(respectively
\mathcal{M}_{+}
) then
X_{t}^{x}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
(respectively
\mathcal{M}_{+}
) for all
t\geq 0
.
In general, we have the following definition.

Definition 2.1
.

A measurable set
A\subset\mathcal{M}
is invariant if
x\in A
implies that almost surely
X_{t}^{x}\in A
for all
t\geq 0
.

Assumption 1
.

\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
are invariant.

Second, we assume a standard continuity on the law of
X_{t}^{x}
as a function of the initial condition
x
and time
t
. Such a condition is commonly known as the “Feller” property.
Let
C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
denote the space of bounded continuous functions on
\mathcal{M}
endowed with the supremum norm
\|f\|=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}|f(x)|
. The following continuity assumption is vital for our analysis:

Assumption 2
.

Assume that
X_{t}^{x}
is Feller in the sense that for any
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
t\geq 0
,
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
, and also
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
pointwise as
t\downarrow 0
.

Remark 2.2
.

Note that the requirement of right-continuity in time (
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
pointwise as
t\downarrow 0
) is automatically satisfied since
X^{x}_{t}
is assumed to have cadlag paths.

2.2.
Two Important Martingales

In this section we introduce important definitions that are used in
Section

2.3
, as well as two martingales that are crucial to our analysis.

Two central operators in the study of Feller processes are the generator
\mathcal{L}
and Carre du Champ
\Gamma
of the Markov semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{t}
, which are discussed rigorously in
[
FS24
]
. For a suitably nice function
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{L}f(x)
represents the average rate of change of
\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t}^{x})]
at
t=0
, so that
M_{t}^{f}
defined as :

(2.1)

M_{t}^{f}(x)\coloneqq f(X_{t}^{x})-f(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}f(X_{s}^{x})ds

is a martingale. Analogously,

\Gamma f(x)
represents the average rate of change of the quadratic variation of (
2.1
) at
t=0
, so that the following process is a martingale:

(2.2)

(M^{f}_{t}(x))^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds\,.

For readers familiar with Itô calculus, we remark that
\mathcal{L}f
(resp.
\Gamma f
) is the
coefficient of the “
dt
” part of “
df
” (resp. “
(df)^{2}
”). This interpretation is useful when computing terms like
\mathcal{L}f^{k}
in the context of SPDEs, where one can utilize Itô’s formula.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{M}
be an open invariant set (see
2.1
). We let
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
be the set of all measurable functions
f:A\to[0,\infty)
for which there exists a measurable function
\mathcal{L}f:A\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
such that
M^{f}_{\cdot}(x)

in (
2.1
) is a cadlag local martingale for all
x\in A
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{M}
be an open invariant set (see
2.1
). We let
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
be set of all measurable functions
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
for which there exist measurable functions
\mathcal{L}f:A\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
and
\Gamma f:A\to[0,\infty)
such that
M^{f}_{\cdot}(x)

in (
2.1
) is a cadlag square integrable martingale and the stochastic process in (
2.2
) is a martingale for all
x\in A
.

Remark 2.5
.

Note that in
2.3
and
2.4
we allow
\mathcal{L}f
take the value
-\infty
, but it is implicit in the definitions that
s\mapsto\mathcal{L}f(X_{s}^{x})
must be in
L^{1}([0,t])
almost surely for all
t>0
. Allowing infinite values is crucial for our study of SPDEs, where
f
is a norm and
\mathcal{L}f
includes a term which is the negative of some stronger norm.

2.3.
Lyapunov Functions

In this section we provide the assumptions necessary for our main results. In
3
we suppose that there is a Lyapunov function which is large near spatial infinity. In conjunction with
6
, it allows us to prove tightness of appropriate measures.
4
is crucial as it gives the existence of an “average Lyapunov function” that forces
X_{t}^{x}
to (on average) move away from
\mathcal{M}_{0}
if
X_{t}^{x}
is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.
5
guarantees that the variances of the mentioned Lyapunov functions evaluated at
X_{t}^{x}
have a controlled growth in time.

Before proceeding, we precisely define what we mean if we write that one function is “smaller” than another.

Definition 2.6
.

For an arbitrary set
A
and functions
f,g:A\to[-\infty,\infty]
, we write
f\lesssim g
if there is some
C>0
such that
f(x)\leq Cg(x)
for all
x\in A
.

Definition 2.7
.

For an arbitrary set
A
and functions
f,g:A\to[-\infty,\infty]
, we write
f<<g
if
\sup_{\{x\in A\mid|g(x)|\leq M\}}|f(x)|<\infty
for all
M>0
.

Remark 2.8
.

For example, if
g
is proper (continuous with compact sublevel sets) and
f
is continuous then
f<<g
. Also, if
f\lesssim g^{k}
for some
k>0
, then
f<<g
.

Assumption 3
.

There exist measurable functions
W:\mathcal{M}\to[1,\infty)
,
W^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to[1,\infty]
, and a constant
K>0
such that:

(1)

W\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
(see
2.4
).

(2)

\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
.

(3)

W<<W^{\prime}
(see
2.7
).

Definition 2.9
.

For an arbitrary set
A
and functions
f:A\to[-\infty,\infty]
and
g:A\to[0,\infty]
, we say
f
vanishes over
g
if one of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(1)

\forall\epsilon>0,\exists M>0
such that
|f|\mathbbm{1}_{|f|>M}\leq\epsilon g

(2)

\forall\epsilon>0,\exists N>0
such that
|f|\leq\epsilon g+N

Remark 2.10
.

Note that
2.9
is different than the definition given in
[
FS24
, Definition 2.18]
.

We define a similar notion for collections of random variables:

Definition 2.11
.

For collections of random variables
\{X_{t}\},\{Y_{t}\}
defined on the same probability space and
Y_{t}>0
a.s., we say
\{X_{t}\}
vanishes over
\{Y_{t}\}
if one of the following equivalent conditions hold:

(1)

\forall\epsilon>0,\exists M>0
such that almost surely
|X_{t}|\mathbbm{1}_{|X_{t}|>M}\leq\epsilon Y_{t}
for all
t
.

(2)

\forall\epsilon>0,\exists N>0
such that almost surely
|X_{t}|\leq N+\epsilon Y_{t}
for all
t
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
P(\mathcal{M})
denote the set of all Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{M}
.
For any
\mu\in P(\mathcal{M})
,
S\subset\mathcal{M}
measurable, and
f:S\to\mathbb{R}
measurable, we define

\mu f:=\int_{S}fd\mu\,.

We endow
P(\mathcal{M})
with topology of weak convergence, that is,
\mu_{n}\to\mu
if for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mu_{n}f\to\mu f
.

Definition 2.13
.

For
F\subset\mathcal{M}
an invariant set (see
2.1
), let
P_{inv}(F)\subset P(\mathcal{M})
denote the set of invariant measures on
F
, that is,
\mu\in P_{inv}(F)
if
\mu(F)=1
and
\mu\mathcal{P}_{t}f=\mu f
for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
t\geq 0
.

Definition 2.14
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
t>0
let
\mu_{t}^{x}
denote the empirical occupation measure

(2.3)

\mu_{t}^{x}(\omega)\coloneqq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{X_{s}^{x}(\omega)}ds,

where
\delta_{y}
is the Dirac measure concentrated at
y
, that is,
\delta_{y}f=f(y)
. Note that
\omega\in\Omega
, and therefore
\mu_{t}^{x}
is a random measure which depends on
\omega
, but often this dependence is suppressed.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be the set of all
f:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty]
such that there exists
K>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}f\leq K\quad a.s.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be the set of all
g:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty]
such that there exists
K>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\mu_{t}^{x}g]\leq K

Assumption 4
.

There is a nonnegative continuous function
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
such that:

(i)

\mathcal{L}V
extends to a continuous function
H:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
and there is a constant
\Lambda<0
such that
\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H\leq\Lambda<0
.

(ii)

There is a positive finite measure
\mu
on
[-r,0]
for some
r\geq 0
and
f\in\mathcal{X}
such that for every
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
\{H(X_{t}^{x})\}_{t>r,x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}
vanishes over the function
\{\int f(X_{t+s}^{x})d\mu(s)\}_{t>r,x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}
(see
2.11
).

Remark 2.16
.

It is shown below in
4.12
that under our assumptions,
H
is
\mu
-integrable for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
, so the condition
\mu H\leq\Lambda
in
4
(i)
is well defined.

Remark 2.17
.

4
(ii)
is a generalization of
[
FS24
, Assumption 4(ii)]
(see also
[
Ben23
, Hypothesis 4(ii)]
), which corresponds to
\mu=\delta_{0}
. Indeed, it will be shown in
2.20
that
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
. This generalization is necessary for treating models with delayed effects such as those analyzed in
[
NNY21
]
.

4
(i)
is the most important assumption. It implies that
V
decreases (on average) when
X_{t}
is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Since
V
is nonnegative, this implies that
X_{t}
cannot spend too much time near
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

The following
5
ensures that
M_{t}^{V}
and
M_{t}^{W}
(see (
2.1
)) satisfy the strong law of large numbers for martingales, as stated in
2.19
. In fact,
5
can be replaced by
2.19
. However, in practice
5
often follows from
3
, and therefore, we assume
5
rather than
2.19
.

Assumption 5
.

Let
W
and
V
be respectively as in
3
and
4
. Assume that
there are measurable functions
U:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty)
,
U^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty]
, a constant
K>0
, and some
g\in\mathcal{Y}
such that:

(i)

U\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
(see
2.3
).

(ii)

\mathcal{L}U\leq K-U^{\prime}
.

(iii)

\Gamma W\leq g
.

(iv)

\Gamma V\leq g
.

Lemma 2.18
.

U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
. In particular, we may replace
g
with
KU^{\prime}
in
5
.

Proof.

See
[
FS24
, Lemma 4.1]
.
∎

A direct consequence of
5
is the following law of large numbers:

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
W,V,U
be as in
3
,
4
,
5
.
For any
x\in\mathcal{M}
we have

(2.4)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{M_{t}^{W}(x)}{t}=0\qquad\textrm{a.s.}

and for any
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
we have

(2.5)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{M_{t}^{V}(x)}{t}=0\qquad\textrm{a.s.}\,,

where
M^{W}_{t}
and
M^{V}_{t}
were defined in (
2.1
).

Proof.

See the arguments in
[
FS24
, Lemma 2.12]
and
[
FS24
, Corollary 4.2]
.
∎

Corollary 2.20
.

W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
(see
3
,
2.15
).

Proof.

By
2.19
,
\frac{-W(X_{t}^{x})}{t}+\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{L}W)\to 0
a.s. as
t\to\infty
. Moreover, from
W\geq 0
and
\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
(see
3
) it follows that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{W(X_{t}^{x})}{t}=\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{L}W)\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}K-\mu_{t}^{x}W^{\prime}\,.

∎

If
W^{\prime}
has compact sublevel sets, as assumed in
[
FS24
]
and
[
Ben23
]
, then
3
–
5
would be enough to guarantee stochastic persistence of
X_{t}
(see
3.1
below). However, in most infinite-dimensional contexts such as stochastic functional equations arising from biological systems with delay (
Section

5
) and stochastic PDEs (see
Section

7
), the compactness of sublevel sets cannot be obtained, and replace it by the following weaker assumption.

Assumption 6
.

There is some
f\in\mathcal{X}
(
2.15
) such that for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
T>0
such that for all
M
sufficiently large there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
which satisfies (regadless of initial condition)

(2.6)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mu_{T}(\mathcal{K}^{c})>\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{t})dt+\sup_{t\leq T}W(X_{t})>M\Big)+\epsilon\,.

In other words, if
f(X^{x}_{t})
and
W(X^{x}_{t})
are small for a long time, then with high probability,
X_{t}
spent most of that time in the compact set
\mathcal{K}
.

Remark 2.21
.

If there exists some
f\in\mathcal{X}
which has compact sublevel sets, then
6
is satisfied. Indeed, for any
M
and any
T
,
\mathcal{K}=\{W^{\prime}\leq M/(T\epsilon)\}
is compact and on the event
\{\int_{0}^{T}W^{\prime}(X_{t})dt\leq M\}
we have

\mu_{T}(\mathcal{K}^{c})=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{f(X_{t})>M/(T\epsilon)}dt\leq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\epsilon}{M}f(X_{t})dt\leq\epsilon\,.

Hence,

(2.7)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mu_{T}(\mathcal{K}^{c})>\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{t})dt>M\Big)

and (
2.6
) follows from
W\geq 0
.

Along with
2.20
, the following lemma is instrumental for proving the existence of the
f\in\mathcal{X}
required for
4
(ii)
and
6
. It is proved below in
Section

4
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
f_{0}\coloneqq(W^{\prime})^{1/2}
(see
3
) and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
be functions satisfying the following. For
i=1,\dots,n
, some
T>0
, and all
x\in\mathcal{M}
:

•

h_{i}
vanishes over
f_{i}
.

•

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}f_{i}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}dt\Big]<\infty
for all
t\geq T
.

•

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(X_{t}^{x})dt\Big]

\displaystyle\leq h_{i}(x)+f_{i-1}(x)+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i-1}(X_{t}^{x})+h_{i}(X_{t}^{x})dt\Big]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}dt\Big]

\displaystyle\leq h_{i}(x)+f_{i-1}(x)^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i-1}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}+h_{i}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}dt\Big]\,.

Then
f_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
and
f_{n}^{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Remark 2.23
.

It will clear from the proof of
2.22
that if we had started with
f_{0}=U^{\prime}
(see
5
) and assumed only the first inequality of (
2.8
) then we could conclude that
f_{n}\in\mathcal{Y}
. However, this will not be needed for our examples.

3.
Main General Results

Theorem 3.1
.

Under our
1
–
6
, the following statements hold:

(i)

For all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, almost surely all limit points
\mu
of
\mu_{t}^{x}
as
t\to\infty
satisfy
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
.

(ii)

X_{t}
is stochastically persistent, meaning for all
\epsilon>0
there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}

\mathbb{P}(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon})\geq 1-\epsilon)=1\,.

(iii)

P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
is nonempty.

Remark 3.2
.

We believe (iii) and a subsequential version of (i) would still hold under weaker assumptions. In particular, in
4
(ii)
and
6
, consider replacing the space
\mathcal{X}
with
\mathcal{Y}
. For
g\in\mathcal{Y}
, Fatou’s Lemma gives that
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}g<\infty
, and then all of our proofs should go through by working on a subsequence
t_{n}
which attains the
\liminf
.

The proof is based on two key lemmas which are proved in
Section

4
below.

Lemma 3.3
.

Under the assumptions of
3.1
, almost surely
(\mu_{t_{n}}^{x})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight for all
t_{n}\to\infty
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Under the assumptions of
3.1
, there is
A>0
such that, almost surely,
t_{n}\to\infty
and
\mu_{n}\coloneqq\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}\to\mu\in P(M)
implies
\mu H=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}H
and
\mu|H|\leq A
.

We also have the following theorem, which is a version of
[
FS24
, Theorem 3.11]
in our setting. It states that the average Lyapunov exponent
\Lambda
is (semi-)continuous in the parameters governing the process
X_{t}
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{0}
) decomposes as
\Theta\times\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
(resp.
\Theta\times\tilde{\mathcal{M}_{0}}
), where
\Theta
is a compact metric space such that
\{\theta\}\times\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is an invariant set (
2.1
) for every
\theta\in\Theta
.

Fix some
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
. Suppose
1
–
6
hold, except
4
(i)
is replaced with: “
\mathcal{L}V
extends to a continuous function
H:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
and there is a constant
\Lambda<0
such that
\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\{\theta_{0}\}\times\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H\leq\Lambda<0
.” Then for all
\theta
sufficiently close to
\theta_{0}
, the results of
3.1
hold on
\{\theta\}\times\mathcal{M}
. To be more specific, let
\{Y_{t}^{x}\}_{t\geq 0,x\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}}
be the Markov process on
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
such that
(\theta,Y_{t}^{x})=X_{t}^{(\theta,x)}
(this is well-defined because
\{\theta\}\times\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is an invariant set). Then we have:

(i)

For all
x\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{+}\coloneqq\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{c}
we have that, almost surely, all limit points
\mu
of
\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{x}\coloneqq(1/t)\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{Y_{s}^{x}(\omega)}ds
as
t\to\infty
satisfy
\mu(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{+})=1
.

(ii)

Y_{t}
is stochastically persistent, meaning for all
\epsilon>0
there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{+}
such that for all
x\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{+}

\mathbb{P}(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon})\geq 1-\epsilon)=1\,.

(iii)

P_{inv}(\{\theta\}\times\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{+})
is nonempty.

Proof.

It is enough to show that

\limsup_{\theta\to\theta_{0}}\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\{\theta\}\times\tilde{\mathcal{M}_{0}})}\mu H\leq\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\{\theta_{0}\}\times\tilde{\mathcal{M}_{0}})}\mu H\,.

The proof is exactly the same as the proof of
[
FS24
, Theorem 3.11]
, with
4.11
and
4.12
used in place of the first and second (respectively) applications of
[
FS24
, Lemma 2.22]
.
∎

4.
Proofs

In this section, we assume that
1
–
6
hold.

4.1.
Proof of
2.22

Recall the set
\mathcal{X}
defined in
2.15
. First we show some preliminary lemmas which will be important for the proofs below in
Section

4
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For any
T>0
,
x\in\mathcal{M}
, and
f:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty]
satisfying
\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{t}^{x})dt<\infty
a.s., we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}f

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{nT}\int_{0}^{r+nT}f(X_{s}^{x})dsdr

\displaystyle=\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})drds\,.

In addition, if
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{t}^{x})dt]<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\mu_{t}^{x}f]

\displaystyle=\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})drds\Big]

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{nT}\int_{0}^{r+nT}f(X_{s}^{x})dsdr\Big]\,.

Proof.

The claims follow from Tonelli’s theorem. Indeed,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{nT}\int_{0}^{r+nT}f(X_{s}^{x})dsdr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nT^{2}}\int_{0}^{(n+1)T}\int_{(s-nT)\vee 0}^{T}f(X_{s}^{x})drds

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{nT}\int_{0}^{(n+1)T}f(X_{s}^{x})ds=\frac{n+1}{n}\mu_{(n+1)T}^{x}f

and by passing
n\to\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{nT}\int_{0}^{r+nT}f(X_{s}^{x})dsdr\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{n+1}{n}\mu_{(n+1)T}^{x}f\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}f\,.

On the other hand, let
(t_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}f=\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}f.

For each
n\geq 1
let
k_{n}
be such that

(k_{n}-1)T\leq t_{n}<k_{n}T
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k_{n}T^{2}}\int_{0}^{(k_{n}+1)T}\int_{(s-k_{n}T)\vee 0}^{T}f(X_{s}^{x})drds

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{k_{n}T}\int_{0}^{k_{n}T}f(X_{s}^{x})ds

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{k_{n}T}\int_{0}^{t_{n}}f(X_{s}^{x})ds=\frac{t_{n}}{k_{n}T}\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}f

and by passing
n\to\infty
we obtain

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{nT}\int_{0}^{r+nT}f(X_{s}^{x})dsdr\\
\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{k_{n}T^{2}}\int_{0}^{(k_{n}+1)T}\int_{(s-k_{n}T)\vee 0}^{T}f(X_{s}^{x})drds\geq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}f

and the first equality follows. To prove the second equality, we
use the substitution
z=s+t
and Tonelli’s theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{tT}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})drds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{tT}\int_{0}^{T}zf(X_{z}^{x})dz+\frac{1}{t}\int_{T}^{t}f(X_{z}^{x})dz

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{tT}\int_{t}^{t+T}(t+T-z)f(X_{z}^{x})dz\,,

Since
z\leq T
in the first integral, then
f\geq 0
implies

0\leq\frac{1}{tT}\int_{0}^{T}zf(X_{z}^{x})dz\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{z}^{x})dz\,.

Since
z\in[t,t+T]
in the third integral, then
f\geq 0
yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{tT}\int_{t}^{t+T}(t+T-z)f(X_{z}^{x})dz\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{t}^{t+T}f(X_{z}^{x})dz\,.

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\int_{T}^{t}f(X_{z}^{x})dz\leq\frac{1}{tT}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})drds\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t+T}f(X_{z}^{x})dz\,,

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{x}f-\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{z}^{x})dz\leq\frac{1}{tT}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})drds\leq\frac{T+t}{t}\mu_{t}^{x}f

and the first assertion follows after passing
t\to\infty
(in
\limsup
) and using that
\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{t}^{x})dt<\infty
.

The second statement follows analogously.

∎

Corollary 4.2
.

For all
f\in\mathcal{X}
,
\epsilon>0
,
T>0
, there exists
M>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})dr>M\}}ds\leq\epsilon\quad a.s.

Proof.

With
K
as in
2.15
, by
4.1
,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})drds\leq KT\quad a.s.

Since

\mathbbm{1}_{\{\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r}^{x})dr>M\}}\leq\frac{1}{M}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r}^{x})dr\,,

the claim follows with
M=KT/\epsilon
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

Recall that
\mathbb{D}([0,T],\mathcal{M})
is the Skorokhod space (see
[
FS24
, Lemma 5.2]
and the discussion preceding it).

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
T>0
and assume
f,g:\mathbb{D}([0,T],\mathcal{M})\to[0,\infty]
satisfy for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f(\{X_{s}^{x}\}_{s\leq T})]\leq\mathbb{E}[g(\{X_{s}^{x}\}_{s\leq T})]\,,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}f(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})^{2}+g(\{X_{s}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})^{2}ds\Big]<\infty\,.

Then

(4.3)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}f(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}g(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds\,.

Furthermore, if
f
has the form
f(\phi)=\int_{0}^{T}\hat{f}(\phi(s))ds
where
\hat{f}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty]
,
g_{2}:\mathbb{D}([0,T],\mathcal{M})\to[0,\infty)
satisfies
g(\phi)\leq\frac{T}{2}\hat{f}(\phi(0))+g_{2}(\phi)
, and (
4.2
) also holds with
g
replaced by
g_{2}
, then

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\hat{f}(X_{s}^{x})ds\leq\frac{2}{T}\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}g_{2}(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds\,.

Proof.

First we introduce the following notation for
t\geq 0
,
t_{0}\in[0,T]
,
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and any function
h:\mathbb{D}([0,T],\mathcal{M})\to[0,\infty]
:

\displaystyle t_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq t_{0}+kT

\displaystyle A_{t}(h)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}h(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}^{t_{0}}(h)

\displaystyle\coloneqq A_{t_{k}}-A_{t_{k-1}}=\int_{t_{k-1}}^{t_{k}}h(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{k}^{t_{0}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{F}_{t_{k}}

\displaystyle M_{n}^{t_{0}}(h)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Delta_{2k-1}^{t_{0}}-\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{2k-1}^{t_{0}}|\mathcal{G}^{t_{0}}_{2k-2}]

\displaystyle N_{n}^{t_{0}}(h)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Delta_{2k}^{t_{0}}-\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{2k}^{t_{0}}|\mathcal{G}^{t_{0}}_{2k-1}]

By
4.1
,

(4.4)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}h(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2nT}A_{t_{0}+2nT}(h)dt_{0}\,.

Thus, our claim is equivalent to

(4.5)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{T}A_{t_{0}+2nT}(f)dt_{0}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{T}A_{t_{0}+2nT}(g)dt_{0}\,.

We write

(4.6)

A_{t_{0}+2nT}=A_{t_{0}}+\sum_{k=1}^{2n}\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{k}^{t_{0}}|\mathcal{G}^{t_{0}}_{k-1}]+M_{n}^{t_{0}}+N_{n}^{t_{0}}

and bound each term separately. In what follows, we suppose
h\in\{f,g\}
.

Step 1:

By (
4.2
)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}A_{t_{0}}(h)dt_{0}\Big]\leq T\mathbb{E}[A_{T}(h)]<\infty\,,

and so

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{T}A_{t_{0}}(h)dt_{0}=0\quad a.s.

Step 2:
To control
M_{n}^{t_{0}}+N_{n}^{t_{0}}
, we first note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|M_{n}^{t_{0}}|^{2}+|N_{n}^{t_{0}}|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2n}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\Delta_{k}^{t_{0}}-\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{k}^{t_{0}}|\mathcal{G}^{t_{0}}_{k-1}]\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=1}^{2n}\mathbb{E}[|\Delta_{k}^{t_{0}}|^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+2nT}h(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})^{2}ds\Big]\,,

and therefore

(4.7)

\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[|M_{n}^{t_{0}}(h)|^{2}+|N_{n}^{t_{0}}(h)|^{2}]dt_{0}\lesssim\int_{0}^{(2n+1)T}\mathbb{E}[h(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{t\leq r\leq t+T})^{2}]dt\,.

By (
4.2
) and (
4.7
), for almost all
t_{0}
the discrete-time process
M_{n}^{t_{0}}(h)
is a
L^{2}
martingale adapted to
\mathcal{G}_{2n}^{t_{0}}
and
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[|M_{n}^{t_{0}}|^{2}]dt_{0}<\infty
. By Doob’s inequality,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{m\leq n}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}M_{m}^{t_{0}}dt_{0}\Big)^{2}\Big]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{m\leq n}|M_{m}^{t_{0}}|^{2}dt_{0}\Big]\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[|M_{n}^{t_{0}}|^{2}]dt_{0}\,.

Then by a similar Borel-Cantelli argument as in
[
FS24
, Lemma 2.12]
we conclude

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{T}M^{t_{0}}_{n}(h)dt_{0}=0\qquad\textrm{a.s.}\,,

and the same exact argument works for
N_{n}^{t_{0}}
as well.

Step 3:
Finally, we note by (
4.1
) and the Markov property that

\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{k}^{t_{0}}(f)|\mathcal{G}_{k-1}^{t_{0}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{k}^{t_{0}}(g)|\mathcal{G}_{k-1}^{t_{0}}]\,,

and so (
4.5
) follows from
Step 1, Step 2,
and (
4.6
). Thus, we have shown (
4.3
).

For the final claim, note that above we showed that

(4.8)

\int_{0}^{t_{0}+2nT}\int_{s}^{s+T}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})drds=A_{t_{0}+2nT}(f)\leq A_{t_{0}+2nT}(g)+c(n,t_{0})\,,

where the first equality follows from the definition and
c(n,t_{0})
is a term satisfying
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{T}c(n,t_{0})dt_{0}=0
. Then we notice that (by Tonelli’s theorem)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t_{0}+2nT}\int_{s}^{s+T}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})drdsdt_{0}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t_{0}+2nT+T}\int_{(r-T)\vee 0}^{(t_{0}+2nT)\wedge r}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})dsdrdt_{0}\\
\begin{aligned} &\geq T\int_{0}^{T}\int_{T}^{t_{0}+2nT}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})drdt_{0}\\
&=T\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t_{0}+2nT}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})drdt_{0}-T^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})dr\,.\end{aligned}

Integrating (
4.8
) from
0
to
T
, applying the inequality above, using that
g(\phi)\leq\frac{T}{2}\hat{f}(\phi(0))+g_{2}(\phi)
, and then subtracting
\frac{T}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t_{0}+2nT}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})drdt_{0}
from both sides gives

\frac{T}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t_{0}+2nT}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})drdt_{0}-T^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})dr\\
\leq\int_{0}^{T}A_{t_{0}+2nT}(g_{2})dt_{0}+\int_{0}^{T}c(n,t_{0})dt_{0}\,.

Dividing by
2nT^{2}
, taking the
\limsup
as
n\to\infty
, and applying (
4.4
) gives

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{4nT}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t_{0}+2nT}\hat{f}(X_{r}^{x})drdt_{0}\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}g_{2}(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds\,.

This finishes the proof by
4.4
.
∎

We conclude this section with the proof of
2.22
:

Proof.

First note that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}f_{0}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}ds\Big]=\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}W^{\prime}(X_{t}^{x})ds\Big]<\infty

follows from
[
FS24
, Lemma 4.1]
, so
f_{0}^{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Next, we inductively show that

(4.9)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}f_{i}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}ds\Big]<\infty\,,

which proves
f_{n}^{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
.
Indeed, since
h_{i}
vanishes over
f_{i}
, there is

M>0
so that
h_{i}^{2}\leq\epsilon f_{i}^{2}+M
, where
\epsilon>0
will be chosen later. Plugging this into (
2.8
), we obtain

(1-\epsilon)\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}dt\Big]\leq\epsilon f_{i}(x)^{2}+M(T+1)+f_{i-1}(x)^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i-1}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}dt\Big]\,.

(We are justified with subtracting
\epsilon\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(X_{t}^{x})^{2}dt\Big]
from both sides because it is finite by assumption.) By Tonelli’s theorem,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{s}^{s+T}f_{i}(X_{r}^{x})^{2}drds\Big]\geq T\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}f_{i}(X_{r}^{x})^{2}dr\Big]-T\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(X_{r}^{x})^{2}dr\Big]

(for
t\geq T
). By Markov property and the above we conclude that

[(1-\epsilon)T-\epsilon]\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}f_{i}(X_{r}^{x})^{2}dr\Big]\\
\leq(1-\epsilon)T\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(X_{r}^{x})^{2}dr\Big]+Mt(T+1)+(T+1)\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t+T}f_{i-1}(X_{r}^{x})^{2}dr\Big]\,.

(Again, we are justified with the subtraction of
\epsilon\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}f_{i}(X_{r}^{x})^{2}dr\Big]
from both sides by assumption.) (
4.9
) follows by induction, where
\epsilon
is chosen such that
(1-\epsilon)T-\epsilon>0
.

The claim that
f_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
is also true by induction. Indeed, the base step
f_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
holds by
2.20
. Since
h_{i}
vanishes over
f_{i}
, by (
2.8
) there is
M^{\prime}>0
such that

(4.10)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(X_{t}^{x})dt\Big]\leq 2f_{i-1}(x)+\frac{T}{2}f_{i}(x)+2\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{i-1}(X_{t}^{x})dt\Big]+M^{\prime}\,.

Then the inductive step follows by
4.4
with
f(\phi)=\int_{0}^{T}f_{i}(\phi(t))dt
,
g_{2}(\phi)=2f_{i-1}(\phi(0))+2\int_{0}^{T}f_{i-1}(\phi(t))dt+M^{\prime}
, and
g(\phi)=\frac{T}{2}f_{i}(\phi(0))+g_{2}(\phi)
((
4.1
) is (
4.10
), (
4.2
) holds by (
4.9
) and
4.1
, and
\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}g_{2}(\{X_{r}^{x}\}_{s\leq r\leq s+T})ds
is bounded by
4.4
and our inductive hypothesis).
∎

4.2.
Proof of
3.3

Next we focus on using
6
to show that the empirical occupation measures are tight almost surely.

Lemma 4.5
.

For all
m,T>0

\lim_{M\to\infty}\inf_{W(x)\leq m}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\leq T}W(X_{t}^{x})\leq M\Big)=1\,.

Proof.

Given
x\in\{W\leq m\}
, define a martingale (see
2.4
)

M_{t}\coloneqq W(X_{t}^{x})-W(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})ds\,.

By
3
,

W(X_{t}^{x})=W(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})ds+M_{t}\leq m+Kt+M_{t}\,,

so that for any
M>0
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\leq T}W(X_{t}^{x})\leq M\Big)\geq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\leq T}M_{t}\leq M-KT-m\Big)\,.

Since
W\geq 0
,
M_{t}
is bounded below by
-m-KT
for
t\leq T
. If we define
\tau\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\mid M_{t}>A\}
, then
any
A\geq 0
we have

0=\mathbb{E}[M_{\tau\wedge T}]\geq A\mathbb{P}(\tau\leq T)+(-m-KT)\mathbb{P}(\tau>T)\,.

Rearranging the inequality and using
\mathbb{P}(\tau\leq T)=1-\mathbb{P}(\tau>T)
gives

\mathbb{P}(\tau>T)\geq\frac{A}{A+m+KT}\,.

If
M
is sufficiently large, by
setting
A=M-KT-m>0
and noting that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\leq T}M_{t}\leq M-KT-m\Big)\geq\mathbb{P}(\tau>T)\,,

we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\leq T}W(X_{t}^{x})\leq M\Big)\geq\frac{M-KT-m}{M}\to 1\text{ as }M\to\infty\,.

Since the above bounds were independent of
x\in\{W\leq m\}
, the claim follows.

∎

Corollary 4.6
.

For all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
m>0
such that for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\{W>m\})\leq\epsilon\quad a.s.

Proof.

By
2.20
,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}W^{\prime}\leq K\quad a.s.

It follows from Chebyshev inequality that
\mu_{t}^{x}(\{W^{\prime}>K/\epsilon\})\leq(\epsilon/K)\mu_{t}^{x}W^{\prime}
, and therefore

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\{W^{\prime}>K/\epsilon\})\leq\epsilon\quad a.s.

Since
W<<W^{\prime}
(see
3
and
2.7
),

m\coloneqq\sup_{W^{\prime}(y)\leq K/\epsilon}W(y)<\infty\,,

and then the claim follows from

\{W>m\}\subset\{W^{\prime}>K/\epsilon\}\,.

∎

Corollary 4.7
.

For all
T>0
,
\epsilon>0
there exists
M>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\sup_{s\leq r\leq s+T}W(X_{r}^{x})>M\}}ds\leq\epsilon\quad a.s.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{M}
.
By
4.6
, there is
m>0
such that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\{W>m\})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\quad a.s.

By
4.5
there is
M>0
such that (for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\sup_{t\leq T}W(X_{t}^{x})>M\}}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{W(X_{0}^{x})>m\}}\Big]\,.

The claim follows from
4.4

with
f((X_{t}))=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\sup_{t\leq T}W(X_{t}^{x})>M\}}\in[0,1]
and
g((X_{t}))=\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{W(X_{0}^{x})>m\}}\in[\frac{\epsilon}{2},\frac{\epsilon}{2}+1]
, hence
(
4.2
) clearly holds.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

For all
\epsilon>0
there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
such that for any
x\in\mathcal{M}

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{K})\geq 1-4\epsilon\quad a.s.

In particular,
3.3
holds.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
.
Let
f,T
be as in
6
and choose
M
large enough such that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r+s}^{x})dr>M/2\}}ds

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\quad a.s.

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\sup_{s\leq r\leq s+T}W(X_{r}^{x})>M/2\}}ds

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\quad a.s.

which is possible by
4.2
and
4.7
. By
6
, there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}^{c}}(X_{t}^{x})dt\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\mathbb{P}\Big[\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}^{c}}(X_{t}^{x})dt>\epsilon\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\epsilon+\mathbbm{1}_{\{\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{t})dt+\sup_{t\leq T}W(X_{t})>M\}}+\epsilon\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{r}^{x})dr>M/2\}}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{\sup_{r\leq T}W(X_{r}^{x})>M/2\}}+2\epsilon\Big]

By
4.4
and (
4.11
),

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}}^{c}(X_{r+s})drds\leq 4\epsilon\,,

which by
4.1
proves the claim.

∎

We finish this subsection by using
4.8
to show that the sets of invariant measures on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
are nonempty and compact.

Lemma 4.9
.

If
x\in\mathcal{M}
(respectively
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
), then almost surely the following holds: for every sequence
t_{n}\to\infty
such that
\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(\omega)
converges to some
\mu\in P(\mathcal{M})
, then
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
(respectively
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
).

Proof.

The claim for
\mathcal{M}
is proven in
[
Ben23
, Theorem 2.2ii]
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, additionally note that the Portmanteau theorem and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
being invariant imply that if
\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}\to\mu
, then
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(\mathcal{M}_{0})=1
a.s.
∎

Recall Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem:

Theorem 4.10
.

If
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
is ergodic then for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\mathcal{M}

\mu_{t}^{x}\to\mu\quad a.s.

Corollary 4.11
.

The sets
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
and
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
are nonempty and compact.

Proof.

First, note that
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
is closed in the set of all probability measures on
\mathcal{M}
with the topology of weak convergence because
X_{t}
is Feller.

By
4.8
, there is a sequence of compact sets
\mathcal{K}_{n}
such that for any
x\in\mathcal{M}

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{K}_{n})\geq 1-\frac{1}{n}\quad a.s.

If
\mu=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}
, then by Portmanteau theorem
\mu(\mathcal{K}_{n})\geq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{K}_{n})
. Then we obtain tightness of
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
from
4.10
and ergodic decomposition, and compactness from Prokhorov theorem. Also,
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
is closed in
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
by Portmanteau theorem, because
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is closed.

The nonemptiness is a consequence of
3.3
and
4.9
. ∎

4.3.
Proof of
3.4

Recall that our goal is to use
4
(ii)
to show
\mu_{n}H\to\mu H
if
t_{n}\to\infty
and
\mu_{n}\coloneqq\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}\to\mu
.

Proof.

For
M>0
, let
H_{M}\coloneqq((H\wedge M)\vee(-M))
. Note that
\mu_{n}H_{M}\to\mu H_{M}
as
n\to\infty
and
|H-H_{M}|\leq|H|\mathbbm{1}_{|H|>M}
.
Thus, to prove
\mu_{n}H\to\mu H
it suffices to show that almost surely there is
T>0
such that

(4.12)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\sup_{t\geq T}\mu_{t}^{x}|H|\mathbbm{1}_{|H|>M}=0\,.

Using the first condition in
2.11
and
4
(ii)
, there is is a finite positive measure
\mu
on
[-r,0]
and
f\in\mathcal{X}
such that

\forall\epsilon>0,\exists M>0
such that almost surely

|H(X_{t}^{x})|\mathbbm{1}_{|H(X_{t}^{x})|>M}\leq\epsilon\int_{-r}^{0}f(X^{x}_{t+s})d\mu(s)

for all
t>r
.

Setting
C=\mu([-r,0])
we have by Fubini-Tonelli theorem and
f\geq 0
that

\int_{r}^{t}|H(X_{s}^{x})|\mathbbm{1}_{|H(X_{s}^{x})|>M}ds\leq\epsilon C\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s}^{x})ds=\epsilon Ct\mu_{t}^{x}f\,.

Let
C^{\prime}(M)=\int_{0}^{r}|H(X_{s})|\mathbbm{1}_{|H(X_{s})|>M}ds
, so that for
t>r

\mu_{t}^{x}|H|\mathbbm{1}_{|H|>M}=\frac{1}{t}C^{\prime}(M)+\frac{1}{t}\int_{r}^{t}|H(X_{s})|\mathbbm{1}_{|H(X_{s})|>M}ds\leq\frac{C^{\prime}(M)}{t}+\epsilon C\mu_{t}^{x}f\,.

Letting
K
be as in
2.15
, almost surely there is
T(\omega)=:T>r
, independent of
M
, so that

\frac{t+r}{t}\mu_{t+r}^{x}f\leq 2K

for all
t\geq T
. Putting these inequalities together yields

(4.13)

\sup_{t\geq T}\mu_{t}^{x}|H|\mathbbm{1}_{|H|>M}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}(M)}{T}+2\epsilon KC\,.

By the continuity of
H
,
\int_{0}^{r}|H(X_{s}^{x})|ds<\infty
,
and the fact that
X_{t}^{x}
has cadlag paths, the dominated convergence theorem implies

C^{\prime}(M)\to 0
as
M\to\infty
) and (
4.12
) follows.

By the same argument as above,
\mu_{n}|H|\to\mu|H|
, and thus

\mu|H|\leq M+\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}|H|\mathbbm{1}_{|H|>M}\leq M+\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{C^{\prime}(M)}{t_{n}}+2\epsilon KC\,.

The claim follows with
A=M+2\epsilon KC
.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

For any
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
we have
\mu|H|\leq A
, where
A
is as in
3.4
. In addition,
\mu\mapsto\mu H
is a continuous
function on
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
and thus
\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H<0
is equivalent to
\mu H<0
for all ergodic

\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
.

Proof.

The claim
\mu|H|\leq A
follows from
3.4
and
4.10
. From (
4.13
) and
4.10
, we obtain that for all
\epsilon>0
there is
M>0
such that
\mu|H|\mathbbm{1}_{|H|>M}\leq 2\epsilon KC
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
. Thus,
\mu\mapsto\mu H
is a uniform limit of the continuous functions
\mu\mapsto\mu((H\vee(-M))\wedge M)
and so it is continuous as well. The last claim follows from the compactness of
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
(see
4.11
). ∎

4.4.
Proof of
3.1

First, we verify that
[
Ben23
, Proposition 4.6]
holds in our setting:

Lemma 4.13
.

•

For all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
, we have
\mu H\leq 0
. Also,
\mu H=0
if and only if
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
.

•

Since
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
is compact, for all
\epsilon>0
there is a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}
such that
\mu(\mathcal{K})\geq 1-\epsilon
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
[
Ben23
, Proposition 4.6i]
, we note that if
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
is ergodic then, by
2.19
and
4.10
, for
\mu
-a.e.
x
almost surely

(4.14)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}H=\mu H\,.

Since
\mathcal{M}_{+}=\bigcup_{M>0}\{V\leq M\}
, there is some
M>0
for which
\mu(\{V\leq M\})\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Since
\mu
is ergodic,
by
4.10
,
X_{t}
enters
\{V\leq M\}
infinitely often, and therefore
\lim_{t\to\infty}V(X_{t}^{x})/t=0
for
\mu
almost every
x
. Then from (
4.14
) follows that

\mu H=0
for each ergodic
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
. The rest of the proof follows as in
[
Ben23
, Proposition 4.6i]
(express each invariant measure as a convex combination of ergodic invariant measures
\mu
supported on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and notice that by
4
(i)
the ones supported on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
satisfy
\mu H\leq\Lambda<0
).

To show that
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
is compact, we use the first part of the lemma which implies
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})=\{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})\mid\mu H=0\}
. Since
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
is compact (
4.11
) and
\mu\mapsto\mu H
is a continuous function on
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
(
4.12
), then
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
is a closed subset of a compact set, and thus it is compact. The tightness of
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
in
P(\mathcal{M}_{+})
follows from Prokhorov’s theorem.

Note that by Portmanteau theorem the subspace topology of
P(\mathcal{M}_{+})
inherited from
P(\mathcal{M})
is the same as the topology of weak convergence in
P(\mathcal{M}_{+})
. Indeed,
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
P(\mathcal{M}_{+})
if and only if
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U})\geq\mu(\mathcal{U})
for all sets
\mathcal{U}
which are open in
\mathcal{M}_{+}
. In addition,
\mathcal{U}
being open in
\mathcal{M}_{+}
is the same as
\mathcal{U}
being open in
\mathcal{M}
because
\mathcal{M}_{+}
is open in
\mathcal{M}
. Thus, if
\mu_{n},\mu\in P(\mathcal{M}_{+})
and
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
P(\mathcal{M})
then
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
P(\mathcal{M}_{+})
. Conversely, if
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
P(\mathcal{M}_{+})
then for all open
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
we have
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U})=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+})\geq\mu(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+})=\mu(\mathcal{U})
.

∎

Remark 4.14
.

We could not directly apply the proof given in
[
Ben23
, Proposition 4.6ii]
because it uses a sequence of compact sets
K_{m}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}
such that
M_{0}=\cap\overline{K_{m}^{c}}
, which is equivalent to
\mathcal{M}_{+}=\cup K_{m}^{\circ}
. Without local compactness, we cannot construct such a sequence. For the same reason, we must give a slightly different proof of
[
Ben23
, Theorem 4.4ii]
below.

Next, we prove
3.1
:

Proof.

The proof of (i) is exactly the same as the proof of
[
Ben23
, Theorem 4.4i]
, and (iii) follows from (ii) and
4.9
.

To prove (ii), we define open neighborhoods
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{M}\mid d(x,\mathcal{M}_{0})<\delta\}
. We claim that for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta_{\epsilon}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}

\mathbb{P}(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{M}_{\delta_{\epsilon}})\leq\epsilon)=1\,.

For a contradiction, suppose that there is some
\epsilon>0
and
x_{n}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
such that for each
n\geq 1

\mathbb{P}(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x_{n}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}})>\epsilon)\geq\mathbb{P}(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x_{n}}(\mathcal{M}_{1/n})>\epsilon)>0\,.

By
3.3
and
3.1
part (i), almost surely for each
n
there is
(t_{m}^{n})_{m=1}^{\infty}
and
\mu_{n}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
with
t^{n}_{m}\to\infty
and
\mu_{t_{m}^{n}}^{x_{n}}\to\mu_{n}
as
m\to\infty
, and

\lim_{m\to\infty}\mu_{t_{m}^{n}}^{x_{n}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}})=\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x_{n}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}})\,.

It follows from Portmanteau theorem that

\mu_{n}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}})\geq\epsilon
on the positive probability event
\{\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x_{n}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}})>\epsilon\}
. Thus, we conclude that there exist
\mu_{n}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
with
\mu_{n}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}})\geq\epsilon
. By compactness of
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
(
4.13
), there is a convergent subsequence
\mu_{n_{k}}\to\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}}
are nested, by Portmanteau theorem we have
\mu(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{1/n}})\geq\epsilon
for all
n
, and thus
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})\geq\epsilon
, contradicting
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{+})=1
.

Finally, we use
4.8

to conclude that for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
compact such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}

\mathbb{P}(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{K})\geq 1-\epsilon)=1\,.

Thus, the second claim of
3.1
follows by setting
\mathcal{K}_{2\epsilon}=\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\delta_{\epsilon}}^{c}
.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

Suppose there is an open set
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}
for which
\mu(\mathcal{U})=1
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
. Then repeating the argument above with
\mathcal{M}_{0}
replaced with
\mathcal{U}^{c}
shows that the compact sets
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
in
3.1
may be assumed to be subsets of
\mathcal{U}
, because for all
\epsilon>0
there exists a
\delta_{\epsilon}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}

\mathbb{P}(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{U}^{c})<\delta_{\epsilon}\})\leq\epsilon)=1\,.

5.
Application 1: Stochastic Functional Kolmogorov Equations

The goal of this section is to show that
1
–
6
are satisfied for stochastic functional Kolmogorov equations considered in
[
NNY21
]
. We assume that our Markov process
(\Phi)_{t\geq 0}
evolves in
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq C([-r,0],[0,\infty)^{n})
, where
r,n>0
are fixed and
C(X,Y)
denotes the space of continuous functions from
X
to
Y
equipped with the supremum norm:

(5.1)

\|\phi\|\coloneqq\sup_{s\in[-r,0]}|\phi(s)|\qquad\textrm{for any}\,\phi\in\mathcal{M}\,,

where
|\cdot|
denotes the standard Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

To differentiate between functions in
\mathcal{M}
and points in
[0,\infty)^{n}
, we use
x
to denote
\phi(0)
for
\phi\in\mathcal{M}

and in general
X^{\phi}(t)\coloneqq\Phi_{t}^{\phi}(0)
is
the state of the process at time
t\geq-r
. Note that
the process
\Phi_{t}^{\phi}
on
\mathcal{M}
contains the information
on
X^{\phi}(x)
on the past interval
s\in[t-r,r]
. Consequently,
\Phi_{t}^{\phi}(s)=X^{\phi}(t+s)
for any
t\geq 0
and
s\in[-r,0]
. Also, denote
\pi_{i}:[0,\infty)^{n}\to[0,\infty)
the projection on the
i
th coordinate and define
X_{i}^{\phi}(t)\coloneqq\pi_{i}(\Phi_{t}^{\phi}(0))
.
We assume that the process
\Phi_{t}^{\phi}
satisfies the following system of stochastic functional differential equations with initial condition

\Phi_{0}=\phi
:

(5.2)

dX_{i}(t)=X_{i}(t)f_{i}(\Phi_{t})dt+X_{i}(t)g_{i}(\Phi_{t})dE_{i}(t)\qquad i=1,\cdots,n\,,

where
f_{i},g_{i}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous functions which are Lipschitz on the bounded sets
\{\|\cdot\|\leq M\}
for all
M>0
and
E_{i}
is a of independent Brownian motions with covariance
\mathbb{E}[E_{i}(1)E_{j}(1)]=\sigma_{ij}
. The process
X_{i}(t)
can be thought of as the population of some species
i
at time
t
, and its evolution depends on the history of the ecosystem starting from time
t-r
.

We consider that extinction occurred if
at least one species vanish, that is, if the process reaches the extinction set:

\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\{\phi\in\mathcal{M}\mid\phi(0)_{i}=0\text{ for some }i\leq n\}\,.

The coexistence set is then

\mathcal{M}_{+}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}=\{\phi\in\mathcal{M}\mid\phi(0)_{i}>0\text{ for all }i\leq n\}\,.

The significance of (
5.2
) and the applicability of

1
–
3
, formulated below, are discussed thoroughly in
[
NNY21
]
in the context of Lotka-Volterra competitive or predator-prey models, stochastic replicator equations in evolutionary game theory, or SIR epidemic models, all with a delay.

In order to verify our general assumptions
3
,
5
, and
6
, we use the following condition formulated in
[
NNY21
, Assumption 2.1 (3)]
.

Functional Assumption 1
.

There exist
c_{i},\gamma_{b},\gamma_{0},A_{0},M>0
,
A_{1}>A_{2}>0
, a continuous function
h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[1,\infty)
, and a probability measure
\mu
on
[-r,0]
such that for any
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
,

F(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}G(\phi)+\gamma_{b}J(\phi)\leq A_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|<M\}}-\gamma_{0}-A_{1}h(x)+A_{2}\int_{-r}^{0}h(\phi(s))d\mu(s)\,,

where
x\coloneqq\phi(0)
and

\displaystyle F(\phi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}f_{i}(\phi)}{1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}}

\displaystyle G(\phi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}c_{i}c_{j}x_{i}x_{j}g_{i}(\phi)g_{j}(\phi)}{(1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i})^{2}}

\displaystyle J(\phi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f_{i}(\phi)|+g_{i}^{2}(\phi)\,.

Additionally, the following assumption originating in
[
NNY21
, Assumption 2.2]
helps us verify
4

(ii)
.

Functional Assumption 2
.

One of the following holds for
J
as in
1
:

(i)

If
h
is as in
1
, then for any
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
we have

(5.3)

J(\phi)\lesssim h(x)+\int_{-r}^{0}h(\phi(s))d\mu(s)\,.

(ii)

There exists a function
h_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[1,\infty)
and a probability measure
\mu_{1}
on
[-r,0]
such that
for any
\phi\in\mathcal{M}

(5.4)

h_{1}(x)\lesssim J(\phi)\lesssim h_{1}(x)+\int_{-r}^{0}h_{1}(\phi(s))d\mu_{1}(s)\,.

Here,
x:=\phi(0)
and
\lesssim
is as in
2.6
.

Finally, to verify
4
(i)
we need the following condition from
[
NNY21
, Assumption 2.3]
.

Functional Assumption 3
.

For any
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
,

\max_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(\mu)>0\,,

where

\lambda_{i}(\mu)\coloneqq\mu\Big(f_{i}-\frac{\sigma_{ii}g_{i}^{2}}{2}\Big)\,.

It follows from standard theory (see the arguments in
[
NNY21
]
) that, under our (local) Lipschitz assumptions on
f_{i},g_{i}
stated above and
3
, the problem (
5.2
) has a unique global solution
\Phi_{t}^{\phi}
for each initial condition
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
, the Markov process
\Phi_{t}^{\phi}
is (
C_{b}
-)Feller, and
\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+}
are invariant sets. In particular,
1
–
2
hold.

We aim to use
3.1
to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

Under
1
–
3
,
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is stochastically persistent in the sense that for all
\delta>0
there are
0<b<B<\infty
such that for any nonnegative initial condition
\phi\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,

\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}m(\{t\leq T\mid b\leq\min_{i\leq n}(X_{i})_{t}\leq\max_{i\leq n}(X_{i})_{t}\leq B\})\geq 1-\delta

almost surely, where
m=m_{T}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
[0,T]
.

In other words, for most of the time (a proportion of
1-\delta
) the ecosystem supports at least a small portion (
b>0
) of every species.

Following
[
NNY21
]
,
for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{M}
and
s\geq 0
we define
\varphi\in\mathcal{M}
as

\varphi_{s}(t)=\varphi((s+t)\wedge 0)
for any
t\in[-r,0]
.
Then
for any (sufficiently smooth) function
V:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
we define

(5.5)

\partial_{t}V(\varphi)=\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{V(\varphi_{s})-V(\varphi)}{s}

and for any
i=1,\cdots,n
, we define

\partial_{i}V(\varphi)(t)=0
for
t\neq 0
and

(5.6)

\partial_{i}V(\varphi)(0)=\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{V(\varphi(0)+se_{i})-V(\varphi(x))}{s}\,,

where
(e_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Similarly, we define higher order derivatives.
The Itô formula for stochastic functional differential equations (
5.2
), see
[
NNY21
, (3.3)]
, has the form

(5.7)

dV(X_{t})=\mathcal{L}V(X_{t})dt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}(t)g_{i}(X)\partial_{i}V(X_{t})dE_{i}(t)\,,

where
\mathcal{L}
is (
5.2
) as in
[
NNY21
, (3.2)]
:

(5.8)

\mathcal{L}V(\varphi)=\partial_{t}V(\varphi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(0)f_{i}(\varphi)\partial_{i}V(\varphi)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(0)\varphi_{j}(0)\sigma_{ij}g_{i}(\varphi)g_{j}(\varphi)\partial_{ij}V(\varphi)\,.

Then carre du champ operator
\Gamma
is

\Gamma V(\phi)\coloneqq\mathcal{L}(V^{2})(\phi)-2V(\phi)\mathcal{L}V(\phi)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{j}\sigma_{ij}g_{i}(\phi)g_{j}(\phi)\partial_{i}V(\phi)\partial_{j}V(\phi)\,.

It follows from the functional Itô formula and a similar argument to
[
FS24
, Lemma 4.1, Corollary 4.2]
that all of the functions defined below belong to the appropriate domains (
2.3
or
2.4
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume
1
, fix
\gamma=\gamma_{b}/2
and define

(5.9)

\Psi(\phi)\coloneqq\ln\Big({1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}}\Big)+A_{2}\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)\,,

where
x_{i}=\phi_{i}(0)
and
h,\mu
are as in
1
.
Then there are
p>0,K>0
so that
W\coloneqq e^{p\Psi}
and
U\coloneqq e^{2p\Psi}
satisfy:

(i)

\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
,

(ii)

\mathcal{L}U\leq K-U^{\prime}
,

(iii)

J+\Gamma W\lesssim U^{\prime}
, where
J
is as in
1
,

where

(5.10)

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq pW(\phi)\Big(\gamma_{0}+Ah(x)+\frac{A_{2}\gamma}{2}\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(s))dud\mu(s)+\frac{\gamma_{b}}{2}J(\phi)\Big)

\displaystyle U^{\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2pU(\phi)\Big(\gamma_{0}+Ah(x)+\frac{A_{2}\gamma}{2}\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(s))dud\mu(s)+\frac{\gamma_{b}}{2}J(\phi)\Big)

and
A=A_{1}-A_{2}\int e^{-\gamma s}d\mu(s)>0
(see
1
).

Remark 5.3
.

Note that in
[
NNY21
, Lemma 3.1]
our
\Psi
is called
U
, but we renamed it to avoid confusion with
U
in
5
.

Proof.

The proof of
(ii)
is exactly the same as the proof of
(i)
with
p
replaced by
2p
, so we only prove
(i)
and
(iii)
.
Note that
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{i}
of the respectively first and the second term in
\Phi
vanish, and therefore by standard manipulations (see also
[
NNY21
, Lemma 3.1]
), we have

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Psi(\phi)

\displaystyle=F(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}G(\phi)+A_{2}h(x)\int_{-r}^{0}e^{-\gamma s}d\mu(s)-A_{2}\int^{0}_{-r}h(\phi(s))d\mu(s)

\displaystyle-A_{2}\gamma\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)\,,

where
F
and
G
are as in
1
.
Using
1
, we obtain

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Psi(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq-\gamma_{b}J(\phi)+A_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|<M|\}}-\gamma_{0}-A_{1}h(x)

\displaystyle+A_{2}\int^{0}_{-r}h(\phi(s))d\mu(s)+A_{2}h(x)\int^{0}_{-r}e^{-\gamma s}d\mu(s)

\displaystyle-A_{2}\int^{0}_{-r}h(\phi(s))d\mu(s)-A_{2}\gamma\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)

\displaystyle=-\gamma_{b}J(\phi)+A_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|<M\}}-\gamma_{0}-Ah(x)

\displaystyle-A_{2}\gamma\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)\,.

Again since
\partial_{i}
of the second term in (
5.9
) is equal to zero, we obtain

(5.13)

\Gamma\Psi(\phi)=\Gamma\ln\Big({1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}}\Big)=G(\phi)\,.

Since
G\lesssim J
, we can choose
p>0
small enough so that
p\Gamma\Psi\leq\gamma_{b}J
. Then the usual Itô formula or direct computation implies

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}W(\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}e^{p\Psi}(\phi)=pe^{p\Psi(\phi)}\Big(\mathcal{L}\Psi(\phi)+\frac{p}{2}\Gamma\Psi(\phi)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq pe^{p\Psi(\phi)}\Big(-\frac{\gamma_{b}}{2}J(\phi)+A_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|<M\}}-\gamma_{0}-Ah(x)

\displaystyle-A_{2}\gamma\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)\Big)

\displaystyle=pe^{p\Psi(\phi)}\Big(A_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|<M\}}-\frac{A_{2}\gamma}{2}\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)\Big)

\displaystyle-W^{\prime}(\phi)\,.

To prove
(i)
, it suffices to show that for some
K>0

(5.15)

pe^{p\Psi(\phi)}\Big(A_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|<M\}}-\frac{A_{2}\gamma}{2}\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)\Big)\leq K\,.

If

\frac{A_{2}\gamma}{2}\int_{-r}^{0}\int_{s}^{0}e^{\gamma(u-s)}h(\phi(u))dud\mu(s)\geq A_{0}\quad\text{or}\quad|x|\geq M\,,

then (
5.15
) is bounded above by
0
. Otherwise

\Psi(\phi)\leq\ln\Big(1+|c|M\Big)+\frac{2A_{0}}{\gamma}\,,

where
c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
, and so
(i)
holds with
K=\ln(1+|c|M)+2A_{0}/\gamma
.

To prove
(iii)
, we use (
5.13
) and the usual Itô formula or direct computation to compute

\Gamma W(\phi)=p^{2}e^{2p\Psi(\phi)}\Gamma\Psi(\phi)=p^{2}U(\phi)G(\phi)\,,

and consequently
U\geq 1
and
G\lesssim J
imply

J+\Gamma W\lesssim UJ\lesssim U^{\prime}\,,

as desired.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Under
1
,
3
and
5
hold with
W,W^{\prime},U,U^{\prime}
as in
5.2
and

(5.16)

V(\phi)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}V_{i}(x_{i})\,,

where
\rho_{i}>0
,
x\coloneqq\phi(0)
, and
V_{i}:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a smooth function such that

(5.17)

V_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}-\ln(x)&x\in(0,1)\,,\\
0&x\geq 2\,.\end{cases}

Proof.

The inequalities
\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{L}U\leq K-U^{\prime}
, and
\Gamma W\lesssim U^{\prime}
are shown in
5.2
.

We overload notation and use
V_{i}
to also denote the function defined for
\phi\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
as
\phi\mapsto V_{i}(x_{i})
. We have for
\phi\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
x_{i}\in(0,1)
that

\Gamma V_{i}(\phi)=\frac{1}{x_{i}^{2}}\sigma_{ii}^{2}x_{i}^{2}g_{i}^{2}(\phi)=\sigma_{ii}^{2}g_{i}^{2}(\phi)\,,

and if
x_{i}\in[1,2]
, then

\Gamma V_{i}(\phi)=V_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})x_{i}^{2}g_{i}^{2}(\phi)\lesssim g_{i}^{2}(\phi)\,,

where we used that
V_{i}
is smooth and thus
V_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x)
is uniformly bounded on
[1,2]
. Since
\Gamma V_{i}(\phi)=0
if
x_{i}\in[2,\infty)
, putting these inequalities together yields
\Gamma V\lesssim J
(it is easily checked that
\Gamma V\leq n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}^{2}\Gamma V_{i}
). By
5.2
,

J\lesssim U^{\prime}
, and thus
5
is satisfied.

Finally,
W<<W^{\prime}
in the sense of
2.7
follows immediately from
W\leq(p\gamma_{0})^{-1}W^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume
1
,
2
and define

(5.18)

H(\phi)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}H_{i}(\phi)\,,

where for any
\phi\in\mathcal{M}

(5.19)

H_{i}(\phi)=\begin{cases}-f_{i}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ii}g_{i}^{2}(\phi)&\textrm{if }x_{i}=\phi_{i}(0)=0\\
V_{i}^{\prime}(x_{i})x_{i}f_{i}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ii}^{2}V_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})x_{i}^{2}g_{i}^{2}(\phi)&\textrm{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

and
V
is defined in
5.4
. Then

4
(ii)
is satisfied with the measure
\mu+\delta_{0}
and
W^{\prime},V
, where
\mu
is defined in
1
and
W^{\prime}
and
V

are as in
5.2
and
5.4
respectively.

Proof.

From standard calculations and the definition of
\mathcal{L}V
in (
5.8
) follows that
H(\phi)=\mathcal{L}V(\phi)
if
\phi\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Clearly,
H
is continuous on
\mathcal{M}
, and therefore

H
is indeed the continuous extension of
\mathcal{L}V
to
\mathcal{M}
.
Also,
|H|\lesssim J
by using similar arguments as in the proof of
\Gamma V_{i}\lesssim J
in
5.4
.

To prove
4
(ii)
with the measure
\mu+\delta_{0}
,
from
2.11
and the almost sure continuity of the paths of (
5.2
), it suffices to show the following statement: for all
\epsilon>0
there is
N>0
such that for all continuous functions
\xi:[-2r,0]\to[0,\infty)^{n}
we have

(5.20)

J(\phi_{0})\leq N+\epsilon\Big(W^{\prime}(\phi_{0})+\int_{-r}^{0}W^{\prime}(\phi_{s})d\mu(s)\Big)\,,

where
\phi_{s}
is defined for
s,t\in[-r,0]
by
\phi_{s}(t)=\xi(s+t)
.

We give a proof of (
5.20
) under either the assumption (
5.3
) or (
5.4
). First, suppose (
5.3
) holds. If
W(\phi)\geq 1/(\epsilon pA)
for some
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
, then by the definition of
W^{\prime}

and
x=\phi(0)
we have

h(x)\leq\epsilon pAW(\phi)h(x)\leq\epsilon W^{\prime}(\phi)\,.

If
W(\phi)\leq 1/(\epsilon pA)
, then
by using
W\geq(1+\sum c_{i}x_{i})^{p}
we obtain

|x|\leq(\tilde{c}/(\epsilon pA))^{\frac{1}{p}}
, where
\tilde{c}>0
is a fixed constant only depending on
c_{i}
. Since
h
is a continuous function,
|h|\leq N^{\prime}

on the compact set
\{x:|x|\leq(\tilde{c}/(\epsilon pA))^{\frac{1}{p}}\}
. To summarize, we have

(5.21)

h(x)\leq N^{\prime}+\epsilon W^{\prime}(\phi)\,,

and (
5.20
) follows from (
5.3
).

If (
5.4
) holds, then by the definition of
W^{\prime}
for any
x\in[0,\infty)^{n}
we have

h_{1}(x)\lesssim J(\phi_{x})\lesssim\frac{W^{\prime}(\phi_{x})}{W(\phi_{x})}\,,

where
\phi_{x}\equiv x
. Since for every
\epsilon>0

there is
M_{\epsilon}
such that
W(\phi_{x})\geq\frac{1}{\epsilon}
if
|x|\geq M_{\epsilon}
and

J(\phi_{x})
is uniformly bounded for
\{x\in[0,\infty)^{n}\mid|x|\leq M_{\epsilon}\}
, we obtain that (
5.21
) holds and (
5.20
) follows from (
5.4
).
∎

To obtain the compactness required by
6
, we use similar argument as in
[
NNY21
, Lemma 3.3]
, which relies on the following version of the Kolmogorov continuity criterion.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a stochastic process on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
T>0
. Suppose there exist constants
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,K,C>0
such that:

•

\mathbb{E}[|X_{t}-X_{s}|^{\alpha}]\leq K|t-s|^{1+\beta}
for all
t,s\in[0,T]
.

•

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[0,T]\cap D}|X_{t}|]\leq C
, where
D
denotes the dyadic numbers
\frac{k}{2^{n}}
.

•

\gamma<\beta/\alpha
.

Then there is a modification
\tilde{X_{t}}
of
X_{t}
with almost surely
\gamma
-Hölder continuous sample paths. Additionally, there is a function
A:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
which depends only on
T,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,K,C
(not on
X
) and satisfies:

•

\lim_{M\to\infty}A(M)=0
.

•

\mathbb{P}(\|\tilde{X}\|_{\gamma}>M)\leq A(M)
, where

\|\tilde{X}\|_{\gamma}\coloneqq|\tilde{X}_{0}|+\sup_{0\leq s<t\leq T}\frac{|\tilde{X}_{t}-\tilde{X}_{s}|}{t-s}\,.

Remark 5.7
.

The usual Kolmogorov criterion implies that for any process
X
