satisfying suitable bounds, it has a modification which is almost surely Hölder continuous, so the (random) Hölder constant
C_{h}
is finite almost surely and thus
\mathbb{P}(C_{h}>M)\to 0
as
M\to\infty
. In
5.6
we note that the rate at which
\mathbb{P}(C_{h}>M)
converges to
0
only depends on the process
X
through certain constants. This observation is necessary for verifying
6
, because we need our
\mathcal{K}
to be independent of the initial condition.

Proof.

The existence of such a modification is given in
[
LG16
, Theorem 2.9]
. Examination of the proof reveals that there is a constant
C_{\gamma}
(depending only on
\gamma
) such that
|X_{t}(\omega)-X_{s}(\omega)|\leq C_{\gamma}|t-s|^{\gamma}
for all (dyadic)
s,t
such that
|s-t|\leq 2^{-n^{*}(\omega)}
, where
n^{*}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-valued random variable with
\mathbb{P}(n^{*}>n)\leq a_{n}
for some sequence
a_{n}\to 0
which depends only on
T,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma,K
. Thus, almost surely

\|\tilde{X}\|_{\gamma}\leq(1+2\cdot 2^{\gamma n^{*}})\sup_{t\in[0,T]\cap D}|X_{t}|+C_{\gamma}\,,

from which the existence of
A
follows.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Under
1
,
6
holds.

Proof.

Recall our choice of
W
in
5.2
. If
W(\Phi_{t})\leq M^{p}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, then
|X_{t}|\leq\tilde{c}M
, where
\tilde{c}>0
is a fixed constant only depending on
c_{i}
and
p
(the same one as in the proof of
5.5
), and thus
\|\Phi_{t}\|\leq\tilde{c}M
for all
t\in[r,T]
.

Thus, on the event
\{W(\Phi_{t})\leq M^{p}\textrm{ for all }t\in[0,T]\}
, the process
\{\Phi_{t}\}_{t\in[r,T]}
agrees with the truncated process
\tilde{\Phi}_{t}(s)=\tilde{X}(t+s)
, where
\tilde{X}_{t}
satisfies the same stochastic functional differential equation as
X_{t}
(see (
5.2
)) but with
f_{i},g_{i}
replaced by bounded Lipschitz functions
f_{i}^{M},g_{i}^{M}
which agree with
f_{i},g_{i}
on the set
\{\|\phi\|\leq\tilde{c}M\}
(for example, such truncated functions are defined in the proof of
[
NNY21
, Lemma 3.3]
), and initial condition
\tilde{\Phi}_{r}=\Phi_{r}
.

It is standard to show that
\{\tilde{X}_{t}\}_{t\in[r,T]}
satisfies the assumptions of
5.6
with
\alpha=4
,
\beta=1
,
\gamma=1/8
, and
K,C
depending only on
M,T
and not on the initial condition
\Phi_{r}
which satisfies
\|\Phi_{r}\|\leq\tilde{c}M
almost surely. Thus, we have that (
2.6
) holds with
\mathcal{K}=\{\phi\in\mathcal{M}\mid\|\phi\|_{\gamma}\leq C_{h}\}
for
C_{h}
large enough depending only on
M,T,\epsilon
. The set
\mathcal{K}
is compact by Arzela-Ascoli theorem, so
6
holds.
∎

Finally we prove
5.1
.

Proof of
5.1
.

At the beginning of this section we noted that
1
–
2
hold. In
5.4
,
5.5
, and
5.8
we proved that all other assumptions except
4
(i)
hold.

Although it is known (see
[
HN18
, Lemma 2.1]
) that
\mu H=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}\lambda_{i}(\mu)
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
, we at least provide some intuition. Clearly, it suffices to show the identity for
\mu
being ergodic. Since
\{x\mid x_{i}=0\}
and
\{x\mid x_{i}>0\}
are invariant for each
i
, each set has
\mu
measure equal to 0 or 1. If for some
i
we have
\mu(\{x\mid x_{i}=0\})=1
, then
\mu
almost surely
\mu H_{i}=-f_{i}+\frac{\sigma_{iig_{i}^{2}}}{2}
and the claim follows. If
\mu(\{x\mid x_{i}=0\})=0
, then as in the proof of
4.13
it holds that
\mu(H_{i})=\lambda_{i}(\mu)=0
.

Choose
\rho_{i}>0
in (
5.16
) and (
5.18
) and
\Lambda<0
such that

\mu H=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}\lambda_{i}(\mu)\leq\Lambda<0

for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
. This is possible by applying Hahn-Banach separation theorem to the set
\{(\lambda_{1}(\mu),\dots,\lambda_{n}(\mu))\mid\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})\}
, which is compact by
4.11
and
4.12
and is disjoint from
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid x_{i}\leq 0\text{ for all }i\leq n\}
by
3
. Then
4
(i)
is satisfied.

Since
\phi\mapsto\min_{i\leq n}\phi(0)_{i}
is continuous and strictly positive on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
, it has a strictly positive infimum on any compact subset of
\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Also,
\phi\mapsto\max_{i\leq n}\phi(0)_{i}
has a finite supremum on any compact set. Thus, the theorem follows from the second statement of
3.1
.
∎

6.
Application 2: Stochastic PDEs

In this section we discuss general stochastic PDEs with positive solutions. Numerous examples are given in
Section

7
.
The considered (system of) stochastic PDE has the form

(6.1)

dx_{t}=(Ax_{t}+F(x_{t},\theta))dt+G(x_{t},\theta)dW_{t}\,.

Let us fix the notation which is used throughout
Section

6
. The precise definitions and assumptions on
F
and
G
are given below after a development of the linear theory.

•

A standing assumption throughout this section is that there is a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
to which all stochastic processes are adapted.

•

We fix
N\geq 1
to be the dimension of the space and
m
to be the dimension of the process. Specifically, we assume that

x_{t}=(x^{(1)}_{t},\dots,x^{(m)}_{t})
and
x^{(j)}
for each
j=1,\dots,m
is a real valued
random function on the domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
, where

D
is either a bounded open connected subset of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with smooth boundary or
D=\mathbb{T}^{N}
is the
N
-dimensional torus.

•

The stochastic process
x_{t}
takes values in the space
C(\overline{D})_{+}\coloneqq\{f\in B\mid f\geq 0\}
, where
f\geq 0
is understood component-wise, and
B\coloneqq C(\overline{D})\coloneqq C(\overline{D},\mathbb{R}^{m})
is the space of all continuous functions
f:\overline{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

equipped with the supremum norm.

•

A=(A_{1},\cdots,A_{m})
acts as
Ax_{t}=(A_{1}x^{(1)}_{t},\cdots,A_{m}x_{t}^{(m)})
, where each
A_{i}
is an elliptic second order differential operator with appropriate boundary conditions specified below.

•

Fix
d\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and write

x_{t}=(u_{t},z_{t})
, where
u_{t}
and
z_{t}
represent respectively the first
d
and last
m-d
components of
x_{t}
. The reader can think of
u_{t}
as representing the population of
d
species for which we investigate the survival/extinction and
z_{t}
models the populations of other species or environmental factors.
In addition,
we use
\pi_{u}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
(resp.
\pi_{z}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
) to denote the projections onto the first
d
(resp. last
m-d
) coordinates. Unless otherwise specified,
x_{u}
(resp.
x_{z}
) denotes the first
d
(resp. last
m-d
) coordinates of the object
x
. For example,
A_{u}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
and
we could rewrite (
6.1
) as

\displaystyle du_{t}

\displaystyle=[A_{u}u_{t}+F_{u}(u_{t},z_{t},\theta)]dt+G_{u}(u_{t},z_{t},\theta)dW_{t}

\displaystyle dz_{t}

\displaystyle=[A_{z}z_{t}+F_{z}(u_{t},z_{t},\theta)]dt+G_{z}(u_{t},z_{t},\theta)dW_{t}

or we use
x_{u}=u
and
x_{z}=z
.

•

Also, if
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
we denote
x\cdot y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
the component-wise multiplication of
x
and
y
.

•

Let
\Sigma
be the Borel sigma-algebra on
D
and
\mu
be a measure admitting a smooth density with respect to Lebesgue measure which is positive and uniformly bounded away from zero.
We use
L^{p}
as a shorthand for
L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\mu)
,
L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\mu;\mathbb{R}^{m})
, which is clear from the context. Note that due to our assumptions on
\mu
,
the norm on
L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\mu)
is equivalent to
L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure. In particular, all Sobolev embeddings hold true for our
\mu
and we use this observation below without notice.

•

\theta
is some (fixed) parameter which is an element of the compact metric space
\Theta
. In our examples,
\theta
represents the strength of the noise.

•

F
is a sufficiently smooth function from
B\times\Theta
to
B
.

•

W_{t}
is a cylindrical Brownian motion on a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{U}
.

•

G
is a sufficiently smooth map from
B\times\Theta
to the space of bounded linear operators from
\mathcal{U}
to
L^{2}
, denoted by
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
.

Below, our assumptions guarantee that
\{(x_{u})_{0}=u_{0}\equiv 0\}
is an invariant set of (
6.1
) and our goal is to develop criteria which imply that
x_{t}
is stochastically persistent on
C(\overline{D})_{+}\setminus\{u\equiv 0\}
in the sense of
3.1
. In ecological models, this indicates survival of the first
d
species. The main applications are to stochastic reaction-diffusion equations coming from biology and models for turbulence.

Additionally, we will study the dependence of our results on
\theta
, which will be quite useful in the small noise regime. In particular, if
\Theta=[0,1]
and
G(u,\theta)=\theta G(u)
, then for
\theta=0
the problem (
6.1
) becomes a deterministic PDE. In
Section

7
this will allow us to say that persistence occurs in the small noise regime if an eigenvalue corresponding to this PDE is positive, and often we are able to explicitly calculate this eigenvalue.

Remark 6.1
.

All of our results also apply to models with regime-switching, meaning they remain valid if
\theta
is allowed to depend on time in such a way that
(x_{t},\theta_{t})
forms a Markov process. In this case,
\Theta
need not be compact as long as
\theta_{t}
is suitably stable (empirical occupation measures are tight, etc.). Since none of our examples have regime-switching, we decided to keep
\theta
a constant for brevity.

To ease notation, throughout the following sections we often suppress the dependence of
F,G
on
\theta
.

6.1.
Semigroups

We begin by imposing some assumptions on the operator
A
and proving some properties of its semigroup and interpolation spaces.

Definition 6.2
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
1<p<\infty
we define the Sobolev spaces recursively by
W^{0,p}\coloneqq L^{p}
and

W^{k,p}\coloneqq\{u\in L^{p}\mid\partial_{i}u\in W^{k-1,p}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,N\}

endowed with the norm

\|u\|_{W^{k,p}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\partial_{i}u\|_{W^{k-1,p}}+\|u\|_{L^{p}}\,.

In the definition, it is implicit that the weak derivatives
\partial_{i}u
exist. For
s>0
(not necessarily an integer), we define
W^{s,p}
via complex interpolation, see
[
BL76
, Theorem 6.4.5 (7)]
with the domain
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, but the results also hold for our domain
D
via extension operators.

If
D=\mathbb{T}^{N}
, we also define
W^{-s,p}
to be the dual of
W^{s,p^{*}}
, where
1/p+1/p^{*}=1
.

SPDE Assumption 1
.

Each operator
A_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
is of the form

\sum_{1\leq j,k\leq N}a^{i}_{jk}(x)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}b_{j}^{i}(x)\partial_{j}+c^{i}(x)\,,

where
a^{i}_{jk},b^{i}_{j},c^{i}
,
j,k=1,\dots,N
are smooth functions on
\overline{D}
and there is
\eta>0
such that for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and all
i=1,\dots,m

\sum_{1\leq j,k\leq N}a^{i}_{jk}(x)y_{j}y_{k}\geq\eta|y|^{2}\qquad\qquad\textrm{(ellipticity).}

We define the domain of the unbounded operator
A_{i}
on
L^{p}
for
1<p<\infty
based on the boundary conditions on
\partial D
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(A_{i})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{u\in W^{2,p}\mid\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}\equiv 0\text{ on }\partial D\}&\text{Neumann Boundary Conditions}\\
W^{2,p}&\text{Periodic Boundary Conditions}\,,\end{cases}

where
\nu
denotes the outward-pointing normal vector to
\partial D
.

We also assume the following assertions for each
i=1,\dots,m
:

(i)

Every
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re z>-1
lies in the resolvent set of
A_{i}
.

(ii)

A_{i}
is self-adjoint when
p=2
.

(iii)

The solution
u
to
\frac{\partial u(t,x)}{\partial t}=A_{i}u(t,x)
is nonnegative if
u(0,\cdot)\geq 0
. In other words, the semigroup
S_{i}
generated by
A_{i}
(
6.5
) preserve positivity.

Remark 6.3
.

We restrict to Neumann or periodic boundary conditions because of our examples below, but our results also hold for more general (first order) boundary conditions as well (see
[
Lun12
, (3.1.3),(3.1.4)]
), for example those investigated in
[
WSW19
]
.

Remark 6.4
.

In our examples, we will not verify (i) or (iii) in

1
, because they are always guaranteed by subtracting a suitably large constant from
c
and then adding that constant to
F
, which, as the reader may check, does not violate any assumptions on
F
stated below.

Specifically, since
D
is bounded, the constant we need to subtract
from
c
to satisfy (i) in
1

does not depend on
p
, see
[
Lun12
, Theorem 3.1.2]
. In addition (iii) in
1
follows from
[
Eva10
, Section 7.1, Theorem 12(ii)]
. Indeed, if
c\leq 0
and
u_{0}\geq 0

suppose that there is
t>0
such that

S(t)u_{0}
is not strictly positive in
D
. Then
S(t)u_{0}
attains non-positive minimum and by the strong maximum principle, see
[
Eva10
, Section 7.1, Theorem 12(ii)]
,
S(t)u_{0}\equiv c\leq 0
. By intermediate value theorem and continuity we obtain
S(\tau)u_{0}\equiv 0
for
\tau=\inf\{t>0:S(t)u_{0}\not>0\}
. If
\tau=0
, then
u_{0}\equiv 0
and consequently
u\equiv 0
. If
\tau>0
, this means that
S(\tau)
is not injective. However, by (i) and (ii) of
1
,

S(\tau)=e^{\tau A}
is compact and self-adjoint with strictly positive eigenvalues, so it is injective, a contradiction.

Finally, we can without loss of generality assume in what follows that
S(t)
is a contraction in
B
, which again is immediate if
c\leq 0
(
[
Eva10
, Section 7.1, Theorem 9]
).

Definition 6.5
.

By
[
Lun12
, Theorem 3.1.3]
, the operators
A_{i}
are sectorial and generate analytic semigroups
S_{i,p}(t)
on
L^{p}(D)
for
1<p<\infty
(see
[
Lun12
, Definition 2.0.2]
). We use
S_{p}(t)=(S_{1,p}(t),\dots,S_{m,p}(t))
to denote the corresponding semigroup on
L^{p}(D;\mathbb{R}^{m})
. In addition,

\|S_{i,p}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p},L^{p})}\lesssim e^{-t}
for any
t\geq 0
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, and
i=1,\dots,m
,
see
[
Lun12
, Proposition 2.1.1]
,

Definition 6.6
.

Let
S
be a semigroup with generator
A
satisfying
\|S(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p},L^{p})}\lesssim e^{-t}
(for example if
A

is a sectorial operator, see
6.5
).
Then for any
\vartheta>0
we define the fractional power of
(-A)
as

(6.2)

(-A)^{-\vartheta}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\vartheta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\vartheta-1}S(t)dt\,,

where
\Gamma
is the usual
\Gamma
-function and the integral exist by the assumed bound on
S
.

Furthermore, we define

(6.3)

(-A)^{\vartheta}=((-A)^{-\vartheta})^{-1}\qquad\textrm{and}\quad A^{0}=I\,.

Remark 6.7
.

Observe that for
\vartheta=1
,
(-A)^{-1}
coincides with the inverse of
(-A)
, which is equal to the resolvent operator for
\lambda=0
, see
[
Lun12
, Lemma 2.1.6]
.
One can verify (see for example
[
Lun12
, Theorem 3.1.3]
) that

(-A)^{-\vartheta}(-A)^{-\varsigma}=(-A)^{-(\vartheta+\varsigma)}

for any
\vartheta,\varsigma>0
. Consequently
if
0
is in the resolvent set of
A
, as assumed in
1
, then

(-A)^{-\vartheta}
is injective. Indeed,
(-A)^{-1}
is injective by definition and thus so is
(-A)^{-n}
for any positive integer
n
. Choosing
n>\vartheta
we have
(-A)^{-(n-\vartheta)}(-A)^{-\vartheta}=(-A)^{-n}
, and thus
(-A)^{-\vartheta}
is injective.

Definition 6.8
.

For
\vartheta>0
, let
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r}=\mathcal{D}((-A)^{\vartheta})=\textrm{Range}((-A)^{-\vartheta})

be the fractional domains
on
L^{r}
with the corresponding norms
\|u\|_{\vartheta,r}\coloneqq\|(-A)^{\vartheta}u\|_{L^{r}}
(see
[
Hai23
, Section 5.4]
for more details). If
\vartheta<0
, we define
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r}
as the completion of
L^{r}
with respect to the norm
\|u\|_{\vartheta,r}\coloneqq\|(-A)^{\vartheta}u\|_{L^{r}}
.
When
r
is fixed, we often omit it and simply write
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta}
or
\|u\|_{\vartheta}
.

Assume
g
is a linear operator mapping
\mathcal{U}
to
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r}

and there exists an orthonormal basis
(h_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{U}
such that

(6.4)

\|g\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r})}:=\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big\|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{n}g(h_{n})\Big\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty\,,

where
(\gamma_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of independent standard Gaussian random variables. Then we say
g
is a
\gamma
-radonifying operator and for a brevity of notation we use
\|g\|_{\vartheta,r}\coloneqq\|g\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r})}

(see
[
vN10
,
vNVW08
,
vNVW07
]
for more about
\gamma
-radonifying operators).

Remark 6.9
.

We remark that the norm in (
6.4
) is independent of the orthonormal basis
(h_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and the space of
\gamma
-radonifying operators is a separable Banach space, since
\mathcal{U}
is assumed to be separable. In addition,

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r})}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,r})}
(see
[
vN10
]
for more details).

The following lemma describes the relationship between
B_{\vartheta,r}
and the Sobolev spaces (
6.2
). Below when we write that two Banach spaces are equal we also mean that their norms are equivalent.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
a=1
if the boundary conditions in
1
are Neumann, and
a=0
otherwise. Then for
1<p<\infty
:

(1)

For
1/(2p)+a/2<\vartheta<1
,
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}=\{u\in W^{2\vartheta,p}\mid Bu\equiv 0\text{ on }\partial D\}
(where
B=\partial_{\nu}
for Neumann and
B=0
for periodic).

(2)

For
0<\vartheta<1/(2p)+a/2
,
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}=W^{2\vartheta,p}
.

(3)

For
-1<\vartheta<0
,
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}=(\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}})^{*}
, where
1/p+1/p^{*}=1
. In particular, for periodic boundary conditions
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}=W^{2\vartheta,p}
for all
-1\leq\vartheta\leq 1
.

Remark 6.11
.

If
A
were not self-adjoint, then
6.10
would still hold except for (3), where we would have
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}=(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}})^{*}
instead, with
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\vartheta,p}
being the interpolation spaces corresponding to
A^{*}
.

Proof.

The first and second claims follow from
[
See71
, Theorem 3 and Corollary after Theorem 4]
, which identifies
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}
as the complex interpolation space
[L^{p},D(A)]_{\theta}
, and
[
See72
, Theorem 4.1]
, which describes the complex interpolation spaces exactly as stated in
6.10
.

For the third claim, consider the map
u\mapsto\phi_{u}
from
\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}
to
(\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}})^{*}
given by
\phi_{u}(v)\coloneqq\langle(-A)^{\vartheta}u,(-A)^{-\vartheta}v\rangle
for
u\in\mathcal{B}_{\vartheta,p}
and
v\in\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}
. This map is an isometry because

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\vartheta,p}

\displaystyle=\|(-A)^{\vartheta}u\|_{L^{p}}=\sup_{\|w\|_{L^{p^{*}}}=1}|\langle(-A)^{\vartheta}u,w\rangle|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|v\|_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}=1}|\langle(-A)^{\vartheta}u,(-A)^{-\vartheta}v\rangle|=\|\phi_{u}\|_{(\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}})^{*}}\,,

where we made the substitution
w=(-A)^{-\vartheta}v
. This substitution is justified because
(-A)^{-\vartheta}
can be defined as an isomorphism from
\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}
to
L^{p^{*}}
:

Indeed, by
6.8

L^{p^{*}}
is dense in
\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}
and
(-A)^{-\vartheta}
is an isometry from the subspace
L^{p^{*}}
of
\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}
to
L^{p^{*}}
, so it can be extended to an isometry on all of
\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}
. Suppose its image is not dense in
L^{p^{*}}
. Then there is
0\not\equiv\bar{w}\in L^{p}
such that
\langle(-A)^{-\vartheta}v,\bar{w}\rangle=0
for each
v\in\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}
. Since
A
is self-adjoint, we obtain
\langle v,(-A)^{-\vartheta}\bar{w}\rangle=0
for all smooth
v
, which yields
(-A)^{-\vartheta}\bar{w}=0
. Using that
(-A)^{-\vartheta}
is injective (
6.7
), we obtain
w\equiv 0
, a contradiction. Thus,
(-A)^{-\vartheta}:\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}\to L^{p^{*}}
is an isometry with dense image, so it is an isomorphism.

Finally,
\phi_{\cdot}
is surjective because every
\phi\in(\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}})^{*}
can be factored as the composition of
(-A)^{-\vartheta}:\mathcal{B}_{-\vartheta,p^{*}}\to L^{p^{*}}
and
\psi\coloneqq\phi\circ(-A)^{\vartheta}:L^{p^{*}}\to\mathbb{C}
, and
\psi
, by the Riesz representation theorem, has the form
\psi(f)=\langle g,f\rangle
for some
g\in L^{p}
, meaning
\phi
has the form
\phi(v)=\langle g,(-A)^{-\vartheta}v\rangle
, and thus
\phi=\phi_{(-A)^{-\vartheta}g}
.
∎

Next, we define convolutions with respect to the semigroup from
6.5
. For more details about (stochastic) integration in infinite dimensions, we refer the reader to
[
vNVW07
,
vNVW08
]
.

Definition 6.12
.

We use
*
to denote convolution and
\diamond
to denote stochastic convolution. That is,

(6.5)

(S*y)_{t}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}S(t-s)y_{s}ds\quad\text{and}\quad(S\diamond Z)_{t}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}S(t-s)Z_{s}dW_{s}\,.

Our main tools when using the semigroup approach are

6.13
, which is instrumental for analyzing the long-term tightness properties of our system
and
6.15
, which is
crucial for proving well-posedness.
We remark that
6.13
and
6.15
hold generally for any analytic semigroup on a UMD Banach space of type
2
with interpolation spaces defined as in
[
Hai23
, Definition 5.30]
. In the proofs below
f*g
denotes the usual convolution
of functions
f,g:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

(f*g)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}f(s)g(t-s)ds

and
L^{p}(0,T)
is
the set of all such
f
with

\|f\|_{L^{p}(0,T)}\coloneqq\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|f(t)|^{p}dt\Big)^{1/p}<\infty\,.

Lemma 6.13
.

Fix
2\leq r<\infty
and
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq L^{r}
. For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\infty>p\geq 1
,
\epsilon>0
,
T>0
the following assertions hold:

(i)

For
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/p+\epsilon}
,

\int_{0}^{T}\|S(t)x\|^{p}_{\alpha}dt\lesssim\|x\|_{\alpha-1/p+\epsilon}^{p}\,.

(ii)

For
y\in L^{p}([0,T];\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1+\epsilon})
,

\int_{0}^{T}\|(S*y)_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha}dt\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\|y_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha-1+\epsilon}dt\,.

(iii)

If
p>1
then for
Z\in L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T];\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon}))
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|(S\diamond Z)_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha}dt\Big]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|Z_{t}\|^{p}_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}dt\Big]\,.

In the above, the constant in the
\lesssim
’s do not depend on
x,y,Z
, and the dependence on
T
is uniform over bounded subsets of
(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

The proof relies heavily on the estimate

(6.6)

\|S(t)x\|_{\alpha}\lesssim t^{\beta-\alpha}\|x\|_{\beta}

(see
[
Hai23
, Exercise 5.41]
). Since
\int_{0}^{1}t^{p(\beta-\alpha)}dt<\infty
if
\beta-\alpha>-1/p
, the statement (i) follows from (
6.6
).

Define
f_{p}(t)\coloneqq t^{p}
and note
\|f_{p}\|_{L^{1}(0,T)}\lesssim_{p}1
for
p>-1
.

For (ii), apply Young’s inequality:

\int_{0}^{T}\|(S*y)_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha}dt\leq\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|S(t-s)y_{s}\|_{\alpha}ds\Big)^{p}dt\\
\begin{aligned} &\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}f_{-1+\epsilon}(t-s)\|y_{s}\|_{\alpha-1+\epsilon}ds\Big)^{p}dt\\
&=\Big\|f_{-1+\epsilon}(t)*\|y_{t}\|_{\alpha-1+\epsilon}\Big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(0,T)}\leq\|f_{-1+\epsilon}\|_{L^{1}(0,T)}^{p}\int_{0}^{T}\|y_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha-1+\epsilon}dt\,.\end{aligned}

To prove (iii), first note that (
6.4
) and (
6.6
) give

(6.7)

\displaystyle\|S(t)Z\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha})}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big\|S(t)\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{n}Z(h_{n})\Big)\Big\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,r}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\beta-\alpha}\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big\|\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{n}Z(h_{n})\Big)\Big\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\beta,r}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim t^{\beta-\alpha}\|Z\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta})}\,.

Then use
[
vNVW07
, Corollary 3.10]
and
(
6.7
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}S(t-s)Z_{s}dW_{t}\Big\|^{p}_{\alpha}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\|S(t-s)Z_{s}\|^{p}_{L^{2}((0,T);\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}))}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|S(t-s)Z_{s}\|^{2}_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha})}dt\Big)^{p/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}f_{-1+2\epsilon}(t-s)\|Z_{s}\|^{2}_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}dt\Big)^{p/2}\Big]\,,

and consequently Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|(S\diamond Z)_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha}dt\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|f_{-1+2\epsilon}(t)*\|Z_{t}\|^{2}_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}\Big\|_{L^{p/2}(0,T)}^{p/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|f_{-1+2\epsilon}\Big\|_{L^{1}(0,T)}^{p/2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|Z_{t}\|^{p}_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}dt\Big]\,.

∎

For our special case of a positivity-preserving semigroup on
L^{r}
,
6.13
gives a bound on the
L^{q}
norm for
q>r
given a bound on the
L^{r}
norm.

Corollary 6.14
.

Fix
T>0,p>1
,
q\in(2,\infty]
and suppose that
u_{t},y_{t}^{+},y_{t}^{-}\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];C(\overline{D})_{+}))
and there is
0<\eta^{*}\leq 1/2
such that, for all
r\in[2,q)
,
Z_{t}\in L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T];\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\eta^{*}-1/2,r}))
. With
y_{t}=y^{+}_{t}-y_{t}^{-}
, suppose

u_{t}=S(t)u_{0}+(S*y)_{t}+(S\diamond Z)_{t}\,.

Then for all
0<\eta\leq\eta^{*}
there is
r\in[2,q)
such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|u_{t}\|_{L^{q}}^{p}dt\Big]\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{r}}^{p}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|y^{+}_{t}\|_{\eta-1,r}^{p}dt\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|Z_{t}\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\eta-1/2,r})}^{p}dt\Big]\,.

Specifically, we may choose

(6.8)

\frac{1}{r}=\Big(\frac{\eta\wedge\frac{1}{p}}{N}+\frac{1}{q}\Big)\wedge\frac{1}{2}\,.

Furthermore, the
\lesssim
only depends on
p,q,\eta,r
(and
T
, but the constant is uniform over all
T
in any bounded subset of
(0,\infty)
).

Proof.

First note that
(S*y)_{t}=(S*y^{+})_{t}-(S*y^{-})_{t}
. Since
S(t)
preserves positivity (
1
),
(S*y^{-})_{t}\geq 0
. The assumed
u_{t}\geq 0
implies

(6.9)

\|u_{t}\|_{L^{q}}\leq\|u_{t}+(S*y^{-})_{t}\|_{L^{q}}\leq\|S(t)u_{0}\|_{L^{q}}+\|(S*y^{+})_{t}\|_{L^{q}}+\|(S\diamond Z)_{t}\|_{L^{q}}\,.

Then by Sobolev embedding there is
r\in[2,q)
and
\epsilon>0
such that
W^{2\eta-2\epsilon,r}\subset L^{q}
and

L^{r}\subset W^{-2/p+2\epsilon,q}
(which is valid if
1/q>1/r-2\eta/N
,
1/r<1/q+2/(Np)
and
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small, that is, if for example (
6.8
) holds), and
6.10
implies
\mathcal{B}_{\eta-\epsilon,r}\subset L^{q}
and
L^{r}\subset\mathcal{B}_{-1/p+\epsilon,q}
. Thus,

\|u_{t}\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim\|S(t)u_{0}\|_{q}+\|(S*y^{+})_{t}\|_{\eta-\epsilon,r}+\|(S\diamond Z)_{t}\|_{\eta-\epsilon,r}

and by
6.13

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|S(t)u_{0}\|_{q}^{p}dt\lesssim\|u_{0}\|^{p}_{-1/p+\epsilon,q}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|^{p}_{L^{r}}

and

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|(S*y^{+})_{t}\|_{\eta-\epsilon,r}^{p}dt+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|(S\diamond Z)_{t}\|_{\eta-\epsilon,r}^{p}dt\\
\lesssim\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|y^{+}_{t}\|_{\eta-1,r}^{p}dt+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|Z_{t}\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\eta-1/2,r})}^{p}dt

as desired.
∎

Lemma 6.15
.

Fix
2\leq r<\infty
and
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq L^{r}
. For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
,
p>\epsilon^{-1}
,
T>0
the following hold:

(i)

For
y\in L^{p}([0,T];\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1+\epsilon})
,

[0,T]\ni t\mapsto(S*y)_{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}

is continuous and

\sup_{t\leq T}\|(S*y)_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha}\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\|y_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha-1+\epsilon}dt\,.

(ii)

For
Z\in L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T];\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon}))
,

[0,T]\ni t\mapsto(S\diamond Z)_{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}

is continuous and

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\leq T}\|(S\diamond Z)_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha}\Big]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|Z_{t}\|^{p}_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}dt\Big]\,.

In the above, the constants in
\lesssim
’s do not depend on
x,y,Z
, and the dependence on
T
is uniform over bounded subsets of
(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

The first claim is a direct corollary of
[
vNVW08
, Lemma 3.6]
.

For the second claim, fix
\beta\in(1/2+1/p-\epsilon,1/2)
and roughly follow
[
KvN12
, Pages 1057-1058]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\leq T}\|(S\diamond Z)_{t}\|^{p}_{\alpha}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|s\mapsto(t-s)^{-\beta}Z_{s}\|^{p}_{\gamma(L^{2}((0,T);\mathcal{U}),\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}\Big]dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|s\mapsto(t-s)^{-\beta}Z_{s}\|^{p}_{L^{2}((0,T);\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon}))}\Big]dt

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-2\beta}\|Z_{s}\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}^{2}ds\Big]^{\frac{p}{2}}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\int_{0}^{T}(t-s)^{-2\beta}\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|Z_{t}\|^{p}_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})}dt\Big]\,,

where in the first inequality we used
[
vNVW08
, Proposition 4.2]
, in the second one the embedding
L^{2}((0,T);\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon}))\subset\gamma(L^{2}((0,T);\mathcal{U}),\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon})
(this is valid because
\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1/2+\epsilon}
has type 2, see
[
vNVW07
, Page 24, the discussion before Corollary 3.10]
and the references cited within),

and in the third one Young’s convolution inequality.
∎

6.2.
Well-posedness and Itô Formulas

Next we define precisely what we mean by a solution
x_{t}=(u_{t},z_{t})
to (
6.1
) and list assumptions which imply that the problem is well-posed and positivity preserving. We also prove these properties for the crucial “projective process”

(6.10)

(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})=(\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}},u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}},z_{t})\,.

Since we work with mild as opposed to what we call “strong” solutions, we derive nontrivial Itô/energy formulas for the evolution of certain norms. Finally, we provide sufficient conditions under which
1
and
2
hold.

Next, we introduce different strong and mild solutions to

(6.11)

dx_{t}=[Ax_{t}+F(x_{t})]dt+G(x_{t})dW_{t}\,.

For technical reasons we must allow local solutions as well, but we will omit the statement “up to time
\tau
” when
\tau=\infty
.

Definition 6.16
.

Fix
r\in[2,\infty),\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, a stopping time
\tau
, and assume
x\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
.
We say a process
x_{t}
is a strong solution in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
to (
6.11
) up to time
\tau

if for all
T>0
:

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|x_{t}\|_{1+\theta,r}dt]<\infty

(ii)

F:B\to\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
and
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|F(x_{t})\|_{\theta,r}dt]<\infty

(iii)

G:B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r})
and
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|G(x_{t})\|_{\theta,r}^{2}dt]<\infty

(see
6.8
for the definition of the norms) and furthermore

(6.12)

x_{t\wedge\tau}=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}Ax_{s}+F(x_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}G(x_{s})dW_{s}\,.

Definition 6.17
.

Fix numbers
r\in[2,\infty),\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, functions
F:B\to\mathcal{B}_{-1+\theta,r}
,
G:B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{-1/2+\theta,r})
, a stopping time
\tau
, and assume
x\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
.
A process
x_{t}
is a mild solution in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
to (
6.11
) up to time
\tau

if there is some
\epsilon>0
such that for all
T>0

(6.13)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|F(x_{t})\|_{-1+\theta+\epsilon,r}+\|G(x_{t})\|_{-1/2+\theta+\epsilon}^{2}dt\Big]<\infty\,,

(see
6.8
for the definition of the norms)
and furthermore

(6.14)

x_{t}=S(t)x_{0}+(S*F(x))_{t}+(S\diamond G(x))_{t}

almost surely on the event
\{t\leq\tau\}
.

Remark 6.18
.

Note that
6.13
still holds for local mild solutions. For example, in the setting of
6.13
(ii) we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|(S*y)_{t}\|_{\alpha}^{p}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|(S*(y_{\cdot}1_{\cdot\leq\tau}))_{t}\|_{\alpha}^{p}dt\leq\int_{0}^{T}\|(S*(y_{\cdot}1_{\cdot\leq\tau}))_{t}\|_{\alpha}^{p}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|y_{t}\|_{\alpha-1+\epsilon}dt\,,

where
6.13
(ii) is applied for the last inequality. The analogous statement holds for
6.13
(iii) by the same reasoning.

The following provides a relationship between strong and mild solutions:

Lemma 6.19
.

Fix
r\in[2,\infty),\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,
F:B\to\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
,
G:B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{1/2+\theta,r})
, a stopping time
\tau
, and assume
x\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
.
If
x_{t}
is a mild solution in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
of (
6.11
) up to time
\tau
and there is some
\epsilon>0
such that

(6.15)

\|x_{0}\|_{\theta+\epsilon,r}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\|F(x_{t})\|_{\theta+\epsilon,r}+\|G(x_{t})\|_{\theta+1/2+\epsilon,r}^{2}dt\Big]<\infty\,,

then
x_{t}
is a strong solution in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
up to time
\tau
. Conversely, if
x_{t}
is a strong solution in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
up to time
\tau
and (
6.15
) holds, then
x_{t}
is a mild solution in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta+1,r}
up to time
\tau
.

Proof.

First, assume that
x_{t}
is a mild solution.
The conditions (ii) and (iii) in
6.16
on
F
and
G
are clearly satisfied by (
6.15
). The condition (i) is a consequence of
6.13
and (
6.12
) follows from (stochastic) Fubini’s theorem as in
[
Liu05
, Proposition 1.3.5]
.

Second, assume that
x_{t}
is a strong solution. Since (
6.15
) holds in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta,r}
, then (
6.13
) holds with
\theta
replaced by
\theta+1
. Then use
[
BvNVW08
, Corollary 2.6]
on
(S(T-t)^{*}l)(x_{t})
, where
l\in\mathcal{D}(A^{\dagger})
(see
[
Hai23
, Proposition 6.7]
for the relevant details) to obtain (
6.14
).
∎

As expected, if
F,G
have some sort of global Lipschitz property then the problem (
6.11
) is well-posed:

Lemma 6.20
.

Recall
B=C(\overline{D})
and set
\mathcal{B}=L^{r}
for some
r>N

(set
\theta=0
in comparison to
6.16
-
6.17
). Fix
\eta\in(N/(2r),1/2)
and
\theta_{F},\theta_{G}\leq 0
that satisfy
\theta_{F}>\eta-1
,
\theta_{G}>\eta-1/2
. If
F:B\to\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{F}}
and
G:B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{G}})
are globally Lipschitz, then for all
p,T>0
the following statements hold:

(i)

The problem (
6.11
) has a unique mild solution
x_{t}\in L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
(
6.17
) for any initial condition
x_{0}\in B
.

(ii)

If
F_{n}:B\to\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{F}}
and
G_{n}:B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\theta_{G}})
are globally Lipschitz for each
n\geq 1
,
F_{n}\to F,G_{n}\to G
pointwise, and
x_{0}^{(n)}\to x_{0}
in
B
, then the solutions
x_{t}^{(n)}
converge to
x_{t}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
.

Proof.

The statements are consequences of
[
KvN12
, Proposition 3.8]
. Note that
[
KvN12
, (A5)]
follows from the Sobolev embedding
W^{2\eta,r}\subset C(\overline{D})
and
6.10
. Furthermore
[
KvN12
, (A4)]
follows from
[
Hai23
, Exercise 5.2]
; indeed, by
[
KvN12
, (A5)]

S(t)x\to x
as
t\downarrow 0
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\eta,r}
, and we have
\|S(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(B,B)}\leq 1
(see
6.4
).
∎

Often we would rather work with strong solutions in order to use Itô’s formula
[
BvNVW08
, Theorem 2.4]
. This is done using the following approximation scheme:

Lemma 6.21
.

Consider the notation and assumptions from
6.20
. For each
n\geq 1
define

R_{n}\coloneqq n(n-A)^{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}ne^{-nt}S(t)dt

and
F_{n}\coloneqq R_{n}F
,
G_{n}\coloneqq R_{n}G
.
Then, there is a unique strong solution
x_{n}(t)
(called Yosida approximations of (
6.11
))
of the problem

(6.16)

dx_{n}(t)=[Ax_{n}(t)+F_{n}(x_{n}(t))]dt+G_{n}(x_{n}(t))dW_{t}\,,\quad x_{n}(0)=R_{n}x_{0}\,.

Furthermore,
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converge as
n\to\infty
to the mild solution
x

of (
6.11
) in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
for all
p,T>0
.

Proof.

Since the proof follows from standard properties of the resolvent listed below, we leave details to the reader.

(i)

From (
6.6
) follows
\|R_{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B}_{\theta},\mathcal{B}_{\theta})}\leq 1
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
6.20
still applies with
F
and
G
replaced respectively by
F_{n}
and
G_{n}
. Hence, there is a unique mild solution
x_{n}
of (
6.16
).

(ii)

By combining
[
Hai23
, Exercise 5.42]

and
[
Lun12
, Proposition 2.1.11]

we obtain that
S(t)
is an analytic semigroup on
\mathcal{B}_{\theta}

for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. In paticular,
\|S(t)x-x\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\theta}}\to 0
as
t\to 0^{+}
, and therefore

R_{m}x\to x
in
\mathcal{B}_{\theta}
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\theta}
. Thus,
F_{m}\to F
as
m\to\infty
point-wise.

(iii)

Since
R_{m}-I=AR_{m}
, then (
6.6
) implies
R_{m}\to I
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B}_{\theta},\mathcal{B}_{\theta-\epsilon})
for all
\epsilon>0
. Hence,
G_{m}\to G
point-wise (after slightly decreasing
\theta_{G}
), and after combining with (ii),
6.20
gives the convergence of
x_{m}
to
x
.

(iv)

Note that
R_{m}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B}_{\theta},\mathcal{B}_{\theta+a})
for all
a<1
, which implies (
6.15
). We conclude by
6.19
that
x_{m}
is a strong solution.

∎

As previously mentioned, we would like to apply Itô’s formula to the Yosida approximations. The following lemma shows that as
n\to\infty
(
6.16
) certain terms coming from Itô’s formula converge as expected:

Lemma 6.22
.

Consider the setup of
6.20
and
6.21
, and suppose
\theta_{F}=\theta_{G}=0
. Let
\Phi:\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}
be twice continuously differentiable with linearly bounded first and second derivatives:
\|\Phi^{\prime}(x)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}+\|\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R}))}\lesssim 1+\|x\|_{B}
. Then for all
T>0
,
p\geq 2
:

•

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}|\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}(t))F_{n}(x_{n}(t))-\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))F(x(t))|^{p}dt\Big]=0
.

•

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}(t))G_{n}(x_{n}(t))-\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))G(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{U}^{*}}^{p}dt\Big]=0
.

•

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}|\operatorname{tr}_{G_{n}(x_{n}(t))}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x_{n}(t))-\operatorname{tr}_{G(x(t))}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x(t))|^{p}dt\Big]=0
. (For any operator
T
on
\mathcal{U}
we define
\operatorname{tr}_{T}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x)[Tu_{j},Tu_{j}]
where
(u_{j})
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{U}
.)

Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}F_{n}(x_{n}))ds+\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}G_{n}(x_{n}))dW_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{tr}_{G_{n}(x_{n})}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x_{n})ds

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(x)F(x)ds+\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(x)G(x)dW_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{tr}_{G(x)}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x)ds

in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T],\mathbb{R}))
, where we omitted the dependence of
x_{n}
and
x
on
s
.

Proof.

We start by proving the first bullet point. Since each
R_{n}
is a contraction in
B
and
\mathcal{B}
, and
F:B\to\mathcal{B}
is globally Lipschitz,

\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}|\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}(t))F_{n}(x_{n}(t))|^{2p}dt\leq\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}^{2p}|F(x_{n}(t))|_{B}^{2p}dt\\
\begin{aligned} &\lesssim\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}^{4p}+|x_{n}(t)|_{B}^{4p}+|x_{0}|_{B}^{4p}dt\\
&\lesssim\sup_{n}\|x_{n}\|_{C([0,T],B)}^{4p}<\infty\,,\end{aligned}

where the last inequalities are from our assumption on
\Phi^{\prime}
and the convergence of
x_{n}
to
x
in
L^{4p}(\Omega;C([0,T],B))
(
6.21
). Thus,
\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n})F_{n}(x_{n})
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2p}(\Omega\times[0,T])
. To show convergence
\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n})F_{n}(x_{n})\to\Phi^{\prime}(x)F(x)
in
L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T])
, it suffices to show convergence in measure. Thus, we fix a subsequence of
n
’s and show that there is a further subsequence which converges almost everywhere (this is an equivalent statement). By the convergence of
x_{n}
to
x
in
L^{4p}(\Omega;C([0,T],B))
(
6.21
) we may WLOG assume that
x_{n}\to x
almost everywhere.

Then the first bullet point follows from

\displaystyle|\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}(t))F_{n}(x_{n}(t))-\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))F(x(t))|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Phi^{\prime}(x_{n}(t))-\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},R)}\|F(x_{n}(t))\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle+\|\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}\|F_{n}(x_{n}(t))-F_{n}(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle+\|\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}\|F_{n}(x(t))-F(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\to 0\,,

where the first term goes to
0
by continuous differentiability of
\Phi
, the second by
F(x_{n}(t))\to F(x(t))
in
\mathcal{B}
and
R_{n}
being a contraction (recall
F_{n}=R_{n}F
), and the third by
F_{n}\to F
point-wise (see the proof of
6.21
).

The other bullet points follow similarly because (see
6.9
)

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))G(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{U}^{*}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}\|G(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Phi^{\prime}(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}\|G(x(t))\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B})}

and

|\operatorname{tr}_{G(x(t))}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x(t))|\leq\|\Phi^{\prime\prime}(x(t))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R}))}\|G(x(t))\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B})}^{2}

(
[
BvNVW08
, Lemma 2.3]
). The final claim follows from BDG inequality.
∎

Next, we describe our assumptions on
F
and
G
.
Recall that
d
was fixed at the beginning of the section and
\pi_{u}

is the projection on the first
d
coordinates.

Remark 6.23
.

In the assumptions below, the constants in every
\lesssim
are allowed to depend on
r
.

SPDE Assumption 2
.

The function
F:B\times\Theta\to B
is given by

F(x,\theta)(\cdot)=x(\cdot)\cdot f_{1}(\cdot,x(\cdot),\theta)+f_{2}(\cdot,x(\cdot),\theta)=:f(\cdot,x(\cdot),\theta)

for some locally Lipschitz functions
f_{1},f_{2}:\overline{D}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We assume:

•

f_{2}(\cdot,x,\theta)\geq 0
if
x\geq 0
.

•

f
is polynomially bounded: there is
k\geq 0
such that
|f(\cdot,x,\cdot)|\lesssim 1+|x|^{k}
.

•

x_{u}=0
implies
\pi_{u}(f_{2}(\cdot,x,\theta))=0
,
and therefore
\pi_{u}(f(\cdot,x,\theta))=0
.

•

f_{1},f_{2}
are differentiable in
x_{1},\dots,x_{d}
with locally Lipschitz derivatives which are polynomially bounded.

•

f^{+}\coloneqq f\vee 0
satisfies
|f^{+}_{u}(\cdot,x,\cdot)|\lesssim|x_{u}|
and
|f^{+}_{z}(\cdot,x,\cdot)|\lesssim 1+|x|
.

In the following assumption on the noise we allow the sequence
(\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}})_{n\geq 1}
(defined below) to be unbounded, unlike in

[
NY21
, Hypothesis 8.1]
and
[
Cer03
, Hypothesis 1-3]
.
We remark that
\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq P(n)
for some polynomial
P
(see
[
Cer03
, Remark 2.2]
).

SPDE Assumption 3
.

The function
G:B\times\Theta\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
is given by

G(x,\theta)=\sigma(\cdot,x(\cdot),\theta)\cdot H

for a function
\sigma:\overline{D}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
and an operator
H\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
. We assume:

•

\sigma
is differentiable in
x
with bounded locally Lipschitz derivative (jointly in all variables).

•

x_{i}=0
implies
\sigma_{i}(\cdot,x,\theta)=0
.

•

There is an (orthonormal) basis
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for
\mathcal{U}
such that
Hu_{n}=a_{n}e_{n}
, where
a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and
e_{n}
are the eigenfunctions of
A
(see
1
) normalized with the unit
L^{2}
norm.

•

If
N>1
then there is some
2\leq p<2N/(N-2)
such that

(6.17)

\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}^{p}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}<\infty\,,

and if
N=1
, then
\sup_{n}|a_{n}|<\infty
(and in this case we say
p=\infty
).

Lemma 6.24
.

Under
3
, we have the following assertions:

(i)

There is
\beta\in(-1/2,0]
such that
\|x\cdot H\|_{\beta,r}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{r}}
for all
r\in[2,\infty)
and
x\in L^{r}
. In particular, the estimate is valid for any
\beta\in\big(-\frac{1}{2},-N(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2p})\big)
, and additionally for
\beta=0
if (
6.17
) holds with
p=2
.

(ii)

For all
r>2
there is
\epsilon>0
such that the following holds for all
x,y\in\{x\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|x\|_{L^{1}}\leq 1\}
:

(6.18)

\displaystyle\|H^{*}(x)\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\|y\|_{\epsilon-1,r}+\|y\cdot HH^{*}(x)\|_{\epsilon-1,r}+\|H^{*}(x)\|_{\mathcal{U}}\|y\|_{\epsilon-1/2,r}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|x\|_{L^{r}}+\|y\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|y\|_{L^{r}}\,,

where the adjoint of
H
is taken in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
.

(iii)

With
\beta
as in (i),

(6.19)

\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}\|x\|_{\beta,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}\|x\|_{L^{1}}\,,

where the adjoint of
G(x)
taken in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
.

Proof.

In what follows assume
p>2
(we leave the case
p=2
to the reader as it is quite similar but simpler).

We start by showing
\|x\cdot H\|_{\beta,r}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{r}}
for all
r\in[2,\infty)
,
x\in L^{r}
, and
\beta<-N(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2p})
. This will prove (i) because by
3

-N(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2p})>-1/2
, so such a
\beta\in(-1/2,0]
exists. First notice that
for any
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
and
\beta\in(-1,0)
we have

(6.20)

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,r})}

\displaystyle=\|(-A)^{\beta}T\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}=\Big\|\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\beta-1}S(t)Tdt\Big\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\beta-1}\|S(t)T\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}dt\,,

which is finite if
\|S(t)T\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}\lesssim t^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha<-\beta
(see (
6.2
) for the second equality).

By
[
vNVW08
, Lemma 2.1]
we have

(6.21)

\|S(t)\circ(x\cdot H)\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}\lesssim\Big\|\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}^{2}|S(t)(xe_{n})|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{r}}\,.

Let
q
be such that
1/q+1/p=1/2
. Then by Hölder’s inequality and (
6.17
) we have

(6.22)

\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}^{2}|S(t)(xe_{n})|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}^{p}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}|S(t)(xe_{n})|^{q}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-\frac{2q}{p}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
\begin{aligned} &\lesssim\Big(\sup_{n\geq 1}|S(t)(xe_{n}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1})|^{q-2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}|S(t)(xe_{n})|^{2}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-2q/p+q-2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\leq(S(t)|x|)^{1-\frac{2}{q}}\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}|S(t)(xe_{n})|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\,,\end{aligned}

where in the last inequality we used the facts that
S(t)
is positivity preserving (
1
) and
-2q/p+q-2=q(1-2/p)-2=0
.

To proceed, we use
[
Cer03
, (2.7),(2.8)]
(see also the references within) to express
S(t)
as an integral operator with gaussian upper bounds. In other words, there is
K:(0,\infty)\times D\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
c>0
such that

[S(t)x](u)=\int_{D}K(t,u,v)\cdot x(v)d\mu(v)

and

(6.23)

0\leq K(t,u,v)\lesssim t^{-N/2}\exp\Big(-c\frac{|u-v|^{2}}{t}\Big)\,.

Then since
e_{n}
is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}
(which is a consequence of
A
being self-adjoint in
1
) we have for
u\in D
that

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}|S(t)(xe_{n})(u)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\Big(\int_{D}K(t,u,v)x(v)e_{n}(v)d\mu(v)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{D}K(t,u,v)^{2}x(v)^{2}d\mu(v)\,.

Combining with (
6.21
) and (
6.22
) yields

\|S(t)\circ(x\cdot H)\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}\lesssim\Big(\int_{D}[S(t)|x|(u)]^{r(1-\frac{2}{q})}\Big[\int_{D}K(t,x,y)^{2}x(v)^{2}d\mu(v)\Big]^{\frac{r}{q}}d\mu(u)\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}}.

By (
6.23
) we have

K(t,u,v)^{2}\lesssim t^{-N/2}k(t,u,v)\,,

where
k
is the kernel of a heat semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{t}:C(\mathbb{R}^{N};\mathbb{R}^{m})\to C(\mathbb{R}^{N};\mathbb{R}^{m})
(meaning
\mathcal{P}_{t}f
solves
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{P}_{t}f=c^{\prime}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{t}f
for some
c^{\prime}>0
). Thus,

\|S(t)\circ(x\cdot H)\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}\lesssim t^{-\frac{N}{2q}}\Big(\int_{D}[S(t)|x|(u)]^{r(1-\frac{2}{q})}[\mathcal{P}_{t}x^{2}(u)]^{\frac{r}{q}}d\mu(u)\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}}\,.

Using Hölder’s inequality (
2/p+2/q=1
) and the fact that
S(t)
and
\mathcal{P}_{t}
are contractions in all
L^{p}
spaces (for
\mathcal{P}_{t}
this is well-known, for
S(t)
see
6.4
) we have

\displaystyle\|S(t)\circ(x\cdot H)\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{r})}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-\frac{N}{2q}}\Big(\Big[\int_{D}[S(t)|x|(u)]^{r}d\mu(u)\Big]^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big[\int_{D}[\mathcal{P}_{t}x^{2}(u)]^{\frac{r}{2}}d\mu(u)\Big]^{\frac{2}{q}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle=t^{-\frac{N}{2q}}\|S(t)|x|\|_{L^{r}}^{2/p}\|\mathcal{P}_{t}x^{2}\|_{L^{r/2}}^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq t^{-\frac{N}{2q}}\|x\|_{L^{r}}^{2/p}\|x^{2}\|_{L^{r/2}}^{1/q}

\displaystyle=t^{-\frac{N}{2q}}\|x\|_{L^{r}}\,.

Since

(6.24)

\frac{N}{2q}=N\Big(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2p}\Big)<-\beta<\frac{1}{2}\,,

(i) follows from the criterion discussed after (
6.20
) with
\alpha=\frac{N}{2q}
.

To prove (
6.18
), we use Hölder inequality, (
6.17
), and
2(q-2)/q=2-4/q=4/p
to obtain

(6.25)

\displaystyle\|H^{*}(x)\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\langle x,a_{n}e_{n}\rangle^{2}\leq\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}^{p}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\Big)^{2/p}\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}|\langle x,e_{n}\rangle|^{q}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-2q/p}\Big)^{2/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{n}|\langle x,e_{n}\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}\rangle|^{2(q-2)/q}\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}\langle x,e_{n}\rangle^{2}\Big)^{2/q}

\displaystyle\leq\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{4/p}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{4/q}\leq\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{4/q}\,,

for any
\|x\|_{L^{1}}\leq 1
.

By (i) and a Sobolev embedding (see
6.10
) we have

(6.26)

\|x\cdot H\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon-1,r})}\lesssim\|x\cdot H\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,s})}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{s}}\,,

where
\epsilon
and
s
are such that

\frac{1}{r}-\frac{2\epsilon-2}{N}=\frac{1}{s}-\frac{2\beta}{N}\,,\qquad\beta\geq\epsilon-1\,.

Choosing
\beta
close to
-1/2
and
\epsilon>0
small, we have that (
6.26
) holds for any
s
such that

(6.27)

\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{N}>\frac{1}{s}\,.

By another Sobolev embedding and making
\epsilon>0
smaller if necessary, for
s
satisfying (
6.27
) we also have
\|y\|_{\epsilon-1/2,r}\lesssim\|y\|_{L^{s}}
.
Additionally, from
6.9
, (
6.26
), and (
6.25
) follows

\displaystyle\|y\cdot HH^{*}(x)\|_{\epsilon-1,r}

\displaystyle\leq\|y\cdot H\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon-1,r})}\|H^{*}(x)\|_{\mathcal{U}}\leq\|y\cdot H\|_{\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon-1,r})}\|H^{*}(x)\|_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|y\|_{L^{s}}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2/q}\,.

Thus, the expression on the left hand side of (
6.18
) is bounded by

\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{4/q}\|y\|_{\epsilon-1,r}+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2/q}(\|x\|_{L^{s}}+\|y\|_{L^{s}})\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|y\|_{\epsilon-1,r}^{p/2}+\|x\|_{L^{s}}^{a}+\|y\|_{L^{s}}^{a}\,,

where
1/a+1/q=1
. We finish the proof of (
6.18
) by showing

(6.28)

\|x\|_{L^{s}}^{a}\lesssim 1+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|x\|_{L^{r}}\qquad\textrm{and}\quad\|y\|_{\epsilon-1,r}^{p/2}\lesssim 1+\|y\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|y\|_{L^{r}}\,.

We only show the details for the former and leave the latter to the reader, as it is extremely similar: just replace
a
with
p/2
so that instead of
1/a=1-1/q
we have
1/a=1-2/q
, and also replace (
6.27
) by
1/r+2/N>1/s
.
(Notice that both sides of the final expression below are multiplied by
2
, so the desired inequality still holds.)

We begin with
\|x\|_{L^{s}}^{a}\leq\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{a\theta}\|x\|_{L^{r}}^{a(1-\theta)}
, where
\theta/2+(1-\theta)/r=1/s
. Since
cd\lesssim 1+c^{k}+d^{l}
if
c,d\geq 0
and
1/k+1/l\leq 1
, it suffices to show that
a\theta/2+a(1-\theta)\leq 1
. We have the equivalent expressions

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\theta}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a}

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{s}-\frac{1}{r}\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{r}\Big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{1}{q}

\displaystyle 2\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{r}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq q\Big(\frac{1}{s}-\frac{1}{r}\Big)\,.

The last relation is indeed satisfied since
q>N
(see (
6.24
)) and (
6.27
) holds with arbitrary precision, which allows us to make the right hand side larger than
1
by choosing
\epsilon
small enough.

Finally, we prove (
6.19
). By (
6.25
)
and
3

\|G^{*}(x)1\|_{\mathcal{U}}=\|H^{*}(\sigma(x))\|_{\mathcal{U}}\lesssim\|\sigma(x)\|_{L^{2}}^{2/q}\|\sigma(x)\|_{L^{1}}^{2/p}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2/q}\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{2/p}\,.

By Sobolev embedding we have
\|x\|_{-N/4-\epsilon,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{1}}
for all
\epsilon>0
, so we conclude via interpolation that

(6.29)

\|G^{*}(x)1\|_{\mathcal{U}}\|x\|_{\beta,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2(1-a)/q+1-b}\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{2/p+2a/q+b}

where
(1-b)(\beta+1/2)+b(-N/4-\epsilon)=\beta
and
(1-a)(\beta+1/2)+a(-N/4-\epsilon)=0
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{2(1-a)}{q}+(1-b)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{q}\Big(1-\frac{\beta+1/2}{N/4+\epsilon+\beta+1/2}\Big)+\Big(1-\frac{1/2}{N/4+\epsilon+\beta+1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2/q)(N/4+\epsilon)}{N/4+\epsilon+\beta+1/2}+\Big(1-\frac{1/2}{N/4+\epsilon+\beta+1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{(2/q)(N+4\epsilon)-2}{N+4\epsilon+4\beta+2}

\displaystyle<1+\frac{(2/N)(N+4\epsilon)-2}{N+4\epsilon+4\beta+2}\,,

where in the last inequality we used
q>N
(recall (
6.24
)). As
\epsilon\downarrow 0
the limit of the right hand side is
\leq 1
, so it follows that the left hand side is
\leq 1
for some small enough
\epsilon>0
.

Thus, the exponent of
\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}
is at most
1
. Since the exponents on the right hand side of (
6.29
) add to
2
and
\|x\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}
, (
6.19
) follows.
∎

Next we introduce some notation which appears frequently in the study of the projective process (
6.10
).

Remark 6.25
.

The definition below is notationally heavy, so we provide alternate insight; if
x_{t}
is an
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued path satisfying
dx/dt=F(x)
and for any
r>0
we make the substitution
v=r^{-1}x_{u}
,
z=x_{z}
(recall
x_{u}
and
x_{z}
are first
d
respectively last
m-d
components of
x
). Then

\tilde{x}=(v,z)
satisfies
d\tilde{x}/dt=\tilde{F}(r,\tilde{x})
for
\tilde{F}
detailed in
6.26
below and properly modified initial condition. If
F
is sufficiently regular, it is possible to continuously extend
\tilde{F}
to
r=0
.

Recall that
\pi_{u}
and
\pi_{z}
(from
\mathbb{R}^{m}
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
) are projections on the first
d
respectively last
m-d
components of a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Definition 6.26
.

If
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a continuous function which is continuously differentiable in
x_{u}
and satisfies
\pi_{u}\circ h\circ\pi_{z}=0
, then
\tilde{h}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is defined as

\tilde{h}(r,x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}r^{-1}\pi_{u}(h(rx_{u},x_{z}))+\pi_{z}(h(rx_{u},x_{z}))&r>0\\
\pi_{u}\big(\sum_{i\leq d}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{i}}(0,x_{z})x_{i}\big)+h(0,x_{z})&r=0\,.\end{cases}

In other words,
\tilde{h}
is the unique continuous function such that

(6.30)

(r\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})(\tilde{h}(r,x))=h(rx_{u},x_{z})\,.

For the functions
F
and
G
from
2
and

3
we also denote

\tilde{F}(r,x,\theta)(\cdot)\coloneqq\tilde{f}(\cdot,r,x(\cdot),\theta)\,,\quad\tilde{G}(r,x,\theta)\coloneqq\tilde{\sigma}(\cdot,r,x(\cdot),\theta)\cdot H\,.

Lemma 6.27
.

Assume that conditions of
6.26
hold for some
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, which is locally Lipschitz and
\partial h/\partial x_{i}

are locally Lipschitz for all
i\leq d
as well. Then

\tilde{h}
is locally Lipschitz on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Proof.

Denote
\nabla_{u}
the gradient with respect to first
d
variables.
For any fixed
r,r^{\prime}>0
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
we have

\displaystyle|\tilde{h}(r,x)-\tilde{h}(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|\tilde{h}(r,x)-\tilde{h}(r^{\prime},x)|+|\tilde{h}(r^{\prime},x_{u},x_{z})-\tilde{h}(r^{\prime},x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z})|

\displaystyle\qquad+|\tilde{h}(r^{\prime},x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z})-\tilde{h}(r^{\prime},x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=:(I)+(II)+(III)\,.

Let us estimate each term separately. First,

\displaystyle(I)\leq\Big|\frac{1}{r}\pi_{u}(h(rx_{u},x_{z}))-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}\pi_{u}(h(r^{\prime}x_{u},x_{z}))\Big|+|\pi_{z}(h(rx_{u},x_{z}))-\pi_{z}(h(r^{\prime}x_{u},x_{z}))|

and since
\pi_{u}h(0,x_{z})=0
, we have for any
j=1,\ldots,d
that

\Big|\frac{1}{r}h_{j}(rx_{u},x_{z})-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}h_{j}(r^{\prime}x_{u},x_{z})\Big|\\
\begin{aligned} &=\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{u}h_{j}(srx_{u},x_{z})x_{u}ds-\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{u}h_{j}(sr^{\prime}x_{u},x_{z})x_{u}ds\Big|\\
&\leq L|r-r^{\prime}|\,,\end{aligned}

where we used Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla_{u}h_{j}
for each
j
and
L
is bounded on bounded sets in
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
In addition, the Lipschitz continuity of
h
yields

|\pi_{z}(h(rx_{u},x_{z}))-\pi_{z}(h(r^{\prime}x_{u},x_{z}))|\leq L|r-r^{\prime}|\,.

To estimate (II), we observe that

\displaystyle(II)=\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}|h(r^{\prime}x_{u},x_{z})-h(r^{\prime}x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z})|\leq L|x_{u}-x_{u}^{\prime}|\,,

where we again used Lipschitz continuity of
h
and
L
is bounded on bounded sets in
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Finally, to obtain (III) we proceed as in the estimate of (I) to have

\displaystyle(III)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}|h(r^{\prime}x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z})-h(r^{\prime}x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{u}h_{j}(sr^{\prime}x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z})x_{u}^{\prime}ds-\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{u}h_{j}(sr^{\prime}x_{u}^{\prime},x_{z}^{\prime})x_{u}^{\prime}ds\Big|\leq L|x_{z}-x_{z}^{\prime}|\,,

where we used Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla_{u}h_{j}
for each
j
and
L
is bounded on bounded sets in
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

If
r^{\prime}=0
and
r>0
, then

(6.31)

\tilde{h}(r^{\prime},x)=\pi_{u}\Big(\nabla_{u}h(0,x_{z})\cdot x_{u}\Big)+\pi_{z}(h(0,x_{z}))\,.

In addition,

\pi_{u}\circ h\circ\pi_{z}=0
and the mean value theorem imply (for all
r\in[0,\infty),x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
)

(6.32)

\tilde{h}(r,x)=\pi_{u}\Big(\nabla_{u}h(u^{*},x_{z})\cdot x_{u}\Big)+\pi_{z}(h(rx_{u},x_{z}))

for some
u^{*}\in[0,rx_{u}]
. The Lipschitz continuity then follows
from Lipschitz continuity of
h
and
\nabla_{u}h
.
The case
r=r^{\prime}=0
directly follows from the Lipschitz continuity of
h
and
\nabla_{u}h
.
∎

Corollary 6.28
.

The following statements hold:

(i)

\tilde{F}:[0,\infty)\times B\times\Theta\to B
is locally Lipschitz.

(ii)

\tilde{G}:[0,\infty)\times B\times\Theta\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\rho})
is locally Lipschitz for all
\rho\in[2,\infty)
, where
\beta
is as in
6.24
(i).

(iii)

For all
M,p>0
there is some constant
C>0
such that

(6.33)

R+\|x\|_{B}\leq M\implies\|\tilde{F}_{u}(R,x,\theta)\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|x_{u}\|_{L^{p}}\,.

(iv)

For any
r\geq 0
,
\theta\in\Theta
, and any
x

(6.34)

|\tilde{\sigma}_{u}(\cdot,r,x,\theta)|\lesssim|x_{u}|\,.

(v)

There is some
k>0
such that, for
\rho\in[2,\infty)
and
\beta
as in
6.24
:

(6.35)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{G}_{u}(R,x,\theta)\|_{\beta,\rho}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x_{u}\|_{L^{\rho}}

\displaystyle\text{and}

\displaystyle\quad\|\tilde{G}(R,x,\theta)\|_{\beta,\rho}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+R)\|x\|_{L^{\rho}}

\displaystyle|\tilde{F}^{+}(R,x,\theta)|

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+(1+R)|x|

\displaystyle\text{and}

\displaystyle\quad|\tilde{F}(R,x,\theta)|

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+R)^{k}|x|^{k}\,.

Proof.

Recall
2
and
3
where we assumed that
f_{1},f_{2},\sigma
and their derivatives are locally Lipschitz as functions from
\overline{D}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\Theta
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. It follows by
6.27
that
\tilde{f},\tilde{\sigma}
are locally Lipschitz as functions from
\overline{D}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\Theta
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and (i) follows.

For (ii) note that by
6.24

\|x\cdot H-y\cdot H\|_{\beta,\rho}=\|(x-y)\cdot H\|_{\beta,\rho}\lesssim\|x-y\|_{L^{\rho}}\lesssim\|x-y\|_{B}\,,

so the result follows from
\tilde{\sigma}
being locally Lipschitz after replacing
x
and
y
by
\tilde{\sigma}
at two different variabes.

To show (
6.33
), note that
\tilde{F}_{u}(R,\pi_{z}(x),\theta)=0
for each
R
and
\theta
, so

|\tilde{F}_{u}(R,x,\theta)|=|\tilde{F}_{u}(R,x,\theta)-\tilde{F}_{u}(R,\pi_{z}(x),\theta)|\lesssim|x-\pi_{z}(x)|=|x_{u}|\,,

where the constant in the
\lesssim
is the (local) Lipschitz constant of
\tilde{f}
. A similar argument implies (
6.34
), because
\sigma
has bounded derivatives (
3
), and therefore it is globally Lipschitz.

To prove (
6.35
), we use the first assertion of
6.24
with
x=\tilde{\sigma}

to conclude that

\|\tilde{G}_{u}(R,x,\theta)\|_{\beta,\rho}\lesssim\|\tilde{\sigma}_{u}(\cdot,R,x(\cdot),\theta)\|_{L^{\rho}}\quad\text{and }\|\tilde{G}(R,x,\theta)\|_{\beta,\rho}\lesssim\|\tilde{\sigma}(\cdot,R,x(\cdot),\theta)\|_{L^{\rho}}.

Since
\sigma

has bounded derivatives and thus it is globally Lipschitz, the inequalities in (
6.35
)
for
\tilde{G}
follow from (
6.34
) and (
6.32
).

By the last assertion of
2
and (
6.32
),

|\tilde{F}_{u}^{+}(R,x,\theta)|\lesssim|x_{u}|\quad\text{and}\quad|\tilde{F}_{z}^{+}(R,x,\theta)|\lesssim 1+|(Rx_{u},x_{z})|\,,

which proves
|\tilde{F}^{+}(R,x,\theta)|\lesssim 1+(1+R)|x|
. The final inequality of (
6.35
) follows from (
6.32
) and the polynomial boundedness of
f
and
\partial f/\partial x_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,d
.
∎

Below we frequently use the fact that
\|x\|_{L^{1}}=\langle x,1\rangle
if
x
is nonnegative. Thus, the following definition will be useful.

Definition 6.29
.

Since
A
is self adjoint

there is a smooth function
\tilde{e}:\overline{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

\langle Ax,1\rangle=\langle x,A1\rangle=\langle x,\tilde{e}\rangle

for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{1,2}
.

Remark 6.30
.

Above we used the fact that
1\in\mathcal{B}_{1,2}
, which is clearly true for the boundary conditions we consider in
1
. If we were to also consider Dirichlet boundary conditions, we would need to be more careful about what happens near the boundary of
D
in all of our proofs regarding positivity (ex.
6.31
(i)
).

Recall that
y_{u}
and
y_{z}
denote the first
d
respectively the last
m-d
components of a vector
y
. To simplify the notation, we denote
u=y_{u}

and
z=y_{z}
. The following theorem will provide the well-posedness of the projective process.

Theorem 6.31
.

Let
r
be large enough. Under
1
-
3
, for every
R\geq 0
,
\theta\in\Theta
, and initial condition
\tilde{x}_{0}\in C(\overline{D})_{+}
there is a unique mild solution in
L^{r}
(see
6.17
), denoted by
\tilde{x}_{t}=(\tilde{u}_{t},\tilde{z}_{t})
, to

(6.36)

\tilde{x}_{t}=S(t)\tilde{x}_{0}+(S*\tilde{F}(R,\tilde{x},\theta))_{t}+(S\diamond\tilde{G}(R,\tilde{x},\theta))_{t}

Furthermore, the following properties hold:

(i)

\tilde{x}_{t}\geq 0
.

(ii)

\tilde{u}_{t}=0\iff\tilde{u}_{0}=0
.

(iii)

t\mapsto\tilde{x}_{t}
is almost surely a continuous map from
[0,\infty)
to
B
.

(iv)

If
(R_{n},\tilde{x}_{0}^{(n)},\theta_{n})\to(R,\tilde{x}_{0},\theta)
in
[0,\infty)\times C(\overline{D})_{+}\times\Theta
, then
\tilde{x}^{(n)}
, the solutions to (
6.36
) with initial condition
\tilde{x}_{0}^{(n)}
and
R=R_{n}
,
\theta=\theta_{n}
, converge to
\tilde{x}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
for all
p,T>0
.

(v)

If
\tilde{u}_{0}\neq 0
then
(r_{t},v_{t})\coloneqq(R\|\tilde{u}_{t}\|_{L^{1}},\tilde{u}_{t}/\|\tilde{u}_{t}\|_{L^{1}})
satisfies

(6.37)

\displaystyle dr

\displaystyle=r[\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle+\langle\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z}),1_{u}\rangle]dt+r\langle\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z})^{*}1_{u},dW_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle dv

\displaystyle=A_{u}vdt+v[-\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle-\langle\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z}),1_{u}\rangle+\|\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z})^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}]dt

\displaystyle+\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z})dt-\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z})\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z})^{*}1_{u}dt

\displaystyle+\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z})dW_{t}-\langle\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,\tilde{z})^{*}1_{u},dW_{t}\rangle v\,,

where the equation for
v
holds in the mild sense of
6.17
. We omitted the dependence on
\theta
for brevity.

(vi)

Let
\bar{x}_{t}\coloneqq(R\tilde{u}_{t},\tilde{z}_{t})=(r_{t}v_{t},\tilde{z}_{t})
, where
(r_{t},v_{t})
was defined in (v). Then

(6.38)

d\|\bar{x}_{t}\|_{\beta,2}^{2}=[-2\|\bar{x}_{t}\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+2\langle\bar{x}_{t},F(\bar{x}_{t})\rangle_{\beta}+\|G(\bar{x}_{t})\|_{\beta,2}^{2}]dt+2\langle\bar{x}_{t},G(\bar{x}_{t})dW_{t}\rangle_{\beta}\,,

where
\beta
is as in
6.24
.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, we drop tildes from
x
,
u
, and
z
in the proof.
We split the proof into the following steps:

(1)

Truncate
\tilde{F},\tilde{G}
to obtain approximations
x_{n}
to (
6.36
) which satisfy
(iii)
,
(iv)
.

(2)

Show that
(i)
holds for
x_{n}
. This is the longest step, but the essential idea is as follows:

Replace
F_{n},G_{n}
with
F_{n}\circ\psi,G_{n}\circ\psi
, where
\psi(x)=x\vee 0
is the positive part of
x
. If the solution of this new equation
y^{*}
is nonnegative, then it solves the same equation as
x_{n}
, so by uniqueness
x_{n}=y^{*}
is nonnegative. To show that
y^{*}
is nonnegative, try applying Itô’s formula to a smooth version
\Phi
of the Lyapunov function
x\mapsto\int_{D}-(x\wedge 0)
. There are three deterministic terms: one coming from
A
, another coming from
F_{n}\circ\psi
, and the third is the Itô correction term. Since we precomposed with
\psi
, the latter two are both
0
. By
6.32
(the crucial argument in this step) the term coming from
A
is
\leq 0
. This implies that
\mathbb{E}[\Phi(y^{*}(s))]
is nonincreasing, and since
\Phi(y^{*}(s))=0
iff
y^{*}(s)\geq 0
, this proves our claim.

Unfortunately, rigorously justifying the proof sketch above requires a couple of nontrivial approximations. For example, we must use the Yosida approximation (
6.21
) because the hypotheses of Itô’s formula require strong solutions as opposed to mild ones. There is also a less obvious approximation which is explained in greater detail below (
6.34
).

(3)

Use another Itô formula/Yosida approximation argument to show that
(vi)
holds for
x_{n}
until the time
\tau_{n}
when
\|x_{n}\|_{B}
first exceeds
n-1
.

(4)

Use another Ito formula/Yosida approximation argument to show that
(v)
holds for
x_{n}=(u_{n},z_{n})
until
\tau_{n}\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}
, where
\sigma_{\epsilon}
is the first time
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{1}}
drops below
\epsilon
. In the process of showing that
v_{t}
is indeed a mild solution, we prove
\sup_{\epsilon>0}\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T}\|u_{n}(s\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|u_{n}(s\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{1}}^{-2}ds]<\infty
.

(5)

Show that
(ii)
is satisfied for
x_{n}
using the Lyapunov function
-\ln{\|u_{n}\|_{L^{1}}}
, which requires the use of the bound above. This implies that
\sigma_{\epsilon}\to\infty
almost surely, and so
(v)
holds for
x_{n}
until
\tau_{n}
.

(6)

Prove
\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\leq T}\|x_{n}\|_{B}^{p}]<\infty
, thus
\sup_{n}\tau_{n}=\infty
a.s.

(7)

Show that there is a well-defined process
x_{t}
which satisfies
x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n})=x_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}
for every
n
and solves (
6.36
).

(8)

Conclude the theorem by noting that
x
solves (
6.36
) and inherits the aforementioned properties of
x_{n}
.

Since
R,\theta
are held constant for much of the proof, we sometimes omit the dependence on
R,\theta
for ease of notation.

Step 1:
Recall
6.26
. The truncation function
\Psi_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
given by

(6.39)

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}(x)=\begin{cases}x&|x|\leq n\\
n\frac{x}{|x|}&|x|>n\end{cases}

is Lipschitz with bounded range and it induces a Lipschitz map
\Psi_{n}:B\to B
via

f\mapsto\Psi_{n}\circ f
. We define the truncated functions

(6.40)

F_{n}(r,x,\theta)\coloneqq\tilde{F}(r,\Psi_{n}(x),\theta)\qquad\textrm{and}\quad G_{n}(r,x,\theta)\coloneqq\tilde{G}(r,\Psi_{n}(x),\theta).

Let
\beta
be as in
6.24
and
r\in[2,\infty)
in the definition of
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq L^{r}
be large. By
6.28
i-ii,
F_{n}(R,\cdot,\theta):B\to B
and
G_{n}(R,\cdot,\theta):B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,r})
are globally Lipschitz.

Then with
\theta_{F}=0
and
\theta_{G}=\beta
we conclude by
6.20
that for any
x_{0}\in C(\overline{D})_{+}
there is a unique
B
-valued processes
x_{n}
(mild solution) satisfying
(iii)
and

(6.41)

x_{n}(t)=S(t)x_{0}+(S*F_{n}(x_{n}))_{t}+(S\diamond G_{n}(x_{n}))_{t}\,,\quad x_{n}(0)=x_{0}\,.

Following the notation of
6.20
, we use
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq L^{r}
, so
\mathcal{B}_{-1}=\mathcal{B}_{-1,r}
, etc. For fixed
x\in B
we have by
6.28
that
F_{n}(\cdot,x,\cdot):[0,\infty)\times\Theta\to\mathcal{B}
and
G_{n}(\cdot,x,\cdot):[0,\infty)\times\Theta\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,r})
are continuous, so by
6.20
we conclude that for each
n
the solutions of (
6.41
) satisfy
(iv)
.

Step 2:
For this step, fix some
n
and approximate
G_{n}
by

\mathcal{G}_{k}(x)\coloneqq G_{n}(x)\circ\mathfrak{p}_{k}\,,

where
\mathfrak{p}_{k}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{Span}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
and
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is the orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{U}
from
3
. Since
by the elliptic regularity the eigenfunctions
(e_{n})
of
A
are in
L^{\infty}
, it follows from (
6.4
) and the definition of
H
that
H\circ\mathfrak{p}_{k}\in\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B})
. Since
\|f\mapsto x\cdot f\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B})}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}
, we also obtain from the local Lipschitzness of
\tilde{\sigma}
(
6.27
) that
\mathcal{G}_{k}:B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B})
is globally Lipschitz (global because we truncated with
\Psi_{n}
in
Step 1
and
R
is fixed).

Notice that, for all
x\in B
,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{G}_{k}(x)=G_{n}(x)
in
\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B})
, and therefore in
\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta})
, by
[
vNVW07
, Proposition 2.4]
(
\mathfrak{p}_{k}
converges to the identity on
\mathcal{U}
).

Then
6.20
implies that there exists a mild solution to

(6.42)

y_{k}(t)=S(t)x_{0}+(S*F_{n}(y_{k}))_{t}+(S\diamond\mathcal{G}_{k}(y_{k}))_{t}\,,\quad y_{k}(0)=x_{0}

and
y_{k}\to x_{n}
as
k\to\infty
in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
for all
p,T>0
. Thus, to show nonnegativity of
x_{n}
it suffices to show the nonnegativity of
y_{k}
for each
k
.

In what follows we fix
k
and
let
y^{*}(t)
be the solution of

(6.43)

y^{*}(t)=S(t)x_{0}+(S*(F_{n}\circ\psi)(y^{*}))_{t}+(S\diamond(\mathcal{G}_{k}\circ\psi)(y^{*}))_{t}\,,\quad y^{*}(0)=x_{0}\,,

where
\psi(f)=f\vee 0
(
\psi
is Lipschitz from
B
to
B
so
6.20
still provides the existence of the solution). To simplify notation for already fixed
n
and
k
, we let
\hat{F}\coloneqq F_{n}\circ\psi
,
\hat{G}\coloneqq\mathcal{G}_{k}\circ\psi
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function which satisfies:

(i)

\phi(x)=0
for
x\geq 0
and
\phi(x)>0
for
x<0
.

(ii)

\phi
is twice continuously differentiable with uniformly bounded first and second derivatives.

(iii)

\phi^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
(so
\phi
is convex).

For example, we may set
\phi^{\prime}(x)=-1
for
x\leq-1
, and

\phi^{\prime}(x)=0
for
x\geq 0
, smoothly and increasingly interpolate between
-1
to
0
for
x\in(-1,0)
, and then set
\phi(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\phi^{\prime}(t)dt
.

Since
S(t)
preserves positivity (
1
), by Krein-Rutman (
S(t)
is compact, see
6.4
) there is a nonnegative eigenfunction
\nu
corresponding to the largest eigenvalue of
S(t)
, and since
\nu\in\mathcal{B}_{\theta,q}
for all
\theta,q
we conclude via Sobolev embedding that
\nu\in C^{\infty}(\overline{D})
(see
6.10
). Similarly,
S(t)^{*}
, the adjoint semigroup, also preserves positivity and so there is
\nu^{*}\geq 0
, a nonnegative eigenfunction for
S(t)^{*}
(with the same eigenvalue). By the strong maximum principle (see
6.4
)
\nu,\nu^{*}
are strictly positive on
D
, and thus the function

\Phi(z)\coloneqq\int_{D}(\phi\circ(z\cdot\nu^{-1}))\nu^{*}\nu

is well-defined (note the integrand is in
L^{r}
since
z\in\mathcal{B}=L^{r}
and
\phi
is globally Lipschitz with
\phi(0)=0
). By (ii) in the definition of
\phi
, dominated convergence theorem, and mean value theorem we have that
\Phi
is twice continuously differentiable from
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathbb{R}
(for
r\geq 3
). Specifically,

(6.44)

\Phi^{\prime}(z)w=\int_{D}(\phi^{\prime}\circ(z\cdot\nu^{-1}))w\nu^{*},\qquad\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z)[w_{1},w_{2}]=\int_{D}(\phi^{\prime\prime}\circ(z\cdot\nu^{-1}))w_{1}w_{2}\frac{\nu^{*}}{\nu}

(the
\nu^{-1}
is not problematic since we work with Neumann or periodic boundary conditions, so the maximum principle implies that
\nu
is uniformly bounded below on
D
).
By (i) in the definition of
\phi
, one observes
z\geq 0
if and only if
\Phi(z)\leq 0
(in fact
\Phi(z)=0
), and therefore if
\Phi(y^{*}(t))\leq 0
almost surely then
y^{*}(t)\geq 0
. Consequently,
\psi(y^{*}(t))=y^{*}(t)
, which implies
y^{*}(t)
solves (
6.42
), and so by uniqueness
y_{k}(t)=y^{*}(t)\geq 0
. Thus, the claim of
Step 2
holds if
\Phi(y^{*}(t))\leq 0
almost surely.

To proceed, we wish to apply Itô’s formula
[
BvNVW08
, Theorem 2.4]
to
\Phi
, but the solutions to (
6.43
) are not strong. For each
\ell\geq 1
let
z_{\ell}(t)
be the strong solution of (
6.43
) (see
6.21
)
with
\hat{F}
and
\hat{G}
(recall
\hat{F}=F_{n}\circ\psi
,

\hat{G}=\mathcal{G}_{k}\circ\psi
) replaced respectively by their
Yosida approximations
\hat{F}_{\ell}\coloneqq R_{\ell}\hat{F}
, and
\hat{G}_{\ell}\coloneqq R_{\ell}\hat{G}
.

Then Itô’s formula can be applied to
z_{\ell}
:

(6.45)

\displaystyle\Phi(z_{\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=\Phi(z_{\ell}(0))+\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(z_{\ell}(s))[Az_{\ell}(s)+\hat{F}_{\ell}(z_{\ell}(s))]ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(z_{\ell}(s))\hat{G}_{\ell}(z_{\ell}(s))dW_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{tr}_{\hat{G}_{\ell}(z_{\ell}(s))}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z_{\ell}(s))ds\,,

where for any operator
T
on
\mathcal{U}
we have
\operatorname{tr}_{T}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z_{\ell}(s))=\sum_{j\geq 1}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z_{\ell}(s))[Tu_{j},Tu_{j}]
and
(u_{j})
is the orthonormal basis as in
3
.

We analyze each term on the right hand side separately. The following lemma treats the term containing
A
.

Lemma 6.32
.

For any
z\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
(not necessarily positive),
\Phi^{\prime}(z)(Az)\leq 0
.

Proof.

From
1
(i) (assumption on the spectrum of
A
) follows that there is

\lambda\leq 0
be such that
S(t)\nu=e^{\lambda t}\nu
and
S(t)^{*}\nu^{*}=e^{\lambda t}\nu^{*}
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}_{g}
denote the multiplication by a function
g
and denote
\mathcal{P}_{t}\coloneqq e^{-\lambda t}\mathfrak{m}_{\nu^{-1}}\circ S(t)\circ\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}
. It is standard to check that
\mathcal{P}_{t}1=1
and
\mathcal{P}_{t}
preserves positivity, and therefore
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is a Markov semigroup with the generator

\mathcal{L}u=-\lambda u+\nu^{-1}A(u\nu)
, and
\nu^{*}\nu
is a fixed point of
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{*}
. Since
\phi
is convex and
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is Markov,
by Jensen’s inequality and the invariance of
\nu^{*}\nu
we have

\int_{D}\phi(\mathcal{P}_{t}u)\nu^{*}\nu\leq\int_{D}\mathcal{P}_{t}\phi(u)\nu^{*}\nu=\int_{D}\phi(u)\nu^{*}\nu\,,

and so we conclude that

\int_{D}\phi^{\prime}(u)[-\lambda u+\nu^{-1}A(u\nu)]\nu^{*}\nu=\int_{D}\phi^{\prime}(u)(\mathcal{L}u)\nu^{*}\nu=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{D}\phi(\mathcal{P}_{t}u)\nu^{*}\nu\Big|_{t=0}\leq 0\,.

Using (
6.44
) and the above with
u=z\nu^{-1}
, one obtains

\Phi^{\prime}(z)(Az)=\int_{D}\phi^{\prime}(z\nu^{-1})(Az)\nu^{*}\leq\lambda\int_{D}\phi^{\prime}(z\nu^{-1})z\nu^{*}\,.

Since
\lambda\leq 0
and
\phi^{\prime}(z\nu^{-1})z\geq 0
(
\phi^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
and
\phi^{\prime}(0)=0
), the claim is proven.
∎

To estimate the other terms in (
6.45
) we observe that
by the convergence of the Yosida approximations (see
6.21
),
\Phi(z_{\ell}(t))\to\Phi(y^{*}(t))
and
\Phi(z_{\ell}(0))\to\Phi(x_{0})=0
because
x_{0}\in C(\overline{D})_{+}
. Moreover, since
\phi^{\prime}(x_{i})=\phi^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})=0
if
x_{i}\geq 0
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}(\cdot,R,\Psi_{n}(\psi(x)),\theta)=0
if
x_{i}\leq 0
(see
3
), it follows from
\hat{G}=(\tilde{\sigma}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{k})\circ\psi
that

\phi^{\prime}(x)\hat{G}(x)u_{\ell}=\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)\hat{G}(x)u_{\ell}=0
, and therefore

\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(y^{*}(s))\hat{G}(y^{*}(s))dW_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{tr}_{\hat{G}(y^{*}(s))}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(y^{*}(s))ds=0\,.

Using the same reasoning as for
\hat{G}
we obtain

\phi^{\prime}(x)(x\vee 0)f_{1}(x\vee 0)=0
. Then from

f(x)\geq x\cdot f_{1}(x)
if
x\geq 0
(
2
) and
\phi^{\prime}\leq 0
follows

\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(y^{*}(s))\hat{F}(y^{*}(s))ds=\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\prime}(y^{*}(s))F_{n}(y^{*}(s)\vee 0)ds\leq 0\,.

Using
6.22
to take the limit as
l\to\infty
on both sides of (
6.45
), we conclude from
6.32
and the above that
\Phi(y^{*}(t))\leq\Phi(x_{0})=0
almost surely, which finishes
Step 2
. Indeed, the assumptions of
6.22
are satisfied since
\|\Phi^{\prime}(z)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R})}+\|\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathbb{R}))}\lesssim 1
. To see this, note that
\phi
has uniformly bounded first and second derivatives, so by (
6.44
) it follows that

|\Phi^{\prime}(z)w|\leq\int_{D}|\phi^{\prime}(z\nu^{-1})w\nu^{*}|\lesssim\|w\|_{L^{1}}\,.

The inequality for
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
follows similarly using that
S(t)
is self-adjoint, and therefore
\nu^{*}\nu^{-1}=1
.

We remark that if we were to allow
S
not self-adjoint but the boundary conditions were still either Neumann or periodic, then
\nu
is bounded away from zero on
D
and this proof still works. However, in the case of Dirichlet boundary conditions one would have to be more careful and use arguments related to the boundary Harnack inequality.

To summarize, in
Step 2
we have shown for each
n\geq 1
that
x_{n}(t)\geq 0
almost surely.

Step 3:

Next we fix
n
and show that
y(t)=x_{n}(t)
solving
(
6.41
) satisfies
(vi)
. It is essentially a consequence of Itô’s formula applied to
\|\cdot\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
. For technical reasons we must use the Yosida approximations again, but since many steps are similar to the other Yosida approximation arguments in this paper (
Step 2
of this theorem,
6.22
, etc.) we omit some of the details.

First, we claim that
\hat{y}(t)=\pi_{u}\circ y(t)
(recall
\pi_{u}
is the projection on the first
d
coordinates) satisfies

(6.46)

\displaystyle\|\hat{y}(t)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\hat{y}(0)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}2\langle\hat{y}(s),F_{n}(y(s))\rangle_{\beta}+\|\pi_{u}\circ G_{n}(y(s))\|_{\beta,2}^{2}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle 2\hat{y}(s),G_{n}(y(s))dW_{s}\rangle_{\beta}-\int_{0}^{t}2\|\hat{y}(s)\|_{\beta+1,2}^{2}ds\,,

where
\beta
is as in
6.24
. By using the same argument, we obtain that (
6.46
) holds with

\pi_{u}
replaced by
\pi_{z}
and
\hat{y}
by
\pi_{z}\circ y
.

For each
\ell\geq 1
we replace
F_{n}
and
G_{n}
in (
6.41
) by the Yosida approximations (
6.21
) respectively
R_{\ell}F_{n}

and
R_{\ell}G_{n}
and denote
y_{\ell}
the corresponding strong solution
(see
6.21
for well posedness).
Applying the energy (or Itô) formula (see
[
LR15
, Theorem 4.2.5]
or
[
LR17
, Theorem 3.3.15]
) to
\hat{y}_{\ell}-\hat{y}_{k}
in the normal triple
(\mathcal{B}_{\beta+1/2,2},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2},\mathcal{B}_{\beta-1/2,2})
(note that
\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}
is a Hilbert space with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\beta}
), and noting
that the norm in
\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2})
is the same as the Hilbert-Schmidt norm
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2})
(see
[
vN10
, Proposition 13.5]
), we obtain

(6.47)

\|\hat{y}_{\ell}(t)-\hat{y}_{k}(t)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}=\|\hat{y}_{\ell}(0)-\hat{y}_{k}(0)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\\
\begin{aligned} &+\int_{0}^{t}2\langle\hat{y}_{\ell}(s)-\hat{y}_{k}(s),R_{\ell}F_{n}(y_{\ell}(s))-R_{k}F_{n}(y_{k}(s))\rangle_{\beta}\\
&+\|\pi_{u}\circ R_{\ell}G_{n}(y_{\ell}(s))-\pi_{u}\circ R_{k}G_{n}(y_{k}(s))\|_{\beta,2}^{2}ds\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}2\langle\hat{y}_{\ell}(s)-\hat{y}_{k}(s),[R_{\ell}G_{n}(y_{\ell}(s))-R_{k}G_{n}(y_{k}(s))]dW_{s}\rangle_{\beta}\\
&-\int_{0}^{t}2\|\hat{y}_{\ell}(s)-\hat{y}_{k}(s)\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}ds\,,\end{aligned}

where we used that
\langle Ay,y\rangle_{\beta}=-\langle(-A)^{1/2}y,(-A)^{1/2}y\rangle_{\beta}=-\|y\|_{\beta+1/2}^{2}
, because
A
is self-adjoint (
1
). Since
\beta\leq 0
and
F_{n}
is globally Lipschitz from
B
to
\mathcal{B}
, it is also globally Lipschitz from
B
to
\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}
since
\mathcal{B}\subset L^{2}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}
. Also, recall from
Step 1
that
G_{n}:B\to\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2})
is globally Lipschitz. Then it
follows by the same argument as
6.22
with
\Phi(x)=\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}

that all of the terms on the right hand side of (
6.47
) converge to
0
in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T],\mathbb{R}))
as
(\ell,k)\to\infty
except perhaps
\int_{0}^{t}2\|\hat{y}_{\ell}(s)-\hat{y}_{k}(s)\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}ds
. Since
\hat{y}_{l}\to\hat{y}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}))
for all
p,T>0
(
6.21
), the left hand side of (
6.47
) converges to
0
. Hence,
(\hat{y}_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
is Cauchy in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathcal{B}_{\beta+1/2,2})
, so
