\int_{0}^{t}2\|\hat{y}_{\ell}(s)\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}ds\to\int_{0}^{t}2\|\hat{y}(s)\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}ds
(the limit must be
\hat{y}
because
\hat{y}_{\ell}\to\hat{y}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}))
).
Then (
6.46
) follows
after setting
\hat{y}_{k}=0
and passing
\ell\to\infty

in (
6.47
).
.

For already fixed
n
define a (random) time

(6.48)

\tau_{n}\coloneqq\inf\{t>0\mid\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}>n-1\}\,,

and
\bar{x}_{n}(t)\coloneqq(Ry_{u}(t),y_{z}(t))
.
Note that (
6.46
) holds for
t\leq\tau_{n}
with

F_{n}
and
G_{n}
replaced respectively by
\tilde{F}
and
\tilde{G}_{u}
because
\Psi_{n}(x)=x
for
\|x\|_{B}\leq n-1
(see (
6.39
)).

We have by (
6.30
) that

(R\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ\tilde{F}(y(t))=F(\bar{x}_{n}(t))\,,

and the analogous identity holds with
F
replaced by
G
. Also,
\|\bar{x}_{n}(t)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}=R^{2}\|\pi_{u}\circ y(t)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}+\|\pi_{z}\circ y(t)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
, because
\langle\pi_{u}\circ y(t),\pi_{z}\circ y(t)\rangle_{\beta}=0
. Similarly

\displaystyle\|(R\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ G_{n}(y(t))\|_{\beta,2}^{2}=R^{2}\|\pi_{u}\circ G_{n}(y(t))\|_{\beta,2}^{2}+\|\pi_{z}\circ G_{n}(y(t))\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle R^{2}\langle\pi_{u}\circ y(s),F_{n}(y(s))\rangle_{\beta}+\langle\pi_{z}\circ y(s),F_{n}(y(s))\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\langle R\pi_{u}\circ y(s),R\pi_{u}\circ F_{n}(y(s))\rangle_{\beta}+\langle\pi_{z}\circ y(s),\pi_{z}\circ F_{n}(y(s))\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\langle(R\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ y(s),(R\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ F_{n}(y(s))\rangle_{\beta}\,,

and the analogous statement holds with
F
replaced by
G
.
Thus, a multiplication of (
6.46
) by
R^{2}
and an addition to (
6.46
) with
u
replaced by
z
implies
for
t\leq\tau_{n}
that

\displaystyle\|\bar{x}_{n}(t)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\bar{x}_{n}(0)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}2\langle\bar{x}_{n}(s),F(\bar{x}_{n}(s))\rangle_{\beta}+\|G(\bar{x}_{n}(s))\|_{\beta,2}^{2}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle 2\bar{x}_{n}(s),G(\bar{x}_{n}(s))dW_{s}\rangle_{\beta}-\int_{0}^{t}2\|\bar{x}_{n}(s)\|_{\beta+1,2}^{2}ds\,.

To summarize, in
Step 3
we showed that
(vi)
holds with
\bar{x}_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n})
in place of
\bar{x}_{t}
.

Step 4:

We fix
n
and show that
y(t)=x_{n}(t)
solving (
6.41
) satisfies
(v)
until
\tau_{n}
as in (
6.48
). Throughout, we assume
u_{0}\coloneqq(x_{0})_{u}\neq 0
as postulated in
(v)
. Let
\hat{F}\coloneqq(F_{n})_{u}
,
\hat{G}\coloneqq(G_{n})_{u}
,
where
F_{n}
and
G_{n}
are as in (
6.40
). Note that
\hat{F}
and
\hat{G}
are different than the ones in Step 2.
We start by noticing that

(6.49)

\|y_{u}(t)\|_{L^{1}}=\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle y(s),\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle+\langle\hat{F}(y(s)),1_{u}\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\hat{G}(y(s))^{*}1_{u},dW_{s}\rangle,

where
\tilde{e}
is as in
6.29
and the adjoint of
\tilde{G}_{u}(y(s))
is in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Indeed, by
Step 2

y(t)\geq 0
, thus
\|y_{u}(t)\|_{L^{1}}=\langle y_{u}(t),1_{u}\rangle
, so (
6.49
) almost follows by
6.22
applied to
\Phi(x)\coloneqq\langle x_{u},1_{u}\rangle
. The careful reader may notice that
\hat{G}
is not globally Lipschitz from
B
to
\gamma(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
, but since the Itô correction term is
0
and the stochastic term
\Phi^{\prime}(x)\hat{G}(x)
is equal to
\hat{G}(x)^{*}1_{u}
, it is enough to have
\hat{G}
globally Lipschitz from
B
to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
. This follows because
H\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
(
3
) and
\tilde{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{n}
is globally Lipschitz (for fixed
R
).

Consequently, the
dr
portion of (
6.37
) follows for
t\leq\tau_{n}
after multiplication by
R
on both sides of (
6.49
) and
using for
R>0
,
\|y\|_{B}\leq n
, and
\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}\neq 0
that

(6.50)

R\hat{F}(y)=R\tilde{F}_{u}(R,y)=F(Ry_{u},y_{z})=R\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}\tilde{F}_{u}\Big(R\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}},\frac{y_{u}}{\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}},y_{z}\Big)\,,

which in
(r,v,z)
coordinates is equal to
r\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z)
,
and similarly for
\hat{G}
term. Note that if
R=0
, the statement is trivial since
r_{t}=0
for all
t
.

To obtain the
dv
portion of (
6.37
), we have to guarantee that
\|y_{u}(t)\|_{L^{1}}
does not vanish (indeed, if
\|y_{u}(t)\|_{L^{1}}=0
then
v_{t}
is not even well-defined). Thus, for every
\epsilon>0

we define

(6.51)

\sigma_{\epsilon}\coloneqq\inf\{t>0\mid\|y_{u}(t)\|_{L^{1}}<\epsilon\}

and apply (
6.49
) and Itô’s formula to the semi-martingale
\|y_{u}(t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{1}}
to obtain

\displaystyle d\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Big(-\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}^{-2}[\langle y_{u},\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle+\langle\hat{F}(y),1_{u}\rangle]+\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}^{-3}\|\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\Big)dt

\displaystyle-\langle\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}^{-2}\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u},dW_{t}\rangle\,.

y_{t}
is a strong solution in
\mathcal{B}_{-1}
by
6.19
((
6.15
) follows from the global Lipschitz properties of
F_{n}
and
G_{n}
noted in
Step 1
), so by
[
BvNVW08
, Corollary 2.6]
we have the following equality in
\mathcal{B}_{-1}
(
v\coloneqq y_{u}/\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}
and
\rho\coloneqq\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}^{-1}
):

(6.52)

\displaystyle dv

\displaystyle=\Big(-\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle-\rho\langle\hat{F}(y),1_{u}\rangle+\rho^{2}\|\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\Big)vdt-\langle\rho\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u},dW_{t}\rangle v

\displaystyle+[A_{u}v+\rho\hat{F}(y)]dt+\rho\hat{G}(y)dW_{t}-\rho^{2}\hat{G}(y)\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u}dt\,,

for all
t\leq\sigma_{\epsilon}
. We want to use
6.19
to show that (
6.52
) holds in
\mathcal{B}
in the mild sense of
6.17
. Thus, we need to verify (
6.13
). We start by showing that for some small enough
\eta>0
(independent of
\epsilon
)

(6.53)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\|b_{t}\|_{-1+\eta}+\|c_{t}\|_{-1/2+\eta}^{2}dt\Big]<\infty\,,

where

\displaystyle b_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big(-\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle-\rho\langle\hat{F}(y),1_{u}\rangle+\rho^{2}\|\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\Big)v+\rho\hat{F}(y)-\rho^{2}\hat{G}(y)\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u}

\displaystyle c_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\langle\rho\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u},\cdot\rangle v+\rho\hat{G}(y)

Using
6.24
(especially (
6.18
)) to handle the terms involving
\hat{G}
, global Lipschitz properties of
\tilde{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{n}
and
\hat{F}
such as
|\hat{F}(y)|\lesssim|y_{u}|
(see
6.28
for further explanation),

\rho^{2}\hat{G}(y)\hat{G}(y)^{*}1_{u}=\rho^{2}\tilde{\sigma}HH^{*}(\pi_{u}\circ\tilde{\sigma})
, and our definition
\rho y_{u}=v
with
\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1
, we derive the following bounds:

(6.54)

\displaystyle\|b\|_{-1+\eta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\|v\|_{L^{1}}+\rho\|y_{u}\|_{L^{1}}\Big)\|v\|_{-1+\eta}+\rho\|y_{u}\|_{L^{r}}+(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{r}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\vee\|v\|_{L^{r}}

\displaystyle\|c\|_{-1/2+\eta}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{r}})^{2}+\rho^{2}\|y_{u}\|_{L^{r}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\vee\|v\|_{L^{r}})^{2}\,,

where the implicit constants are independent of
\epsilon
(see the definition of
\sigma_{\epsilon}
in (
6.51
)).
Then (
6.53
) follows from
\rho_{t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\leq\epsilon^{-1}
and
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\leq T}\|y_{t}\|_{B}^{p}]<\infty
for all
p,T>0
(see
6.20
and note we assumed
r>N
and
y=x_{n}
is the mild solution of (
6.41
)). So indeed we can apply
6.19
to deduce that (
6.52
) holds in the mild sense of
6.17
up to time
\sigma_{\epsilon}
for every fixed
\epsilon>0
. To establish uniform bounds in
\epsilon
, we first use
6.13
(see also
6.18
) and (
6.54
) to conclude that for any small
\eta>0

(6.55)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\|v_{t}\|_{\eta/2}^{2}\Big]\lesssim\|v_{0}\|_{L^{r}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{r}}^{2}\vee\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{4}\Big]

for all
T,\epsilon>0
, where the implicit constants are independent of
\epsilon
. Next, by Sobolev embedding
L^{1}\subset W^{-N-1,r}
for all
1<r<\infty
, and thus with
a=(N+1)/(\eta+N+1)<1
we have by interpolation and
\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1
that

(6.56)

\|v\|_{L^{r}}\leq\|v\|_{W^{\eta,r}}^{a}\|v\|_{W^{-N-1,r}}^{1-a}\lesssim\|v\|_{W^{\eta,r}}^{a}\,.

Then
6.10
and Young’s inequality imply that
\|v\|^{2}_{L^{r}}
vanishes over
\|v\|_{\eta/2}^{2}
(
2.9
). In addition, by (
6.56
) and Hölder’s inequality with
b(r)=r/(2(r-1))\to 1/2
as
r\to\infty

\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{4}\leq\|v\|_{L^{r}}^{4b(r)}\|v\|_{L^{1}}^{4(1-b(r))}=\|v\|_{L^{r}}^{4b(r)}\lesssim\|v\|_{\eta/2}^{4ab(r)}\,,

and so
\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{4}
(thus
\|v\|_{L^{r}}^{2}\vee\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{4}
as well) also vanishes over
\|v\|_{\eta/2}^{2}
when
r
is large enough. We conclude from (
6.55
) that

(6.57)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{r}}^{2}\vee\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{4}\Big]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\|v_{t}\|_{\eta/2}^{2}\Big]\lesssim\|v_{0}\|_{L^{r}}^{2}+1\,,

where the implicit constants are independent of
\epsilon
.

In the Step 5 below, we establish the following result.

Lemma 6.33
.

We have
\sigma_{\epsilon}\to\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
almost surely.

Then by
6.33
, (
6.53
) and (
6.57
) hold with
\sigma_{\epsilon}
removed and we conclude that
v_{t}
is a mild solution in
\mathcal{B}
(in the sense of
6.17
) to (
6.52
). Also, if
\|y\|_{B}\leq n
, then
\rho\hat{F}(y)=\tilde{F}_{u}(R,y_{u},y_{z})=\tilde{F}_{u}(R\rho^{-1},\rho y_{u},y_{z})
and similarly
\rho\hat{G}(y)=\tilde{G}_{u}(R\rho^{-1},\rho y_{u},y_{z})
. Since
r=R\rho^{-1}
and
v=\rho y_{u}
, the
dv
portion of (
6.37
) follows for
x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n})
by (
6.52
).

To summarize, in
Step 4
we have shown that
(v)
holds with
x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n})
in place of
x_{t}
.

Step 5:

Next we fix
n
and show that
y(t)=x_{n}(t)
satisfies
(ii)
and
6.33
holds. First assume
u_{0}=0
and observe that
\hat{F}(0,y_{z})=(F_{n})_{u}(0,y_{z})=0
and
\hat{G}(0,y_{z})=(G_{n})_{u}(0,y_{z})=0
. Thus, if
z(t)
is the solution to

z(t)=S_{z}(t)z_{0}+(S_{z}*F_{n}(0,z))_{t}+(S_{z}\diamond\tilde{G}(0,z))_{t}\,,\quad z(0)=z_{0}

(existence of
z
follows in the same way as existence of
x_{n}
in
Step 1
), then
(0,z(t))
satisfies (
6.41
). By the uniqueness of mild solutions (see
6.20
),
y(t)=(0,z(t))
, and therefore
y_{u}(t)=0
. Thus, it remains to show that
\tilde{u}_{0}\neq 0\implies y_{u}(t)\neq 0
.

Throughout the rest of this step we assume
\tilde{u}_{0}\neq 0
.
Define the function
h:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
by
h(x)\coloneqq g(-\ln x)
, where
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a nonnegative smooth function such that
g(x)=x
on
[1,\infty)
,
g(x)=0
on
(-\infty,0]
, and
g
has bounded first and second derivatives.

Recall
\|y_{u}(t)\|_{L^{1}}
is a
\mathbb{R}
-valued semi-martingale,
\rho_{t}\coloneqq\|y_{u}(t)\|^{-1}_{L^{1}}
,

v_{t}=\rho_{t}y_{u}(t)
, and
\sigma_{\epsilon}
is the stopping time defined in (
6.51
).

Fix any small
\epsilon>0
and use (
6.49
) and
Itô’s formula to calculate

(6.58)

h(\|y_{u}(t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{1}})=h(\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}})+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\mu_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\langle\nu_{s},dW_{s}\rangle\,,

where

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq h^{\prime}(\rho_{t}^{-1})[\langle y_{u}(t),\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle+\langle\hat{F}(y_{t}),1_{u}\rangle]+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}(\rho_{t}^{-1})\|\hat{G}(y_{t})^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq h^{\prime}(\rho_{t}^{-1})\hat{G}(y_{t})^{*}1_{u}\,.

If
\rho_{t}\in[e,\infty)
for some
t
, then

|h^{\prime}(\rho_{t}^{-1})|=\rho_{t}
and
|h^{\prime\prime}(\rho_{t}^{-1})|=\rho_{t}^{2}
.
If
\rho_{t}\in(0,e)
, then

|h^{\prime}(\rho_{t}^{-1})|\leq\|g^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}|\rho_{t}|
and
|h^{\prime\prime}(\rho_{t}^{-1})|\leq(\|g^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}})+\|g^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}})|\rho_{t}|^{2}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle|\mu_{t}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\langle v_{t},\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle|+\rho_{t}|\langle\hat{F}(y_{t}),1_{u}\rangle|+\rho_{t}^{2}\|\hat{G}(y_{t})^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\|\nu_{t}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\rho_{t}^{2}\|\hat{G}(y_{t})^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\,.

Then using (
6.25
), global Lipschitz properties of
\tilde{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{n}
and
\hat{F}
such as
|\hat{F}(y)|\lesssim|y_{u}|
(see
6.28
for further explanation), we have

|\mu_{t}|+\|\nu_{t}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\lesssim\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{4}\,.

It follows from (
6.57
) that
\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}\langle\nu_{s},dW_{s}\rangle
is a martingale and that there is
M>0
, independent of
\epsilon
, such that
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon}}|\mu_{s}|ds]\leq M
. Using
h(\|y_{u}(\sigma_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{1}})=-\ln\epsilon
(for small enough
\epsilon
), Chebyshev inequality, and
optional stopping one observes

(-\ln\epsilon)\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{\epsilon}<t)\leq\mathbb{E}[h(\|y_{u}(t\wedge\sigma_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{1}})]\leq h(\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}})+M\,,

and thus
\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{\epsilon}<t)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. We conclude that
\sigma_{\epsilon}\to\infty
almost surely, which means that
\|y_{u}(t)\|_{L^{1}}>0
for all
t
and
6.33
holds, as desired.

To summarize, in
Step 5
we have shown that
(ii)
holds with
x_{n}(t)
in place of
x_{t}
.

Step 6:

Next if
x_{n}
solves (
6.41
) we prove a uniform bound on
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\leq 1}\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}^{p}]
for all (large enough)
p
.
Using the same argument as in
6.14
with
q=\infty
, we conclude that by choosing small
\eta>0
and
r
large enough, we have for all
t\geq 0

(6.59)

\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{B}+\|(S*F_{n}^{+}(x_{n}))_{t}\|_{\eta/2}+\|(S\diamond G_{n}(x_{n}))_{t}\|_{\eta/2}\,,

where
F_{n}^{+}\coloneqq F_{n}\vee 0
and the constant in the
\lesssim
depends only on
r,\eta
(and
t
, but the dependence is uniform over
t\leq T
). By decreasing
\eta
and choosing
\beta>-1/2
sufficiently close to
-1/2
, there are
\eta
and
\beta
satisfying respectively
6.14
(
6.8
) (with
q=\infty
) and
6.24
and additionally
\eta=\beta+1/2>0
.

By
6.15
and (
6.59
) it follows that (for all large enough
p
)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}^{p}\Big]\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{B}^{p}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\|F_{n}^{+}(x_{n}(s))\|^{p}ds\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\|G_{n}(x_{n}(s))\|_{\eta-1/2}^{p}ds\Big]\,.

By (
6.35
),
\|F_{n}^{+}(x)\|\lesssim\|x\|+1+R\|x\|
and
\|G_{n}(x)\|_{\eta-1/2}\lesssim\|x\|+R\|x\|
(where the constants do not depend on
n
), so we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\leq t}\|x_{n}(s)\|_{B}^{p}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{B}^{p}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}1+\|x_{n}(s)\|^{p}ds\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{B}^{p}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}1+\sup_{r\leq s}\|x_{n}(r)\|_{B}^{p}ds\Big]\,.

For every
T>0
, by Gronwall’s inequality there is some
C>0
(independent of
n
and uniform over
\theta\in\Theta
and
R,T
in bounded subsets of
[0,\infty)
) such that

(6.60)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\leq T}\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}^{p}]\leq C(1+\|x_{0}\|_{B}^{p})\,.

By Chebyshev’s inequality we conclude
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\tau_{n}<T)=0
(see (
6.48
)), and thus
\sup_{n}\tau_{n}=\infty
almost surely.

Step 7:

Next we fix integers
m<n
and define
\sigma\coloneqq\inf\{t>0\mid\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}>m\}
. Note that
F_{n}(x_{n}(t\wedge\sigma))=F_{m}(x_{n}(t\wedge\sigma))
and
G_{n}(x_{n}(t\wedge\sigma))=G_{m}(x_{n}(t\wedge\sigma))
.
Also, if
\tau
is a bounded stopping time and
Z_{s}
is such that
Z_{s}1_{s\leq\tau}=0
, then
\int_{0}^{t}S(t-s)Z_{s}1_{s\leq\tau\wedge t}dW_{s}=0
and by
[
vNVW07
, Proposition 5.3]
, we have the following localization result:

(S\diamond Z)_{\tau}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\tau}S(\tau-s)Z_{s}dW_{s}=0
.
Setting
\tau=t\wedge\sigma
and
Z_{s}=G_{n}(x_{n}(s))-G_{m}(x_{n}(s))
and using the analogous (obvious) localization result for deterministic convolutions, we obtain

x_{n}(t\wedge\sigma)=S(t\wedge\sigma)x_{0}+(S*F_{m}(x_{n}))_{t\wedge\sigma}+(S\diamond G_{m}(x_{n}))_{t\wedge\sigma}\,.

Then by our choice of
\eta
as
r
in
Step 6
(cf. (
6.59
)) we have

\displaystyle\|x_{n}(t\wedge\sigma)-x_{m}(t\wedge\sigma)\|_{B}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(S*F_{m}(x_{n}))_{t\wedge\sigma}-(S*F_{m}(x_{m}))_{t\wedge\sigma}\|_{\eta/2}

\displaystyle+\|(S\diamond G_{m}(x_{n}))_{t\wedge\sigma}-(S\diamond G_{m}(x_{m}))_{t\wedge\sigma}\|_{\eta/2}\,.

Since
F_{m}:B\to\mathcal{B}
and
G_{m}:B\to\mathcal{B}_{-1/2+\eta}
are globally Lipschitz (see
Step 1
), by
6.15
we have (for all large enough
p
)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}\|x_{n}(s\wedge\sigma)-x_{m}(s\wedge\sigma)\|_{B}^{p}\Big]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\|x_{n}(s\wedge\sigma)-x_{m}(s\wedge\sigma)\|_{B}^{p}ds\Big]\,.

We conclude by Gronwall’s inequality that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\leq T}\|x_{n}(t\wedge\sigma)-x_{m}(t\wedge\sigma)\|_{B}^{p}\Big]=0\,.

On the event
\{\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}\leq m-1\text{ for all }t\}
, clearly
\tau_{m}\leq\sigma
because they are both
\infty
. Otherwise there is some (random)
t<\sigma
such that
m-1<\|x_{n}(t)\|_{B}=\|x_{m}(t)\|_{B}<m
, and again we conclude
\tau_{m}\leq\sigma
. Thus,
x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{m})=x_{m}(t\wedge\tau_{m})
almost surely and
\tau_{m}\leq\tau_{n}
.

It follows from
\sup_{n}\tau_{n}=\infty
(
Step 6
) that there exists a unique process
x_{t}
which satisfies
x_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}=x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n})
for all
n
and
t>0
. Since
\Psi_{n}(x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n}))=x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n})
, then
x_{t}
is a mild solution of (
6.36
) (note that by
Step 6

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\leq T}\|x_{t}\|_{B}^{p}]<\infty
for all
p
, and thus
6.17
holds with
\epsilon=\eta
by (
6.35
) and the polynomial boundedness of
\tilde{F}
assumed in
2
). If
z_{t}
is another mild solution, then the same argument as presented above shows that
z_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}=x_{n}(t\wedge\tau_{n})
, so (
6.36
) has a unique mild solution.

Step 8:
In the previous steps we have shown that
x_{n}(t)
satisfies
(i)
-
(vi)
, at least until
\tau_{n}
. We claim that
x_{t}
also satisfies
(i)
-
(vi)
for all
t\geq 0
. The only non-trivial proof is that of
(iv)
, so we present below and leave the rest to the reader.

First, Chebyshev inequality and (
6.60
) imply

(6.61)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(x_{n}(t)\neq x(t)\text{ in }C([0,T];B)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\tau_{n}<T)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[\|x_{n}(t)\|_{C([0,T];B)}^{p}\Big]}{(n-1)^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+\|x_{0}\|_{B}^{p})}{(n-1)^{p}}\,,

where
C
(and thus the right-hand side) is bounded
uniformly in
\theta\in\Theta,R\leq M,x_{0}\in\{\|\cdot\|_{B}\leq M\}
for any fixed
M,T,p>0
. Thus, if
x_{n}^{(k)}
denote the solutions to (
6.41
) with initial condition
(R^{(k)},x_{0}^{(k)},\theta^{(k)})
and
(R^{(k)},x_{0}^{(k)},\theta^{(k)})\to(R^{(\infty)},x_{0}^{(\infty)},\theta^{(\infty)})
as
k\to\infty
in
\mathbb{R}\times B\times\Theta
, then by (
6.61
) for all
\delta>0
there exists
n
such that, for all

k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(x_{n}^{(k)}\neq x^{(k)}\text{ in }C([0,T];B)\Big)<\delta\,.

For this fixed
n
,
x_{n}
satisfies
(iv)
(see
Step 1
), so we have
x_{n}^{(k)}\to x_{n}^{(\infty)}
as
k\to\infty
in probability. Then there is
K=K(n)
such that, for all
k>K
,
\mathbb{P}\big(\|x_{n}^{(k)}-x_{n}^{(\infty)}\|_{C([0,T];B)}>\delta\big)<\delta
, and thus
\mathbb{P}\big(\|x^{(k)}-x^{(\infty)}\|_{C([0,T];B)}>\delta\big)<3\delta
. It follows that
x^{(k)}\to x^{(\infty)}
in probability, and thus also in
L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];B))
for all
p>0
since by (
6.60
) and monotone convergence we have
\sup_{k}\mathbb{E}[\|x^{(k)}(t)\|_{C([0,T];B)}^{2p}]<\infty
. (Indeed,
x_{n}^{(k)}(\cdot\wedge\tau_{n})\to x^{(k)}(\cdot)
almost surely and
\|x_{n}^{(k)}(\cdot\wedge\tau_{n})\|_{C([0,T];B)}
is increasing in
n
by
Step 7
.)

∎

Remark 6.34
.

Although our proof of
6.31

(i)
was inspired by
[
NY19
, Lemma 3.1]
,

our proof has extra approximation steps, because

[
NY19
]
uses the following unjustified statement:

(6.62)

Suppose
f_{2}=0
in
2
. Since the resolvent is positivity preserving,
\phi^{\prime}(x)R_{m}F(x\vee 0)=0

for any function
\phi
which satisfies
\phi(x)=0
for
x\geq 0
, and similarly other terms in the Itô formula vanish.

It is not obvious why such claim should be true because here
x
is a function and “positivity preserving” is not a pointwise property. Suppose
x
takes positive and negative values, so
x\vee 0
is not constantly zero and there is some
a
such that
x(a)<0
. Consider the simple case of the 1-dimensional heat equation on the torus and
F(x)=x
. Even though
F(x\vee 0)(a)=0
,
R_{m}F(x\vee 0)(a)
is some weighted integral of the (nonzero, nonnegative) function
F(x\vee 0)
, and thus
R_{m}F(x\vee 0)(a)>0
. Since
\phi^{\prime}(x(a))\neq 0
in general, we cannot conclude that
\phi^{\prime}(x(a))R_{m}F(x\vee 0)(a)=0
.

However, (
6.62
) is true without
R_{m}
, that is, since
(x\vee 0)(a)=0
implies that
F(x\vee 0)(a)=0
. Thus we have
\phi^{\prime}(x)F(x\vee 0)=0
. So after applying the Itô formula we pass
m\to\infty
to remove
R_{m}
. In order to justify this limit for the Itô correction (second-order) term we need
G(x)\in\gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B})
, which motivates the approximation of
G=xH
by
xR_{k}H
.

Corollary 6.35
.

Under
1
-
3

the problem (
6.1
) is well-posed for
(x_{0},\theta)\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\times\Theta
in the sense that it has a unique mild solution (in
L^{r}
for some large
r
) (
6.17
) which is continuous in time and depends continuously on its initial condition.

Proof.

The proof is a direct consequence of
6.31
because (
6.36
) and (
6.1
) coincide when
R=1
(see
6.26
).
∎

From the well-posedness of (
6.36
) also follows the well-posedness of the projective process (
6.10
). Below, we given an alternative definition of (
6.10
) (
6.36
) and show that our definitions coincide (
6.37
).

Definition 6.36
.

Given

(r_{0},x_{0},\theta)\in\mathcal{M}\coloneqq[0,\infty)\times\{x\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|x_{u}\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times\Theta\,,

let
\tilde{x}_{t}=(\tilde{u}_{t},\tilde{z}_{t})=(\tilde{u}_{t},z_{t})
be the unique mild solution to

\tilde{x}_{t}=S(t)x_{0}+(S*\tilde{F}(r_{0},\tilde{x},\theta))_{t}+(S\diamond\tilde{G}(r_{0},\tilde{x},\theta))_{t}\,,

with initial condition
\tilde{x}_{0}=x_{0}

as guaranteed by
6.31
. As in
6.31

(v)
, define
(r_{t},v_{t})\coloneqq(r_{0}\|\tilde{u}_{t}\|_{L^{1}},\tilde{u}_{t}/\|\tilde{u}_{t}\|_{L^{1}})
.

We also define
\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}\cap\{r=0\}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

Corollary 6.37
.

Using notation from
6.36
,
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
forms a Markov process on
\mathcal{M}
which satisfies
1
and
2
. Furthermore,
(r_{t}v_{t},z_{t})
is the unique mild solution to (
6.1
) with initial condition
(r_{0}v_{0},z_{0})=(r_{0}\tilde{u}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})
.

Proof.

Since
\|\tilde{u}_{0}\|_{L^{1}}=\|\pi_{u}x_{0}\|_{L^{1}}=1
, we have

\tilde{u}_{0}\neq 0
, and by
6.31
(ii)

\tilde{u}_{t}\neq 0
for all
t\geq 0
. Thus,
r_{t}=r_{0}\|\tilde{u}_{t}\|_{L^{1}}=0
if and only if
r_{0}=0
and

1
follows.

To show
2
, that is,
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
pointwise for each
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
, it is equivalent to show:

(i)

X_{t}^{x_{n}}\to X_{t}^{x}
in distribution
as
x_{n}\to x
.

(ii)

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}X_{t}^{x}=x
in distribution.

Since
(r_{0},u,z)\mapsto(r_{0}\|u\|_{L^{1}},u/\|u\|_{L^{1}},z)
is continuous as a map from

[0,\infty)\times\{(u,z)\in C(\overline{D})_{+}:u\neq 0\}
to
[0,\infty)\times C(\overline{D})_{+}
, (i) follows from
6.31
(iv)
and (ii) from
6.31
(iii)
.

Finally,
applying
r_{0}\pi_{u}+\pi_{z}
to both sides of (
6.36
) and using that
\pi_{u}
and
\pi_{z}
commute with
S
, shows that
\hat{x}_{t}\coloneqq(r_{0}\tilde{u}_{t},z_{t})=(r_{t}v_{t},z_{t})
satisfies

\hat{x}_{t}=S(t)(r_{0}v_{0},z_{0})+(S*(r_{0}\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ\tilde{F}(r_{0},\tilde{x},\theta))_{t}+(S\diamond(r_{0}\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ\tilde{G}(r_{0},\tilde{x},\theta))_{t}

and
\hat{x}_{0}=(r_{0}v_{0},z_{0})
.
Hence, the final claim follows from
(r_{0}\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ\tilde{F}(r_{0},\tilde{x},\theta)=F(r_{0}\tilde{u},z,\theta)=F(\hat{x},\theta)
and
(r_{0}\pi_{u}+\pi_{z})\circ\tilde{G}(r_{0},\tilde{x},\theta)=G(r_{0}\tilde{u},z,\theta)=G(\hat{x},\theta)
(see (
6.30
)) and uniqueness of mild solutions (
6.35
).
∎

6.3.
Lyapunov Functions

In this section, we assume
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
is the fixed Markov process on
\mathcal{M}
defined in
6.36
and
\beta
is as in
6.24
. In most of the expressions below we omit the dependence on
\theta
because it is irrelevant (for example, we write
(r,v,z)\in\mathcal{M}
). We also write
x_{t}=(r_{t}v_{t},z_{t})
(see
6.37
).

We introduce the Lyapunov functions which are central to our analysis. In particular, we introduce functions which satisfy
3
and
5
(i)
-
(iii)
, as well as the function satisfying
4
. We encourage the reader to recall
\mathcal{L}
and
\Gamma

introduced respectively in
2.3
and
2.4
.

Lemma 6.38
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}
or
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}_{+}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
and
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
,
b:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, and
\sigma:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R})\cong\mathcal{U}
be measurable functions such that
df(r,v,z)=b(r,v,z)dt+\sigma(r,v,z)dW_{t}
holds, meaning that for all
(r_{0},v_{0},z_{0})\in\mathcal{A}

we have

(6.63)

f(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})=f(r_{0},v_{0},z_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}b(r_{s},v_{s},z_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(r_{s},v_{s},z_{s})dW_{s}

and all the integrals in (
6.63
) are well-defined. Then the following statements hold:

(i)

If
f\geq 0
, then
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
with
\mathcal{L}f=b
.

(ii)

Assume
\hat{f},\hat{b},\hat{\sigma}
satisfy the same assumptions as
f,b,\sigma

above,
\hat{f}\geq 0
, and there are some constants
K,C>0
such that
\sup_{A}(\|\sigma\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}+K\hat{b})=:C<\infty
. Then
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
with
\mathcal{L}f=b
and
\Gamma f=\|\sigma\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.

Proof.

Note that (i) directly follows from the definitions and properties of (stochastic) integration. To prove (ii), first note that by
(i) we have
\hat{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
and that
\mathcal{L}\hat{f}=\hat{b}\leq(C-\|\sigma\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2})/K
. Then as in
[
FS24
, Lemma 4.1]
we obtain that
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{t}\|\sigma(r_{s},v_{s},z_{s})\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}ds]<\infty
, and consequently
\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(r_{s},v_{s},z_{s})dW_{s}
is a square integrable martingale with quadratic variation
\int_{0}^{t}\|\sigma(r_{s},v_{s},z_{s})\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}ds
, proving the claim.
∎

We use
6.38
to prove that the Lyapunov functions defined below indeed belong to the extended domains
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}
and
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}
. Even if the function
f
from
6.38
is not known yet to be in
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}
, we make the convention of writing
\Gamma f=\|\sigma\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
. This leads to following notation:

Definition 6.39
.

If
\mathcal{A},f,b,\sigma
are as in
6.38
and
f\geq 0
, then we write
f\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{A})
(this is a definition of
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{A})
) and formally set
\mathcal{L}f=b
and
\Gamma f=\|\sigma\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.

Let be
h:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
given by
h(x)\coloneqq g(-\ln x)
, where
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a nonnegative smooth function such that

(6.64)

g(x)=\begin{cases}x&x\geq 1\\
0&x\leq 0\end{cases}

and
g
has bounded first and second derivatives. Our average Lyapunov function for
4
is

(6.65)

V(r,v,z)\coloneqq h(r)\,.

Lemma 6.40
.

For
V
as in (
6.65
) we have
V\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
(see
6.39
) and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}V(r,v,z)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime}(r)r\Big[\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle+\langle\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z),1_{u}\rangle\Big]+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}(r)r^{2}\|\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u}\|_{U}^{2}

\displaystyle\Gamma V(r,v,z)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime}(r)^{2}r^{2}\|\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u}\|_{U}^{2}\,,

where the adjoint of
G_{u}(r,v,z)
is taken in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
and
\tilde{e}
is as in
6.29
.

Proof.

Recall that by
6.31
(v)
the process
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})
satisfies (
6.37
). Then the claim follows immediately from Itô’s formula and
6.38
.
∎

To construct
W
for
3
, we combine the following functions (here
x=(rv,z)
):

(6.66)

\displaystyle W_{1}(r,v,z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}=1+\frac{1}{2}\|(rv,z)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}

\displaystyle W_{2}(r,v,z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\|x\|_{L^{1}}=1+\langle x,1\rangle=1+r\langle v,1_{u}\rangle+\langle z,1_{z}\rangle\,.

Lemma 6.41
.

For
W_{1},W_{2}
from (
6.66
) we have
W_{1},W_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
and with
x=(rv,z)
:

(6.67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}W_{1}(r,v,z)

\displaystyle=-\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+\langle x,F(x)\rangle_{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\|G(x)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}

\displaystyle\Gamma W_{1}(r,v,z)

\displaystyle=\|G(x)^{*}x\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\,,

where the adjoint of
G(x)
is taken in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2})
. Moreover,

(6.68)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}W_{2}(r,v,z)

\displaystyle=\langle x,\tilde{e}\rangle+\langle F(x),1\rangle

\displaystyle\Gamma W_{2}(r,v,z)

\displaystyle=\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\,,

where the adjoint of
G(x)
is taken in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
and
\tilde{e}
is as in
6.29
.

Proof.

Recall that by
6.31
(vi)
the process

x_{t}=(r_{t}v_{t},z_{t})
(denoted
\bar{x}
in
6.31
(vi)
) satisfies (
6.38
) with
\bar{x}
replaced by
x
and (
6.67
) follows.

Using that
x
satisfies (
6.1
) (
6.37
), applying Itô’s formula to
\Phi(x)=\langle x,1\rangle
shows

d\|x\|_{L^{1}}=[\langle x,\tilde{e}\rangle+\langle F(x),1\rangle]dt+\langle G(x)^{*}1,dW_{t}\rangle\,,

and (
6.68
) holds. (Technically since we work with mild solutions one cannot apply Itô’s formula directly. Since the details of the truncation + Yosida approximation argument that handles this subtelty are contained in the proof of
6.31
, we do not repeat the argument here. See the proof of (
6.49
).)

The claim
W_{1},W_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
follows from
6.38
.
∎

Corollary 6.42
.

For all
p,C>0
we have
W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p}\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
and the following hold with
x=(rv,z)
:

\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}(W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p})&=\frac{p}{2}W_{1}^{p/2-1}\mathcal{L}W_{1}+\frac{p}{4}(p/2-1)W_{1}^{p/2-2}\Gamma W_{1}\\
&\qquad+pCW_{2}^{p-1}\mathcal{L}W_{2}+\frac{p}{2}(p-1)CW_{2}^{p-2}\Gamma W_{2}\end{aligned}\\
\begin{aligned} &=\frac{p}{2^{p/2}}(2+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p/2-1}\Big(-\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+\langle x,F(x)\rangle_{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\|G(x)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\Big)\\
&\qquad+\frac{p}{2^{p/2}}(p/2-1)(2+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p/2-2}\|G(x)^{*}x\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\\
&\qquad+pC(1+\|x\|_{L^{1}})^{p-1}\Big(\langle x,\tilde{e}\rangle+\langle F(x),1\rangle\Big)\\
&\qquad+\frac{p}{2}(p-1)C(1+\|x\|_{L^{1}})^{p-2}\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\end{aligned}

and

\Gamma(W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p})\lesssim(p/2)^{2}W_{1}^{p-2}\Gamma W_{1}+p^{2}C^{2}W_{2}^{2p-2}\Gamma W_{2}\\
=\frac{p^{2}}{2^{p}}(2+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p-2}\|G(x)^{*}x\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}+p^{2}C^{2}(1+\|x\|_{L^{1}})^{2p-2}\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Follows immediately from
6.41
, Itô’s formula, and
6.38
.
∎

SPDE Assumption 4
.

There are
K,c>0
such that

(6.69)

\langle x,\tilde{e}\rangle+\langle F(x),1\rangle=\langle Ax+F(x),1\rangle\leq K-c\|x\|_{L^{1}}\,,

for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(A)\cap C(\overline{D})_{+}
(
\tilde{e}
is as in
6.29
).

Let
F^{+}(x)\coloneqq F(x)\vee 0
and
F^{-}(x)\coloneqq-(F(x)\wedge 0)
. Then (at least) one of the following hold:

(i)

With
p
as in
3
, there is some
\beta\in(-1/2,-N(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2p}))
(or we can take
\beta=0
if
p=2
) such that the function

C(\overline{D})_{+}\ni x\mapsto\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}

vanishes over (see
2.9
)

C(\overline{D})_{+}\ni x\mapsto\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(ii)

|F_{u}(x)|\lesssim|x_{u}|
and
|F(x)|\lesssim 1+|x|
.

If (i) is assumed in
4
, then we show that there is a nonlinear negative drift and the process enjoys several bounds in expectation. However, such bounds are not available if (ii) is assumed, which occurs when all terms are linear or lower order.

Remark 6.43
.

For example, consider the SPDE on the torus

(6.70)

du=[u_{xx}-u]dt+10udB_{t}\,,

where
B_{t}
is a standard (one-dimensional) Brownian motion. One solution to (
6.70
) is
u_{t}\equiv a_{t}
, where
a_{t}
is a real-valued process, constant in the spacial variable
x
, satisfying

da=-adt+10adB_{t}\,,

which has the explicit solution

a_{t}=a_{0}\exp(-51t+10B_{t})\,.

Then

\mathbb{E}[a_{t}^{2}]=a_{0}^{2}e^{98t}\,,

so that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\|a_{t}\|_{L^{1}}^{2}dt\Big]\to\infty\quad\text{as}\quad T\to\infty\,.

In the context of
3
, this means that we cannot set
W=W^{\prime}=\|u\|_{L^{1}}
, because
\Gamma W=100\|u\|_{L^{1}}^{2}
cannot satisfy
5
(iii)
for any
g\in\mathcal{Y}
(all
g\in\mathcal{Y}
satisfy
\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}g(u_{t})\|dt\big]\lesssim 1
). Thus, if we wanted to use expectation of norms as a tool for obtaining bounds, we would need to work with
W=\|u\|_{L^{1}}^{p}
for some
p<1
. Indeed, we could use
6.13
to show that there is some
p\in(1/2,1)
such that

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\|u_{t}\|_{W^{\eta,r}}^{2p}dt\Big]<\infty\,,

where
W^{\eta,r}\subset L^{\infty}
compactly (this type of argument will be given in
Section

6.4
). However, this does not immediately imply
\|x\|_{W^{\eta,r}}^{p}\in\mathcal{X}
, and we cannot apply the same argument with
2p
replaced by
p
because
6.13
(iii) requires
p>1
.

This sort of example is why we need two separate cases in
4
. Note that if we are only interested in existence of invariant measures (point (iii) in
3.1
), then by
3.2
the issue above is irrelevant and we would not need these separate cases.

The following lemma is useful for verifying
3
and
5
(i)
-
(iii)
at the same time:

Lemma 6.44
.

Suppose
W\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
(see
6.39
) and
W^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty]
are nonnegative functions satisfying:

•

\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
for some constant
K>0
.

•

\Gamma W/W
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
(see
2.9
).

•

W<<W^{\prime}
(see
2.7
).

Then for all
k\geq 1
,
W^{k}\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
and there is a constant
K^{\prime}>0
such that

(6.71)

\mathcal{L}W^{k}\leq K^{\prime}-\frac{k}{4}W^{k-1}W^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma W^{k}\leq K^{\prime}+k^{2}W^{2k-1}W^{\prime}\,.

Proof.

Recalling
6.38
, we start by applying Itô’s formula to obtain:

(6.72)

\mathcal{L}W^{k}=kW^{k-1}\mathcal{L}W+\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)W^{k-2}\Gamma W

(6.73)

\Gamma W^{k}=k^{2}W^{2k-2}\Gamma W\,.

In (
6.72
) we use our assumptions
\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
and
\Gamma W/W
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
to conclude that there is some constant
K^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}W^{k}

\displaystyle=W^{k-1}(k\mathcal{L}W+\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)\Gamma W/W)

\displaystyle\leq W^{k-1}(kK-kW^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)\Gamma W/W)

\displaystyle\leq W^{k-1}(K^{\prime}-\frac{k}{2}W^{\prime})

Next we note that if
kW^{\prime}/2>2K^{\prime}
, then
K^{\prime}-kW^{\prime}/2<-kW^{\prime}/4
. Also, by our assumption
W<<W^{\prime}
there is some
K^{\prime\prime}
such that
kW^{\prime}/2\leq 2K^{\prime}
implies
W\leq K^{\prime\prime}
. Thus,

\mathcal{L}W^{k}\leq K^{\prime}(K^{\prime\prime})^{k-1}-\frac{k}{4}W^{k-1}W^{\prime}\,.

Similarly, from (
6.73
) we have

\Gamma W^{k}=W^{2k-1}\Big(k^{2}\frac{\Gamma W}{W}\Big)\leq W^{2k-1}(K^{\prime\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{2}k^{2}W^{\prime})\leq K^{\prime\prime\prime}(K^{\prime\prime\prime\prime})^{2k-1}+k^{2}W^{2k-1}W^{\prime}\,,

where
K^{\prime\prime\prime}
is such that
\Gamma W/W\leq K^{\prime\prime\prime}/k^{2}+W^{\prime}/2
and
K^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
is such that
k^{2}W^{\prime}/2\leq K^{\prime\prime\prime}
yields
W\leq K^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
.

This finishes the proof of (
6.71
). Finally, the statement
W^{k}\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
follows from
6.38
applied with
f=W^{k}
,
\hat{f}=W^{2k}
.
∎

Lemma 6.45
.

3
and
5
(i)
-
(iii)
hold for
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
as in
6.37
.
Furthermore, for
x=(rv,z)
we have

(i)

If
4
(i)
holds, then for all
p>1
,
W,W^{\prime},U,U^{\prime}
can be chosen such that
1+\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim W^{\prime}
and
1+\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim U^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
4
(ii)
holds, then there is some
p>1
such that
1+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{p/2}\lesssim W^{\prime}
and
1+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{p}\lesssim U^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Cauchy-Schwartz inequality,
\beta\leq 0
, and
2
(in particular the last bullet point) we have

(6.74)

\langle x,F^{+}(x)\rangle_{\beta}\leq\|x\|_{\beta,2}\|F^{+}(x)\|_{\beta,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}\|F^{+}(x)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+1\,.

By
6.24
and
3
(recall that for the following inequality the adjoint of
G(x)
is taken in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2})
)

(6.75)

\|G(x)^{*}x\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\leq\|G(x)\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\,.

Combining
6.42
, Young’s inequality, (
6.74
), (
6.75
),
\|G(x)\|_{\beta,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
(see
6.24
), and (
6.69
) imply the existence of
K,c,c_{0}>0
(which may change from line to line) such that for any
p\leq 2

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(W_{1}^{\frac{p}{2}}+CW_{2}^{p})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p}{2^{\frac{p}{2}}}(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\Big(-\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}-\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}+c_{0}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+pCK-pCc\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}+\frac{p}{2}(p-1)C(1+\|x\|_{L^{1}})^{p-2}\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\Gamma(W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{p^{2}}{2^{p}}(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p-1}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+p^{2}C^{2}(1+\|x\|_{L^{1}})^{2p-2}\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\,.

By
3
and
\beta>-1/2
it follows easily that

(6.76)

\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}=\|H^{*}\sigma(x)\|_{\mathcal{U}}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}

(recall
H\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
, and therefore
H^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2},\mathcal{U})
). Then we conclude by (
6.19
) that

(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\lesssim(1+\|x\|_{L^{1}})^{2}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}\,.

For
p\leq 2
, we can raise the above inequality to the power of
1-p/2
on both sides and combine this with
\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{p}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{p}
(see (
6.76
)) to conclude that

(6.77)

(1+\|x\|_{L^{1}})^{p-2}\|G(x)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\lesssim(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p/2-1}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2-p}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{p}\,.

Thus, after using Young’s inequality, for every
C>0
there is some
p^{*}(C)\in(1,2]
such that for all
p\leq p^{*}(C)

(6.78)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(W_{1}^{\frac{p}{2}}+CW_{2}^{p})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p}{2^{\frac{p}{2}}}(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},2}^{2}-\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}+c_{0}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+pCK-pCc\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}\,,

and, by replacing
p
with
2p
in (
6.77
), for
p\leq 1

(6.79)

\Gamma(W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p})\lesssim(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p-1}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2-2p}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2p}\,.

Case 1:

Suppose
4
(i)
holds. Since
-1/2<\beta\leq 0
, for all
\epsilon>0
there is
C_{\epsilon}
such that

(6.80)

\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\epsilon\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+C_{\epsilon}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\,,

so we conclude from
4
(i)
that
\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
vanishes over
\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}
.

Then from (
6.78
) we obtain for
p\leq p^{*}(C)
that

\mathcal{L}(W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p})\leq K-pc(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p/2-1}\Big(\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}\Big)-pc\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}

By
6.42
,

W\coloneqq W_{1}^{p^{*}(C)/4}+CW_{2}^{p^{*}(C)/2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})

and
\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
for

(6.81)

W^{\prime}\coloneqq c(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p^{*}(C)/4-1}\Big(\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}\Big)+c\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p^{*}(C)/2}+1\,,

which clearly satisfies
W<<W^{\prime}
(see
2.7
. In fact
W\lesssim W^{\prime}
because
\|x\|_{\beta,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}
and, for
p\geq 0
,
(1+a)^{p}\lesssim(1+a)^{p-1}a+1
as functions of
a\in[0,\infty)
.)

By (
6.79
) with
p=p^{*}(C)/2
,

W\geq(W_{1})^{p^{*}(C)/4}
, interpolation of
L^{2}=\mathcal{B}_{0,2}
between

\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\beta+1/2,2}
and Young’s inequality (as in (
6.80
)), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma W}{W}

\displaystyle\leq K(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p^{*}(C)/4-1}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2-p^{*}(C)}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{p^{*}(C)}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p^{*}(C)/4-1}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+C_{\epsilon}(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p^{*}(C)/4-1}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\,.

Thus, by
4
(i)
and we conclude that
\Gamma W/W
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
. We have verified the assumptions of
6.44
, so by
2.18
and
6.38
we have that

3
and
5
(i)
-
(iii)
hold with
W=W^{k}
and
U=W^{2k}
, for any
k\geq 1
. By (
6.81
) and
\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}
, we indeed have that
1+\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim W^{\prime}
and
1+\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim U^{\prime}
.

Case 2:

Suppose
4
(ii)
holds, so, using
\beta\leq 0
,

|\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}|\leq\|x\|_{\beta,2}\|F^{-}(x)\|_{\beta,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}\|F^{-}(x)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,.

Then from (
6.78
) we obtain (for
p\leq 2
)
that (recall we may change
K,c,c_{0}
from line to line)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p}{2^{p/2}}(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p/2-1}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+c_{0}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+pCK-pCc\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}\,.

Using
6.10
and Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, we conclude that there is
a\in[0,1]
such that

\|x\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{a}\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{1-a}

and by
-1/2<\beta\leq 0
we have for some
b\in[0,1]
that

\|x\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{b}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{1-b}\,.

Thus,

\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq(\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{a}\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{1-a})^{c}(\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{b}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{1-b})^{2-c}

where

c\in[0,2]
is chosen such that
c(1-a)(2-p)=(2-c)(1-b)p
(it is possibe for example by the intermediate value theorem). Then by Young’s inequality we obtain that for all
\epsilon>0
there is
C_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\epsilon\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+C_{\epsilon}\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2-p}\,.

By choosing
\epsilon
small enough and then
C
large enough, we obtain that for all
p\leq p^{*}(C)

\mathcal{L}(W_{1}^{p/2}+CW_{2}^{p})\leq K-pc(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p/2-1}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}-pc\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p}\,.

Thus, by
6.38
and
6.42
the functions
W\coloneqq W_{1}^{p^{*}(C)/4}+CW_{2}^{p^{*}(C)/2}
and
W^{\prime}\coloneqq c(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p^{*}(C)/4-1}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+c\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p^{*}(C)/2}+1
satisfy
3
. Similarly, using (
6.79
) and
\|x\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}
we see that
U\coloneqq W_{1}^{p^{*}(C)/2}+CW_{2}^{p^{*}(C)}
and
U^{\prime}\coloneqq c(1+\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2})^{p^{*}(C)/2-1}\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}^{2}+c\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p^{*}(C)}+1
satisfy
5
(i)
-
(iii)
(see
2.18
). The claims
1+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{p/2}\lesssim W^{\prime}
and
1+\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{p}\lesssim U^{\prime}
follow since
\|x\|_{\beta,2}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|x\|_{\beta+1/2,2}
.
∎

6.4.
Semigroup Method Bounds

In
Section

6.3
we used variational methods to construct certain Lyapunov functions (
6.45
), but they do not provide adequate control over
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})
(see
6.36
). Indeed, we have some control over the magnitude of
x_{t}=(rv_{t},z_{t})
in spaces like
L^{1}
or
\mathcal{B}_{\beta+1/2,2}
, but not in
L^{\infty}
(unless the dimension of our space is small enough and the noise term smooth enough that
\mathcal{B}_{\beta+1/2,2}\subset L^{\infty}
). Even more importantly, we have no control over the “angle”
v_{t}
. In this section we will use the mild formulation of SPDE to “upgrade” the bounds given to us by our Lyapunov functions. In particular, we verify
4
(ii)
,
5
(iv)
, and
6
.

Lemma 6.46
.

Define
\eta=1/2+\beta>0
, where
\beta
is as in
6.24
and recall that
N
is the dimension of domain
D
. For all
T>0
,
p>1
,
q\in(2,\infty]
we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|x_{t}\|_{L^{q}}^{p}dt\Big]\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{L^{r}}^{p}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}1+\|x_{t}\|_{L^{r}}^{p}dt\Big]\,,

where
r
satisfies

\frac{1}{r}=\Big(\frac{\eta\wedge p^{-1}}{N}+\frac{1}{q}\Big)\wedge\frac{1}{2}\,.

Proof.

In
6.14
we take
u_{t}=x_{t}
,
y_{t}=F(x_{t})
,
Z_{t}=G(x_{t})
(recall that
x_{t}
solves (
6.1
) by
6.37
). By the last bullet point of
2
and
\eta\leq 1
we obtain

\|y^{+}_{t}\|_{\eta-1,r}=\|F^{+}(x_{t})\|_{\eta-1,r}\lesssim\|F^{+}(x_{t})\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim 1+\|x_{t}\|_{L^{r}}\,.

By
6.24
and
3
,

\|Z\|_{\eta-1/2,r}=\|G(x_{t})\|_{\eta-1/2,r}=\|G(x_{t})\|_{\beta,r}\lesssim\|x_{t}\|_{L^{r}}\,.

Then the claim follows from
6.14
.
∎

Lemma 6.47
.

Under
4
(i)
, for all
p>1
we have
\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}\in\mathcal{X}
(see
2.15
). In fact, we may take
f_{n}(r,v,z)=\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}
for some
n\geq 1
in
2.22
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the set of pairs
(p,q)\in[1,\infty)\times[2,\infty]
such that
f_{n}(r,v,z)=\|x\|_{L^{q}}^{p}
satisfies the assumptions of
2.22
for some
n\geq 1
, which implies
\|x\|_{L^{q}}^{p}\in\mathcal{X}
.
Suppose
(p,r)\in S
with
r<\infty
,
p>1
and that
\infty\geq q>2
is such that
1/r\leq(\eta\wedge p^{-1})/(4N)+1/q
, where
\eta>0
is as in
6.46
. Then by
6.46
,

(6.82)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}\|x_{t}\|_{L^{q}}^{p}dt\Big]\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{L^{r}}^{p}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}1+\|x_{t}\|_{L^{r}}^{p}dt\Big]

and the statement also holds with
p
replaced by
2p
.
We proceed by showing:

(1)

If
(p,2)\in S
and
p>1
then
(p,\infty)\in S
.

(2)

If
(p,\infty)\in S
then
(3p/2,2)\in S
.

(3)

(p^{*},2)\in S
for some
p^{*}>1
.

Then, by induction,
(p,\infty)\in S
for all
p>1
, which finishes the proof.

To prove (1), suppose
(p,r)\in S
with
2\leq r<\infty
,
p>1
, that is,
f_{n}=\|x\|_{L^{r}}^{p}
satisfies the assumptions of
2.22
for some
n\geq 1
. Let
q
be as in (
6.82
), and set
f_{n+1}=\|x\|_{L^{q}}^{p}
. Then, by (
6.82
), we obtain that
f_{n+1}
satisfies the assumptions of
2.22
with
h_{n+1}=0
, and consequently
(p,q)\in S
.
Since it is possible to decrease
1/q
by
(\eta\wedge p^{-1})/(4N)
until
q=\infty
, we obtain that (for
p>1
)
(p,2)\in S
implies that
(p,\infty)\in S
, and (1) follows.

To prove (2), suppose
(p,\infty)\in S
. Then by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities

(6.83)

\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{3p/2}\leq\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{3p/4}\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3p/4}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{3p}+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}\,.

By
6.45
(i) we may assume
\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{6p}\lesssim W^{\prime}
. Let
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be as in
2.22
with
f_{n}=\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}
. Replace
f_{i}
with
f_{i}+(W^{\prime})^{1/2}
for all
i=1,\ldots n
. Then the assumptions of
2.22
still hold and
\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{3p}+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}\lesssim f_{n}
, thus
(3p/2,2)\in S
by (
6.83
).

To prove (3), we fix
r=2
,

p\in(1,2)
sufficiently close to 1 (specified below), and

q>2
such that
1/r\leq(\eta\wedge 2^{-1})/(4N)+1/q
, and in particular (
6.82
) holds.
After decreasing
p>1
and
q>2
if necessary, Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities imply that there is
a>1
such that

(6.84)

\|x\|_{L^{r}}^{p}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{q}}+\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{a}\quad\text{and}\quad\|x\|_{L^{r}}^{2p}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{q}}^{2}+\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{2a}\,.

By
6.45
(i) we may assume that
\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{a}\lesssim(W^{\prime})^{1/2}
. Then by (
6.82
) and (
6.84
), (
2.8
) is satisfied with
f_{1}(r,v,z)=C_{2}\|x\|_{L^{q}}^{p}
and
h_{1}(r,v,z)=C_{1}\|x\|_{L^{q}}
. The assertion (3) holds true by (
2.22
) because

\|x\|_{L^{q}}
vanishes over
\|x\|_{L^{q}}^{p}
and

\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T}\|x_{t}\|_{L^{q}}^{2p}dt]<\infty
, which follows from
the proof of
6.31
(specifically
Step 6
), where we showed
x_{t}\in L^{p}(\Omega,C([0,T];B))
for all
t,p>0
.
∎

The proof of the following lemma is similar to the argument given in
Step 4
of
6.31
.

Lemma 6.48
.

Let
\eta
be as in
6.46
and

v
be as in
6.36
.
For all large enough
q<\infty
,
\|v\|_{\eta/2,q}^{2}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\|v\|_{\eta/2,q}^{4}\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof.

By (
6.37
),

v_{t}=S_{u}(t)v_{0}+(S_{u}*y)_{t}+(S_{u}\diamond Z)_{t}\,,

where

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=v[-\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle-\langle\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z),1_{u}\rangle+\|\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}]

\displaystyle\qquad+\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z)-\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u}

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)-\langle\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u},\cdot\rangle v

and we omitted the dependence on
\theta
.
By
6.13

(6.85)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|v_{t}\|_{\eta/2,q}^{2}dt\Big]\lesssim\|v_{0}\|^{2}_{\eta-1/2,q}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|y_{t}\|^{2}_{\eta-1,q}dt\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|Z_{t}\|_{\eta-1/2,q}^{2}dt\Big]\,.

Then
2
and the mean value theorem show that
|\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z)|\lesssim|v|(1+|x|^{k})
for some
k
(related to the polynomial growth of
f
and its derivatives).
Note that if
4
(ii)
holds, then we take
k=0
.

Then
(
6.18
), (
6.35
), and Young’s inequality imply, similarly to (
6.54
), that
for every
\epsilon>0
there is
c_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|y\|_{\eta-1,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k})\|v\|_{L^{q}}+(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{q}})

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k})^{c_{\epsilon}}+(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\vee\|v\|_{L^{q}})^{1+\epsilon}

\displaystyle\|Z\|_{\eta-1/2,q}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\vee\|v\|_{L^{q}})^{2}\,.

From
6.47
(if
4
(i)
is assumed) or
k=0
(if
4
(ii)
holds) it follows that
it is possible to set
f_{n}(r,v,z)=(1+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k})^{c_{\epsilon}}
in
2.22

for some
n\geq 1
.
As explained after (
6.55
), for any large
q
we have that
h_{n+1}(r,v,z)\coloneqq(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2+2\epsilon}\vee\|v\|_{L^{q}}^{1+1\epsilon})^{2}
vanishes over
f_{n+1}(r,v,z)\coloneqq\|v\|_{\eta/2,q}^{2}
for some small enough
\epsilon>0
. Then from
(
6.85
) follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{n+1}(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})dt\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim f_{n}(r_{0},v_{0},z_{0})+h_{n+1}(r_{0},v_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}f_{n}(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})+h_{n+1}(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})dt\Big]\,,

and the same inequality holds when every function is squared. Since the right hand side is locally finite (that is, it is finite when
T
is replaced by
T\wedge\tau_{m}
for an increasing sequence of stopping times
\tau_{m}\uparrow\infty
),
h_{n+1}
vanishes over
f_{n+1}
, and
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T}f_{n}(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})dt]<\infty
(see the end of the proof of
6.47
), we conclude that the left hand side is finite. Then we can apply
2.22
, which shows that
f_{n+1}\in\mathcal{X}
and
f_{n+1}^{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
, as desired.

For more details on the localization argument, notice that
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{T}h_{n+1}(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})dt]
is the only term on the right hand side which is not a-priori finite. Since
h_{n+1}
vanishes over
f_{n+1}
, there is some
M>0
such that
h_{n+1}\leq f_{n+1}/2+M
. We want to use this bound, subtract
1/2
of the left hand side from both sides, and then multiply by
2
to get a finite right hand side. We do not know a-priori that the left hand side is finite, so we cannot do this subtraction. However, this manipulation is valid if we replace
T
with
T\wedge\tau_{M}
, where
\tau_{M}
is the first time that
h_{n+1}(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})+f_{n}(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})
exceeds
M
. Since
h_{n+1}+f_{n}
is a continuous function defined on all of
\mathcal{M}
, the state space of
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})
lives, and
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t})
is continuous in time, we conclude that
\tau_{M}\to\infty
as
M\to\infty
. Thus, we can proceed with our manipulation when
T
is replaced by
T\wedge\tau_{M}
and then take the limit as
M\to\infty
on both sides via Monotone convergence theorem.
∎

In
6.45
we obtained control over
r
since
r\leq\|x\|_{L^{1}}
.
In
6.48
we obtained control over
v\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\cap\{\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}}=1\}
(indeed,
\mathcal{B}_{\eta/2,q}\subset C(\overline{D})
compactly for large enough
q
by
6.10
and a Sobolev embedding). It remains to obtain control over
z\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m-d})_{+}
. In the case of
4
(i)
we have the following result.

Lemma 6.49
.

Under
4
(i)
, for all
r\in[2,\infty)
we have
\|x\|_{\eta/2,r}^{2}\in\mathcal{X}
, where
\eta
is as in
6.46
.

Proof.

Using
6.13
we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|x_{t}\|_{\eta/2,r}^{2}dt\Big]\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{L^{r}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|F(x_{t})\|_{L^{r}}^{2}+\|G(x_{t})\|_{\beta,r}^{2}dt\Big]\,.

Using
6.24
and the polynomial boundedness of
F
(
2
) we obtain

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|x_{t}\|_{\eta/2,r}^{2}dt\Big]\lesssim\|x_{0}\|_{L^{r}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}1+\|x_{t}\|_{L^{r}}^{2k+2}dt\Big]

and a similar estimate holds after squaring all norms.

The claim follows from
2.22
with
f_{n+1}(x)=\|x_{t}\|_{\eta/2,r}^{2}
and
f_{n}(x)=1+\|x_{t}\|_{L^{r}}^{2k+2}

(the assumptions of
2.22
are justified by

6.47
).
∎

The case of
4
(ii)
requires a similar argument to
6.48
.

Lemma 6.50
.

Under
4
(ii)
, for all large enough
q<\infty

\Big(\frac{\|x\|_{\eta/2,q}}{\|x\|_{L^{1}}+1}\Big)^{2}\in\mathcal{X}\,,

where
\eta
is as in
6.46
.

Proof.

Let
w=x/(\|x\|_{L^{1}}+1)
and
l=\|x\|_{L^{1}}
. We define

(6.86)

\hat{F}(l,w)\coloneqq(1+l)^{-1}F((1+l)w)\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{G}(l,w)=(1+l)^{-1}G((1+l)w)\,.

Analogously as in
6.31
(v)
(set
R=1
and replace
r_{t}
by
r_{t}+1
), we obtain that
w
is a mild solution (see
6.17
) of

\displaystyle dw

\displaystyle=Awdt+w[-\langle w,\tilde{e}\rangle-\langle\hat{F}(l,w),1\rangle+\|\hat{G}(l,w)^{*}1\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}]dt

\displaystyle\qquad+\hat{F}(l,w)dt-\hat{G}(l,w)\hat{G}(l,w)^{*}1dt

\displaystyle\qquad+\hat{G}(l,w)dW_{t}-\langle\hat{G}(l,w)^{*}1,dW_{t}\rangle w

Then the same argument as in the proof of
6.48
(using
|\hat{F}(l,w)|\lesssim 1+|w|
instead of
|\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z)|\lesssim|v|
in the case of
4
(ii)
) shows that
\|w\|_{\eta/2,q}^{2}\in\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Lemma 6.51
.

Suppose
1
–
4
hold.
Then
4
(ii)
,
5
(iv)
, and
6
hold for
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
(see
6.37
), where
V
is as in (
6.65
). Additionally,
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
and
\mathcal{L}V
(see
6.40
) extends continuously to some
H:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
H
also denotes the operator which is part of the noise (see
3
), but the distinction should be clear from the context.

Proof.

For
4
(ii)
, first note that
\mathcal{L}V
as calculated in
6.40
extends to a continuous function on
\mathcal{M}
, because
h(x)=g(-\ln x)
with
g
as in (
6.64
), and therefore
h^{\prime}(r)r
and
h^{\prime\prime}(r)r^{2}
are continuous when set respectively equal to
-1
and
1
when
r=0
. By
|h^{\prime\prime}(r)|\lesssim r^{-2}
,
H\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2})
, and (
6.34
) we have

(6.87)

\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}(r)r^{2}\|\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\lesssim\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

(see for example the proof of (
6.35
)). Similarly by
|h^{\prime}(r)|\lesssim r^{-1}
we have

h^{\prime}(r)r\Big[\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle+\langle\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z),1_{u}\rangle\Big]\lesssim 1+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k},\qquad\textrm{where}\quad x=(rv,z)\,.

If
4
(ii)
holds (see the proof of
6.48
), then
k=0
, and therefore
\mathcal{L}V
vanishes over
\|v\|_{\eta/2,2}^{2}
by
6.10
, and Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality (since
\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1
we have
\|v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|v\|_{\eta/2,2}^{1-\epsilon}
for some
\epsilon>0
). If
4
(i)
holds,
\mathcal{L}V
vanishes over
\|v\|_{\eta/2,2}^{2}+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k+1}
. In either case,
4
(ii)
with
\mu=\delta_{0}
and
f(r,v,z)=\|v\|^{2}_{\eta/2,2}
or
f(r,v,z)=\|v\|^{2}_{\eta/2,2}+\|v\|_{\eta/2,2}^{2}+\|x\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k+1}
is satisfied by
6.48
and
6.47
.

Next we discuss
5
(iv)
. For
\Gamma V
calculated in

6.40
, we have as in (
6.87
) that

\Gamma V(r,v,z)\lesssim\|\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u}\|_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\lesssim\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,.

Since
\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
again vanishes over
g(r,v,z):=\|v\|_{\eta/2,q}^{4}
for all
q\geq 2
,
5
(iv)
follows from
6.48
. Similarly as in
6.38
, this also shows that
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
.

To establish
6
, we verify the sufficient condition
from
2.21
, which requires the existence of

f\in\mathcal{X}
with compact sublevel sets.
Define
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
depending on whether
4
(i)
or
4
(ii)
holds:

\displaystyle f(r,v,z)=\begin{cases}\|v\|_{\eta/2,q}^{2}+\|x\|_{{}_{\eta/2,q}}^{2}&\text{in case }(i)\,,\\
\|v\|_{\eta/2,q}^{2}+\|x\|_{L^{1}}^{p/2}+\Big(\frac{\|x\|_{{}_{\eta/2,q}}}{\|x\|_{L^{1}}+1}\Big)^{2}&\text{in case (ii)}\,,\end{cases}

where
x=(rv,z)
,
q
is sufficiently large as specified below, and
p>1
is as in
6.45
(ii). To show that
f\in\mathcal{X}
, first note that by
2.20
we have
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
. Then
f\in\mathcal{X}
follows from
6.45
,
6.48
,
6.49
, and
6.50
.

To obtain that
f
has compact sublevel sets, first note that by
6.10
and a Sobolev embedding we may choose
q
large enough that
\mathcal{B}_{\eta/2,q}\hookrightarrow C(\overline{D})
compactly. Thus,
\|v\|_{\eta/2,q}^{2}
has compact sublevel sets as a function of
\{v\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}
. Also,
r\leq\|x\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\|x\|_{\eta/2,q}
, so
\|x\|_{\eta/2,q}\leq M
implies that
(r,z)
lies in a compact subset of
[0,\infty)\times C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m-d})_{+}
. Thus, in the first case,
f
has compact sublevel sets. For the second case, note that if
\|x\|_{L^{1}}\leq M
and
\|x\|_{\eta/2,q}/(\|x\|_{L^{1}}+1)\leq M
then
\|x\|_{\eta/2,q}\leq M(M+1)
. Thus, in the second case,
f
has compact sublevel sets.

∎

6.5.
Main Results

To summarize, in
Section

6.2
we defined a Feller Markov process
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
on the state space

\mathcal{M}=[0,\infty)\times\{v\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m-d})_{+}\times\Theta

which has
\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}\cap\{r=0\}
and its complement
\mathcal{M}_{+}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
as invariant sets. The connection between this Markov process and our original equation (
6.1
) is that
x_{t}=(u_{t},z_{t})=(r_{t}v_{t},z_{t})
, so that
r
is the distance from
u
to
0
and
v
is the angle. In
Section

6.3
and
Section

6.4
we used variational and semigroup methods to obtain control on

(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)

in various norms over this projective process, and we are ready to apply
3.1
and
3.5
to investigate the stability of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

For those who are not familiar with the preceding sections, one may understand the main definitions and statements of the theorems in this section having only read the introduction of
Section

6
and
1
-
3
. For example, we provide two definitions for
\Lambda
. One is used in applications of our theory to examples in
Section

7
. A more concise definition of
\Lambda
, used in the proofs in this subsection, is also given but requires deeper understanding of preceding sections.

Definition 6.52
.

Define the system

(6.88)

\displaystyle du(\cdot)

\displaystyle=[A_{u}u(\cdot)+\hat{f}(\cdot,z(\cdot),\theta)u(\cdot)]dt+\hat{\sigma}(\cdot,z(\cdot),\theta)u(\cdot)H_{u}dW_{t}

\displaystyle dz(\cdot)

\displaystyle=[A_{z}z(\cdot)+f_{z}(\cdot,u(\cdot),z(\cdot),\theta))]dt+\sigma_{z}(\cdot,u(\cdot),z(\cdot),\theta)H_{z}dW_{t}\,,

obtained from (
6.1
) by linearizing in
u
in the first
d
components. By this we mean that
\hat{f},\hat{\sigma}:D\times\mathbb{R}^{m-d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
are calculated from
f_{u},\sigma_{u}
by ignoring any term which is nonlinear in
u
. Then we define

(6.89)

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\nu}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Big[-\int_{D}\sum_{i=1}^{d}A_{i}v_{i}(\cdot)+\hat{f}_{i}(\cdot,z(\cdot),\theta)v_{i}(\cdot)d\mu(\cdot)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}^{2}\Big(\int_{D}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\sigma}_{i}(\cdot,z(\cdot),\theta)v_{i}(\cdot)(e_{n})_{i}(\cdot)d\mu(\cdot)\Big)^{2}\Big]\,,

where
v=u/\|u\|_{L^{1}}
,
(v,z,\theta)\sim\nu
, and
\nu
ranges over all invariant measures (stationary distributions) on
\{(v,z)\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times\Theta
for
(u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}},z_{t},\theta)
, where
(u_{t},z_{t},\theta)
solves (
6.88
).

Remark 6.53
.

More precisely, in
6.52
, we have
\hat{f}(\cdot,z,\theta)\coloneqq\tilde{f}_{u}(\cdot,0,z,\theta)
, where
0
in the argument of
\tilde{f}
corresponds to the scaling variable
R

(see
6.26
). Also,
v_{i}
is not guaranteed to be in the domain of
A_{i}
(for example, if we are in one dimension and
dW_{t}/dt
is space-time white noise, then, at best,
v_{i}\in\mathcal{D}((-A)^{1/4-\epsilon})
), so technically we understand
A_{i}v_{i}
as
\tilde{e}_{i}v_{i}
(see
6.29
), or equivalently in the sense of distributions.

Alternatively, recall
2.12
and
2.13
and then (omitting the dependence on
\theta\in\Theta
) let

(6.90)

\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu(-\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle-\langle\tilde{F}_{u}(0,v,z),1_{u}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\|\tilde{G}_{u}(0,v,z)^{*}1_{u}\|_{U}^{2})\,,

where
\tilde{F},\tilde{G}
are defined in
6.26
,
\tilde{e}
is as in
6.29
, and the adjoint of
G_{u}(r,v,z)
is taken in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{U},L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. We claim that (
6.90
) is equivalent to
6.52
. Indeed, (
6.88
) is obtained by setting
r_{0}=0
in
6.36
, so we have
u/\|u\|_{L^{1}}=v
, where
u
solves (
6.88
). Thus,
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
is essentially the same as the set of invariant measures for
(u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}},z_{t})
, except that
\nu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
is technically a measure on
\{0\}\times\{(v,z)\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times\Theta
instead of
\{(v,z)\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times\Theta
. Thus, taking
\sup_{\nu}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[]
in
6.52
is the same as taking
\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu()
in (
6.90
). Finally, notice that the expression inside the
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[]
in (
6.89
) is just the expression inside
\mu()
in (
6.90
) expanded out according to
6.29
,
2
, and
3
(see
6.53
).

Remark 6.54
.

Under our assumptions, it can be shown that
-\Lambda
is the smallest possible value of
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\ln\|\tilde{u}_{t}\|_{L^{1}}
over all initial conditions with
R=0,u_{0}\neq 0
, where
x_{t}=(\tilde{u}_{t},z_{t})
solves (
6.36
). Since (
6.36
) with
R=0
is the linearized version of (
6.1
), the following result can be interpreted as saying that
\{u=0\}
is a repeller for (
6.1
) if it is a repeller for the linearized system.

Theorem 6.55
.

Suppose
1
–
4
hold. Let
(x_{t},\theta)=(u_{t},z_{t},\theta)
be the solution to (
6.1
) with initial condition
x_{0}=(u_{0},z_{0})
. If
\Lambda<0
then:

(i)

For all
u_{0}\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
, almost surely, all limit points
\mu
of the random measures
\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{u_{s}}ds
on
C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}
as
t\to\infty
satisfy
\mu(\{0\})=0
.

(ii)

u_{t}
is stochastically persistent, meaning for all
\epsilon>0
there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\subset C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
such that for all
u_{0}\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\}

\mathbb{P}(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}(\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon})\geq 1-\epsilon)=1\,.

In particular, for all
\epsilon>0
, there are
a,A>0
such that

\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}m(\{t\leq T\mid a\leq\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}}\leq A\})\geq 1-\epsilon\quad a.s.,

where
m
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
[0,T]
.

(iii)

There is a measure
\mu_{+}
on
C(\overline{D})_{+}\times\Theta
such that
\mu_{+}(\{x_{u}\neq 0\})=1
and
\mu_{+}
is invariant for
(x_{t},\theta)
in the sense of
2.13
.

Remark 6.56
.

6.55
is only applied below for time independent
\theta
, that is,
\Theta=\{\theta\}
, however, it is stated more generally. As noted in
6.1
, there might be contexts in which
\theta
is time-dependent and our results (perhaps with extra assumptions for non-compact
\Theta
) still apply. Of course after modifications of Itô formula when
\theta_{t}
is a jump or some other process, there are possibly extra terms in (
6.90
)), but the core of the theorem is the same. We do not pursue the most general setting for brevity and since we did not encounter regime-switching systems in our examples. We encourage readers to generalize oour results if a need arises.

Remark 6.57
.

In
6.55
(ii), we can improve
a\leq\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}}\leq A
to
a\leq u_{t}|_{K}\leq A
for some fixed compact
K\subset D
if
u_{0}\neq 0
implies
u_{t}>0
for all
t>0
(strong maximum principle). This is a consequence of
4.15
with
\mathcal{U}=\{u>0\}
. Proving such a strong maximum principle in our setting is an open problem. It has been proved in special cases, for example: in
[
Wan16
, Theorem 1.2]
the noise is Hilbert-Schmidt ((
6.17
) holds with
p=2
) and the nonlinearity
F
has linear growth; in
[
SKJ25
, Theorem 2.1]
the noise is of a simple form (finite-dimensional); and in
[
KKMS23
, Theorem 2.3]
when
N=1
.
Since we did not find a short way to incorporate
these references to our results, we leave it for future endeavors.

Proof.

In order to apply
3.1
, we need to verify
1
-
6
for
X_{t}\coloneqq(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
from
6.36
.
1
and
2
were already verified in
6.37
.

By
6.45
,
3
and
5
(i)
-
(iii)
hold. Recall the function
V:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to[0,\infty)
defined in (
6.65
) which agrees with
-\ln{r}
when
r\in(0,e^{-1})
.

By
6.51
,
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
and
\mathcal{L}V
extends continuously to some
H:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
which for
r>0
satisfies

H(r,v,z)=h^{\prime}(r)r\Big[\langle v,\tilde{e}_{u}\rangle+\langle\tilde{F}_{u}(r,v,z),1_{u}\rangle\Big]+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}(r)r^{2}\|\tilde{G}_{u}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{u}\|_{U}^{2}\,.

As noted in the proof of
6.51
,
h^{\prime}(r)r\to-1
and
h^{\prime\prime}(r)r^{2}\to 1
as
r\downarrow 0
. Thus, the quantity defined in (
6.90
) is equal to
\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H
. We are assuming
\Lambda<0
, so
4
(i)
is satisfied. The remainder of
4
-
6
follow from
6.51
.

With
1
–
6
verified, our conclusions are almost direct consequences of
3.1
as shown next.

•

Denote
\Upsilon(r,v,z,\theta)=(rv,z,\theta)
, which is a homeomorphism from
\mathcal{M}_{+}
to
\{x\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid x_{u}\neq 0\}\times\Theta
. Since the solution
x_{t}
to (
6.1
) is equal to
(r_{t}v_{t},z_{t})
(
6.37
), pushing forward by
\Upsilon
gives a bijection between
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
and the set of invariant measures
\mu
for
x_{t}
with
\mu(\{x_{u}\neq 0\})=1
. Thus, (iii) follows from
3.1
(iii).

•

To prove (ii) notice that
\Upsilon
gives a correspondence between compact subsets of
\mathcal{M}_{+}
and compact subsets of
\{x\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid x_{u}\neq 0\}\times\Theta
, which relates

\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
in
3.1
(ii) to
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
in (ii)
(apply
\Upsilon
and project onto the
u
coordinate to obtain a compact subset of
C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
). Finally, (ii) follows since
\|u\|_{L^{1}}
is a continuous strictly positive function on
C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
, and thus attains a strictly positive minimum on
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
.

•

Let
\nu_{t}
be the empirical occupation measures for
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
(see
2.14
) and
\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{u_{s}}ds
. Then
\mu_{t}
is
\nu_{t}
pushed forward by
\Upsilon_{u}
(
\Upsilon
followed by projection onto the
u
coordinates). Suppose
t_{n}\to\infty
and
\mu_{t_{n}}\to\mu
weakly as measures on
C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}
. Although this does not imply that
\nu_{t_{n}}
converges, by
3.3
there is a convergent subsequence and by
3.1
(i) the limit point
\nu
lies in
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{+}=\Upsilon_{u}^{-1}(C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\})
, we conclude that
\mu(C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\})=\nu(\Upsilon_{u}^{-1}(C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\setminus\{0\}))=\nu(\mathcal{M}_{+})=1
, which proves (i).

∎

In the case of Lotka-Volterra or other models for species competition, we may want to know when
all
of the species coexist, as opposed to just saying that at least one will survive. In this case, we use the following result.

Theorem 6.58
.

Suppose
1
-
4
hold when
x_{t}
is written as
(u_{t},z_{t})
where
u_{t}=(x_{t})_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,d
. Consider the processes

(r_{i}(t),v_{i}(t),x_{-i}(t),\theta)=(\|(x_{t})_{i}\|_{L^{1}},(x_{t})_{i}/\|(x_{t})_{i}\|_{L^{1}},x_{-i}(t),\theta)
(where
x_{-i}
is the vector with
i
th coordinate omitted) on

\mathcal{M}^{(i)}=[0,\infty)\times\{x\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|x_{1}\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times\Theta

with invariant set
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(i)}=\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(i)}\cap\{r_{i}=0\}
.
Define
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{M}^{(i)}\to C(\overline{D})_{+}\times\Theta
with
\phi_{i}(r_{i},v_{i},x_{-i},\theta)=(x,\theta)
, where
x
is obtained from
x_{-i}
by inserting
r_{i}v_{i}
to
i
th coordinate. Furthermore, we set

\mathcal{M}^{*}\coloneqq C(\overline{D})_{+}\times\Theta\,,\quad\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*}\coloneqq\{x\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid x_{i}=0\text{ for some }i=1,\dots,d\}\times\Theta\,.

For each choice of
i=1,\dots,d
and
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*})
with
\mu(\{x_{i}=0\})=1
define

(6.91)

r_{i}(\mu)\coloneqq\inf_{\nu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(i)}),\phi_{i}^{*}\nu=\mu}\mu(\langle v,\tilde{e}_{i}\rangle+\langle\tilde{F}_{i}(r,v,z),1_{i}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|\tilde{G}_{i}(r,v,z)^{*}1_{i}\|_{U}^{2})

(here
\phi_{i}^{*}\nu
is the pushforward of the measure
\nu
, i.e.
\phi_{i}^{*}\nu(A)=\nu(\phi_{i}^{-1}(A))
). For arbitrary
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*})
, we write
\mu=p\mu_{1}+(1-p)\mu_{2}
for some
p\in[0,1]
and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*})
satisfying
\mu_{1}(\{x_{i}=0\})=1
and
\mu_{2}(\{x_{i}=0\})=0
, and then define
r_{i}(\mu)\coloneqq pr_{i}(\mu_{1})
(this is possible because
\{x_{i}=0\}
is an invariant set).

If
\max_{i=1,\dots,m}r_{i}(\mu)>0
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*})
then
x_{t}
is stochastically persistent in the sense of
3.1
(with
\mathcal{M}_{+}=\mathcal{M}^{*}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*}
and
X_{t}=(x_{t},\theta)
).

Proof.

The proof follows the same steps as the proof of
6.55
, with a few key differences. Instead of using

[0,\infty)\times\{v\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m-d})_{+}\times\Theta

as our state space, we need to use

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq[0,\infty)^{d}\times\{v\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R})_{+}^{d}\mid\|v_{i}\|_{L^{1}}=1\}\times C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m-d})_{+}\times\Theta

and
\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}\cap\{r=0\}
. (We use the notation
(r,v,z,\theta)\in\mathcal{M}
where
r\in[0,\infty)^{d}
, etc.)
Our Markov process
(r_{t},v_{t},z_{t},\theta)
is given by
r_{t}=(r_{1}(t),\cdots,r_{d}(t))
and
v_{t}=(v_{1}(t),\dots,v_{d}(t))
. Thus, our
\Upsilon:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathcal{M}_{+}^{*}
is given by
\Upsilon(r,v,z,\theta)=(r\cdot v,z,\theta)
.
Finally, for
V
we use

V(r,v,z,\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\rho_{i}h(r_{i})\,,

where
h
is as in (
6.65
) and choose
\rho_{i}>0
so that
\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*})}(-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\rho_{i}r_{i}(\mu))<0
(this is possible by Hahn-Banach separation theorem, see the proof of
5.1
).

Then the proof follows from
6.37
,
6.45
,
6.51
, and
3.1
in exactly the same way as
6.55
.
∎

The following theorem shows that the stability of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
depends continuously on the parameters of the system (meaning
\theta\in\Theta
).

Theorem 6.59
.

Suppose that the assumptions of
6.55
or
6.58
are satisfied when
\Theta
is replaced by
\{\theta_{0}\}
for some
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
. Then there is some open
\mathcal{U}\subset\Theta
such that
\theta_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and the conclusions of
6.55
or
6.58
respectively hold when
\Theta
is replaced by
\mathcal{U}
. In other words, as long as the parameter
\theta
is close enough to
\theta_{0}
, the process is stochastically persistent.

Proof.

The proof is an immediate consequence of
3.5
.

∎

7.
Examples

In this section we present four examples of stochastic reaction-diffusion type PDEs to which we apply the theorems from
Section

6
. We give criteria which ensure that a species survives (
Section

7.1
), a fixed point is repelling, indicating turbulence (
Section

7.2
), a disease is endemic (
Section

7.3
), and an ecosystem of species in competition can coexist (
Section

7.4
). We work with a variety of noise types from space-time white noise (
Section

7.2
) to standard Brownian motion (
Section

7.3
) and even some interesting intermediate examples (
Section

7.4
). Rather than formulating results in all possible settings such as different noise, boundary conditions, number of equations etc.,
we provide examples that illustrate applications of our results.

In what follows, well-posedness of the problem and nonnegativity of the solution is a consequence of
6.35
, so we do not discuss it further and instead focus on the long-term dynamics. Throughout the following, recall that
C(\overline{D})_{+}
is the set of all nonnegative continuous functions on
\overline{D}
, the closure of
D
,
with range
[0,\infty)^{m}
for a specified integer
m\geq 1
.

7.1.
Logistic Growth Model on a Domain

We consider a population evolving in some domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
which is open, connected, and bounded with smooth boundary. At each point
x\in D
there is a carrying capacity
K(x)
, an intrinsic growth rate
r(x)
, and a harvesting rate
E(x)
.
Specifically,
fix
d>0
,
C^{1}
functions
K,E,r:\overline{D}\to[0,\infty)
with
K>0
,
smooth functions

g_{i}:\overline{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, and a
C^{1}
function
\sigma:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
with
\sigma(0)=0
and with locally Lipschitz, globally bounded first derivatives. Also, assume
w_{t}
is a Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon\in\Theta:=[0,1]
is a constant.

Then the logistic growth model for the population with stochasticity is

(7.1)

du=\Big[d\Delta\Big(\frac{u}{K(x)}\Big)+r(x)u\Big(1-\frac{u}{K(x)}\Big)-E(x)u\Big]dt+\epsilon\sigma(u)K(x)dw_{t}\,,

where we impose
Neumann boundary conditions
\frac{d(u/K)}{dn}\equiv 0
on
\partial D
with
n

being the outer unit normal vector .

Our solution
u_{t}
to (
7.1
) with a specified initial condition
u_{0}\in C(\overline{D})_{+}
is guaranteed to exist as a continuous (with respect to time)
C(\overline{D})_{+}
-valued process and is unique by
6.35
(indeed, we will verify
1
-
3
below).

Using the results in
Section

6.5
, we prove theorems related to the persistence of the population. Heuristically, we show that the species survives in the long term if analogous statements hold for the linearized system:

(7.2)

du=\Big[d\Delta\Big(\frac{u}{K(x)}\Big)+(r(x)-E(x))u\Big]dt+\epsilon\sigma^{\prime}(0)K(x)udw^{(i)}_{t}\,,

and that this reduces to a (deterministic) eigenvalue problem if we are allowed to choose
\epsilon
arbitrarily small.
We remark that our results also apply to other growth models (see
[
KKB13
]
for examples), but we choose logistic for concreteness and ease of presentation.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\lambda
be the largest eigenvalue of the problem

d\Delta\Big(\frac{u}{K(x)}\Big)+(r(x)-E(x))u=\lambda u\qquad\textrm{on }D

with the same (Neumann) boundary conditions imposed on (
7.1
). If
\lambda>0
, then there is some
\overline{\epsilon}>0
such that for all
\epsilon<\overline{\epsilon}
, (
7.1
) is stochastically persistent in the sense that:

•

For every
\delta>0
there are
0<b<B<\infty
such that for any nonnegative initial condition
u_{0}\neq 0
,

\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}m\Big(\Big\{t\leq T\mid b\leq\int_{D}u_{t}\leq B\Big\}\Big)\geq 1-\delta

almost surely, where
m
is the Lebesgue measure.

•

There is an invariant measure
\mu_{+}
for (
7.1
) with
\mu_{+}(\{0\})=0
.

Remark 7.2
.

Note that
\int_{D}u_{t}
is the total population of the species at time
t
.

Remark 7.3
.

7.1
should be compared to
[
KKB13
, Corollary 1]
, where the authors proved a deterministic version of the theorem (
\epsilon=0
) under the assumption that there exists some
c>0
such that
\gamma\coloneqq\inf_{x\in D}(1-c)r(x)-E(x)>0
. Thus,
E<r
, and consequently

\lambda=\sup_{u\neq 0}\frac{\int_{D}-d\|\nabla(uK^{-1})\|^{2}+(r-E)u^{2}K^{-1}dm}{\int_{D}u^{2}K^{-1}dm}\geq\frac{\int_{D}(r-E)Kdm}{\int_{D}Kdm}>0

(we used
u=K
in the first inequality). Hence, the condition for persistence stated in
7.7
is sharper than the existing one, even in the deterministic case.

Remark 7.4
.

In
7.3
,
\lambda
is expressed as a Rayleigh quotient. The proof of
7.1
indicates that, for linear deterministic PDE,
\Lambda
as defined in (
6.90
) is also a Rayleigh quotient, except it uses the
L^{1}
norm instead of the
L^{2}
norm (the quotient is the same either way).

Proof.

First we verify that
1
-
4
hold for (
7.1
).
1
(i) and (iii) follow from
6.4
. To satisfy
1
(ii) we work in
L^{2}(D,\Sigma,\mu)
, where
\Sigma
is the Borel sigma-algebra of
D
and the measure
\mu
has density
K^{-1}
with respect to Lebesgue measure
m
. We define

(7.3)

Au\coloneqq d\Delta\Big(\frac{u}{K(x)}\Big)-cu

for some suitably large
c>0
and use integration by parts (twice):

\displaystyle\langle Au,v\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{D}d\Delta\Big(\frac{u}{K}\Big)v-cuvd\mu=\int_{D}d\Delta\Big(\frac{u}{K}\Big)\frac{v}{K}-cuvK^{-1}dm

\displaystyle=\int_{D}d\Delta\Big(\frac{v}{K}\Big)\frac{u}{K}-cuvK^{-1}dm=\int_{D}d\Delta\Big(\frac{v}{K}\Big)u-cuvd\mu

\displaystyle=\langle u,Av\rangle

and
1
(ii) follows.

Note that we have no
z
component since
m=1
(see
Section

6
), and so
2
is verified for
f_{2}=0
,
f_{1}(\cdot,u)=cu+r(\cdot)u(1-uK(\cdot)^{-1})-E(\cdot)u
. For
3
, we have that
\sigma(\cdot,u,\theta)=\theta\sigma(u)
satisfies all assumptions. Since
K(x)
is an eigenvector for
A
, indeed (
6.17
) is satisfied with
p=2
(
a_{n}=0
for
n>1
).

Next, we set
\beta=0
in
6.24
and verify
4
(recall
c
is a fixed constant involved in the definition of
A
(
7.3
)):

\displaystyle\langle x,\tilde{e}\rangle+\langle F(x),1\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|x\|_{L^{1}}\|\tilde{e}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\int[c+r-E]K^{-1}x-rK^{-2}x^{2}dm

\displaystyle\leq\|x\|_{L^{1}}(\|\tilde{e}\|_{L^{\infty}}+c+\|r\|_{L^{\infty}})-\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\inf rK^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq C-c^{\prime}\|x\|_{L^{1}}\,,

because
\|x\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{2}}
vanishes over
\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
(cf.
2.9
). Similarly we have
\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}=\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
vanishes over

\displaystyle\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\langle x,F^{-}(x)\rangle\geq\int rx^{3}K^{-2}-\int[c+r]x^{2}K^{-1}

\displaystyle\geq\inf rK^{-1}\|x\|_{L^{3}}^{3}-[c+\sup r]\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,,

because
\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|x\|_{L^{3}}^{2}
vanishes over
\|x\|_{L^{3}}^{3}
.

Then we want to apply
6.59
with
\theta_{0}=0
, which requires verifying the assumptions of
6.55
. In particular, we need to check that (
6.90
) is strictly negative when
\epsilon=0
in (
7.1
). First we need to find the set of all invariant measures on
\{v\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}
(we omit
\Theta
because we are fixing
\epsilon=0
) for
v_{t}=u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}}
, where
u_{t}
solves (
7.2
) (observe that (
7.2
) is really (
6.88
), since we are only dealing with one species, and therefore there is no
z
).

By Krein-Rutman theorem and the strong maximum principle, the eigenfunction
e_{1}
corresponding to the largest eigenvalue of the operator
u\mapsto d\Delta(uK^{-1})+(r-E)u
is strictly positive on
D
, and no other eigenfunction is nonnegative. Since the operator is self-adjoint (this was verified above), it is standard to check that the eigenfunctions form an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(D,\Sigma,\mu)
.
If
u_{t}
is the solution to (
7.2
) with
\epsilon=0
and

u_{0}\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
(which implies
\langle u_{0},e_{1}\rangle>0
), then
from uniqueness of
e_{1}
(up to scaling) follows
\lim_{t\to\infty}u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}}=e_{1}/\|e_{1}\|_{L^{2}}
in
L^{2}
for all
u_{0}\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\setminus\{0\}
.
Since
x\mapsto x/\|x\|_{L^{1}}
is continuous on
\{x\in L^{2}\mid\|x\|_{L^{1}}>0\}
, for
x=u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}}

we conclude

\lim_{t\to\infty}v_{t}=\lim_{t\to\infty}u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}}=e_{1}/\|e_{1}\|_{L^{1}}\qquad\textrm{in }L^{2}\,.

Thus, in
6.52
the only invariant measure
\nu
is the dirac delta mass at
e_{1}/\|e_{1}\|_{L^{1}}
, and we obtain
