\Lambda=-\Big\langle d\Delta\Big(\frac{e_{1}}{\|e_{1}\|_{L^{1}}}K^{-1}\Big)+(r-E)\frac{e_{1}}{\|e_{1}\|_{L^{1}}},1\Big\rangle=-\frac{1}{\|e_{1}\|_{L^{1}}}\langle\lambda e_{1},1\rangle=-\lambda\,,

so that the condition
\Lambda<0
corresponds exactly to
\lambda>0
as assumed in
7.1
. Thus, the proof follows from
6.59
(in particular, the last two assertions of
6.55
).

∎

We can also make conclusions about the persistence of the population for an arbitrary value of
\epsilon
, but it requires computing a stochastic version of the eigenvalue
-\lambda
, which we call the average Lyapunov exponent, defined as follows.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary and
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
denote the set of all invariant measures of the process
v_{t}\coloneqq u_{t}/\|u_{t}\|_{L^{1}}
on
\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\{v\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1\}
, where
u_{t}
solves (
7.2
). We define

(7.4)

\displaystyle\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu\Big(

\displaystyle-\int_{D}d\Delta\Big(vK^{-1}\Big)K^{-1}+(r-E)vK^{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{\prime}(0)^{2}\Big[\int_{D}v\Big]^{2}\Big)\,.

Remark 7.6
.

For non-smooth
v
, the Laplacian of
v
is understood in the sense of distributions,
see
6.29
and our alternative definition for
\Lambda
in (
6.90
), which is used in the proofs.

Theorem 7.7
.

Fix some
\epsilon>0
(not necessarily small) and suppose
\Lambda<0
(see
7.5
). Then (
7.1
) is stochastically persistent in the sense of
7.1
(the bullet points hold).

Proof.

The proof follows immediately from
6.55
. Indeed, it was already shown in
7.1
that (
7.1
) satisfies
1
-
4
.

∎

7.2.
Fisher-KPP on a Torus with Space-Time White noise

The following equation is a typical reaction-diffusion equation with applications to the study of turbulence
[
KKMS23
,
ZTPSM00
]
and biology
[
VP25
]
. We work in one spatial dimension so that we may add space-time white noise and the solutions are still functions. For simplicity, we consider the equation on a torus
\mathbb{T}
(periodic boundary conditions):

(7.5)

du=[u_{xx}+u-u^{2}]dt+\epsilon\sigma(u)dW_{t}\,,

where

W_{t}
denotes cylindrical Brownian motion on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
(with Lebesgue measure) so that
dW_{t}/dt
is space-time white noise. We assume
\sigma:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is a differentiable function with bounded locally Lipschitz derivative which satisfies
\sigma(0)=0
and
\epsilon>0
is a constant representing the strength of the noise. The following result was already proven in
[
KKMS23
, Theorem 2.4]
, but we show how it follows from our method.

Theorem 7.8
.

There is some
\overline{\epsilon}>0
such that for all
0<\epsilon<\overline{\epsilon}
, (
7.5
) is stochastically persistent in the sense that:

•

There is an invariant measure
\mu_{+}
for (
7.5
) with
\mu_{+}(\{0\})=0
.

•

For all nonnegative initial conditions
u_{0}\neq 0
, almost surely all limit points
\mu
of the measures

\mu_{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{u_{s}}ds

as
t\to\infty
satisfy
\mu(\{0\})=0
.

Remark 7.9
.

Using
[
KKMS23
, Theorem 2.3]
, we could replace
\mu_{+}(\{0\})=0
with
\mu_{+}(\{u\in C(\overline{D})_{+}\mid u>0\})=1
, and same for
\mu
in the second bullet point (see
6.57
).

Remark 7.10
.

Using
6.55
, we obtain a similar result for arbitrary
\epsilon>0
not necessarily small if we assume
\Lambda<0
, for
\Lambda
as in
6.52
.

This relates to the open problem posed after
[
KKMS23
, Remark 2.5]
because if one could show that
\Lambda
is increasing in
\epsilon
(obviously this is true for fixed
\nu
, but it is not sufficient since the invariant measure
\nu
depends on
\epsilon
), then a corresponding extinction theory to this paper (analogous to how
[
FS24
]
complements
[
Ben23
]
) would verify the conjecture.

Remark 7.11
.

In
[
KKMS23
]
the linearized version of the deterministic system is
d\psi=\psi_{xx}+\psi-F^{\prime}(0)\psi
, so their condition (called (F2))
\limsup_{x\downarrow 0}F^{\prime}(x)<1
is equivalent to our condition
\Lambda<0
in
6.55
. Additionally,
[
KKMS23
, Lemma 2.1(2)]
is enough to guarantee our
4
. Thus,
7.8
holds when
u^{2}
is replaced by any
F(u)
satisfying (F1)-(F3) in
[
KKMS23
]
, we just chose
u^{2}
for concreteness.

Proof.

We use
6.59
with
\Theta=[0,1]
and
\theta_{0}=0
. As in the proof of
7.1
, we have that the value
\Lambda_{0}
is equal to
-\lambda
, where
\lambda
is the largest eigenvalue of the operator
u\mapsto u_{xx}+u
.
\lambda
is easily seen to be
1
(the eigenvector is
u\equiv 1
), so that the assumption
\Lambda_{0}<0
is satisfied.

It remains to verify
1
-
4
. We define
Au=u_{xx}-2u
and then
1
follows from
6.4
and the fact that the Laplacian is self-adjoint.
2
holds true because
f_{2}=0
,
m=1
(there is no
z
), and polynomials are smooth. For
3
, we have by definition of space-time white noise that
\mathcal{U}=l^{2}
and
Hu=\sum u_{n}e_{n}
, where
e_{n}
is the
n
th eigenfunction of the Laplacian on the torus. In other words,
a_{n}=1
for all
n
, so
3
is satisfied with
p=\infty
because
\mathbb{T}
is one-dimensional (
N=1
). Also, (
6.69
) follows from

\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{xx}+u-u^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{xx}+1-u=1-\|u\|_{L^{1}}

(indeed,
(u-1)^{2}=u^{2}-2u+1\geq 0\implies u-u^{2}\leq 1-u
).
To verify
4
(i)
, we work with the Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}=W^{2\beta,2}(\mathbb{T})
(see
6.10
) and we use the following auxilliary lemma.

Lemma 7.12
.

For all
\beta\in(-1/2,-1/4)
we have:

(i)

L^{1}(\mathbb{T})\subset\mathcal{B}_{\beta,2}
and
\|u\|_{\beta}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{1}}
for all
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
.

(ii)

If
f,g\in C(\overline{D})_{+}
then

\|f\|_{L^{1}}\|g\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\langle f,g\rangle_{\beta}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{1}}\|g\|_{L^{1}}\,.

Proof.

The Sobolev embedding
\mathcal{B}_{-\beta,2}=W^{-2\beta,2}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
and duality imply

\|u\|_{\beta}=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{B}_{-\beta,2},\|v\|_{-\beta,2}=1}\langle u,v\rangle\lesssim\sup_{v\in\mathcal{B}_{-\beta,2},\|v\|_{-\beta,2}=1}\|u\|_{L^{1}}\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{1}}\,.

and
(i)
follows.
The upper bound in
(ii)
is a consequence of Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and
(i)
:

(7.6)

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\langle A^{\beta}f,A^{\beta}g\rangle\leq\|f\|_{\beta,2}\|g\|_{\beta,2}\leq\|f\|_{L^{1}}\|g\|_{L^{1}}\,.

To prove the lower bound, we use (
6.2
), the fact that
A
is self-adjoint (and therefore
S
is self-adjoint), the substitution
s+t=r
,
and a substitution
s/r=q
to obtain

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\Gamma(-\beta))^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\beta-1}s^{-\beta-1}\langle S(t)f,S(s)g\rangle dsdt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\Gamma(-\beta))^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(ts)^{-\beta-1}\langle S(t+s)f,g\rangle dsdt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\Gamma(-\beta))^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{r}((r-s)s)^{-\beta-1}\langle S(r)f,g\rangle dsdr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\Gamma(-\beta))^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle S(r)f,g\rangle r^{-2\beta-1}\int_{0}^{1}((1-q)q)^{-\beta-1}dqdr\,.

Using properties of beta function, we have

\int_{0}^{1}((1-q)q)^{-\beta-1}dq=\mathcal{B}(-\beta,-\beta)=\frac{(\Gamma(-\beta))^{2}}{\Gamma(-2\beta)}\,,

and consequently

\langle f,g\rangle_{\beta}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-2\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle S(r)f,g\rangle r^{-2\beta-1}dr\,.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{t}
be the heat semigroup, meaning the semigroup generated by
u\mapsto u_{xx}=Au+2u
, so
\mathcal{P}_{t}=e^{2t}S(t)
. Then continuing the above we have

(7.7)

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-2\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-2\beta-1}\langle S(t)f,g\rangle dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-2\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-2\beta-1}e^{-2t}\langle\mathcal{P}_{t}f,g\rangle dt\,.

By Harnack inequality for
f\in C(\overline{D})_{+}
we have
\|f\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\mathcal{P}_{t}f
uniformly in
t\in[1,2]
(for example, use that
\mathcal{P}_{t}f(x)=\mathbb{E}[f(x+\sqrt{2}B_{t})]
, where
B_{t}
is a Brownian motion and the normal distribution projected to the torus has a strictly positive density which is jointly continuous in
t
and
x
), so that for
t\in[1,2]

\|f\|_{L^{1}}\|g\|_{L^{1}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\|f\|_{L^{1}}g(x)dx\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{P}_{t}f)(x)g(x)dx=\langle\mathcal{P}_{t}f,g\rangle

(we used
g\geq 0
in the first equality). Combined with (
7.7
), this proves
(ii)
.
∎

Next, we proceed with the proof of
4
(i)
. For any
\beta\in(-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4})
(
p=\infty
and
N=1
in
4
(i)
) we have by
7.12
(i)
that
\|u\|_{\beta,2}^{2}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{1}}^{2}
. Also, by
7.12
(ii)
we have

\|F^{-}(u)\|_{L^{1}}\|u\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\langle F^{-}(u),u\rangle_{\beta}\,.

Since
F(u)=cu-u^{2}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
u
vanishes over
u^{2}
, we have also that
u
vanishes over
F^{-}(u)\geq u^{2}-|c|u
, and thus for all
K>0
there is some
C>0
such that
\|F^{-}(u)\|_{L^{1}}\geq K\|u\|_{L^{1}}-C
, so

\|u\|_{L^{1}}\|F^{-}(u)\|_{L^{1}}\geq K\|u\|_{L^{1}}^{2}-C\|u\|_{L^{1}}\geq\frac{K}{2}\|u\|_{L^{1}}^{2}-C^{\prime}\,,

which proves that
\|u\|_{L^{1}}^{2}
(and thus
\|u\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
) vanishes over
\|F^{-}(u)\|_{L^{1}}\|u\|_{L^{1}}
, and thus
\langle F^{-}(u),u\rangle_{\beta}
.

∎

7.3.
SIR Epidemic Model

In this section we investigate a stochastic version of the classic SIR epidemic model analyzed numerically in
[
AYI+23
]
, which is similar to the one treated analytically in
[
SKJ25
]
.
We consider a disease infecting some portion of a population living in some domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
which is open, connected, bounded, and has smooth boundary. The function
S
represents the density of the population which is susceptible and
I
is the density of population which is currently infected. There are positive parameters
\lambda,\eta,\delta,\sigma
representing respectively the recruitment rate, natural death rate, death rate due to disease, recovery rate, as well as diffusion constants
d_{1},d_{2}>0
and
\beta,c_{1},c_{2}>0
,
c_{3}\geq 0
governing the rate at which the disease spreads. In fact, we could allow all of these constants to be functions on
D
and our results would still apply, just with slightly more complicated formulas. We add noise to the death rate
\eta
, so at each time
t
we have a death rate of
\eta+\alpha dw/dt
, where
w^{(1)},w^{(2)}
are independent Brownian motions and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\geq 0
govern the strength of the noise. (We could have made our example more complicated by choosing space-time white noise in one dimension like in
Section

7.2
, or the type of noise considered in
Section

7.4
, a generalization of the Hilbert-Schmidt noise considered in
[
NNY20
]
, but we already have examples with those types of noise, so we kept it simple for this one.) The system is:

(7.8)

\displaystyle dS

\displaystyle=\Big[d_{1}\Delta S+\lambda-\eta S-\frac{\beta SI}{1+c_{1}S+c_{2}I+c_{3}SI}\Big]dt+\alpha_{1}Sdw^{(1)}_{t}

\displaystyle dI

\displaystyle=\Big[d_{2}\Delta I-(\eta+\delta+\sigma)I+\frac{\beta SI}{1+c_{1}S+c_{2}I+c_{3}SI}\Big]dt+\alpha_{2}Idw^{(2)}_{t}

with Neumann boundary conditions (
dS/dn=dI/dn=0
on
\partial D
) and independent standard Brownian motions
w^{(1)},w^{(2)}
. We study conditions under which the disease persists.

In the deterministic setting (
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
), it is known (
[
AKR+21
, Lemma 2.1]
) that the system has an endemic equilibrium (one where
I\neq 0
) if the quantity

(7.9)

\tilde{\lambda}\coloneqq\frac{\beta\lambda-(\eta+\delta+\sigma)(c_{1}\lambda+\eta)}{c_{1}\lambda+\eta}>0\,.

Via an analysis of the linearized system

(7.10)

\displaystyle d\tilde{S}

\displaystyle=\Big[d_{1}\Delta\tilde{S}+\lambda-\eta\tilde{S}\Big]dt+\alpha_{1}\tilde{S}dw^{(1)}_{t}

\displaystyle d\tilde{I}

\displaystyle=\Big[d_{2}\Delta\tilde{I}-(\eta+\delta+\sigma)\tilde{I}+\frac{\beta\tilde{S}\tilde{I}}{1+c_{1}\tilde{S}}\Big]dt+\alpha_{2}\tilde{I}dw^{(2)}_{t}\,,

we can make similar statements about the stochastic system.

Theorem 7.13
.

Suppose either:

•

\tilde{\lambda}>0
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are small enough.

•

\Lambda<0
, where

(7.11)

\displaystyle\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu\Big(-\int_{D}\frac{\beta\tilde{S}}{1+c_{1}\tilde{S}}\tilde{I}\Big)+\eta+\delta+\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}^{2}

and
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
denotes the set of all invariant measures of the process
(\tilde{S}_{t},\tilde{I}_{t}/\|\tilde{I}_{t}\|_{L^{1}})
on

\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\{(S,I)\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{2})\mid\|I\|_{L^{1}}=1,S\geq 0,I\geq 0\}\,,

where
(\tilde{S}_{t},\tilde{I}_{t})
solves (
7.10
).

Then the disease is stochastically persistent in the sense that

•

There is an invariant measure
\mu_{+}
for (
7.8
) with
\mu_{+}(\{I=0\})=0
.

•

For all
\delta>0
there are
0<b<B<\infty
such that for any nonnegative initial condition
(S_{0},I_{0})
with
I_{0}\neq 0
,

\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}m\Big(\Big\{t\leq T\mid b\leq\int_{D}I_{t}\leq B\Big\}\Big)\geq 1-\delta

almost surely, where
m
is the Lebesgue measure.

Remark 7.14
.

It should be noted that in
7.13
we obtain persistence in the almost sure sense, as opposed to the statements about expectation in
[
NNY20
, Theorem 4.1]
,
[
LZ23
, Theorem 3.3]
,
[
SKJ25
, Theorem 3.1]
. Additionally, we believe our criteria for persistence are tighter than in

[
NNY20
,
LZ23
,
SKJ25
]
if
\beta,\eta,
etc. are allowed to depend on the space variable. Indeed, in
[
NNY20
,
LZ23
,
SKJ25
]
it is not obvious if the threshold for persistence is the same as the threshold for extinction (except in the case of constant
\beta,\eta,
etc.). In the forthcoming project we will present a extinction theory (as
[
FS24
]
complements
[
Ben23
]
) showing that if the infimum of the expression appearing in the definition of
\Lambda
(see (
7.11
)) is positive then extinction occurs.

Proof.

We start by verifying
1
-
4
. We note that in the notation of
Section

6
we have
m=2
,
d=1
,
u=I
denotes the species which persistence we investigate, and
z=S
represents the auxiliary species. Since the Laplacian is self-adjoint,
1
follows from
6.4
. For
2
we have (ignoring the aforementioned
c
)

f_{1}(I,S)=\Big(-(\eta+\delta+\sigma)+\frac{\beta S}{1+c_{1}S+c_{2}I+c_{3}SI},-\eta-\frac{\beta I}{1+c_{1}S+c_{2}I+c_{3}SI}\Big)

and
f_{2}(I,S)=(0,\lambda)
, so
2
is easily verified.
3
also follows with
\sigma(I,S)=(I,S)
,
Hu_{1}=(1,0)
,
Hu_{2}=(0,1)
, and
Hu_{n}=0
for all other
n
. Indeed, (
6.17
) holds with
p=2
. Finally, all of our terms are of linear order or lower, so
4
(ii)
holds, and (
6.69
) follows from
Neumann boundary conditions and integration by parts:

\langle Ax+F(x),1\rangle=\int_{D}d_{1}\Delta S+d_{2}\Delta I+\lambda-\eta S-(\eta+\delta+\sigma)I\leq\lambda-\eta\|(I,S)\|_{L^{1}}\,.

Next, we claim that (
7.11
) is equal to
-\tilde{\lambda}
when
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
.
Indeed, by (
7.10
), the equation for
\tilde{S}
is independent of
\tilde{I}
. If
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
, then
\tilde{S}_{E}=\lambda/\eta
is the only equilibrium of

\partial_{t}\tilde{S}=d_{1}\tilde{\Delta}S+\lambda-\eta\tilde{S}\,.

because, for example by the energy method, we obtain
\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{S}_{t}=\lambda/\eta
(see also
7.15
).
Thus, all invariant measures
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
when
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
satisfy
\mu(\{\tilde{S}=\lambda/\eta\})=1
.

Replacing
\tilde{S}
with
\lambda/\eta
in (
7.11
) and using
\int_{D}\tilde{I}=\|\tilde{I}\|_{L^{1}}=1
when
(\tilde{S},\tilde{I})\sim\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
, we indeed obtain
\Lambda=-\tilde{\lambda}
when
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
.

Finally, the claim follows from
6.55
and
6.59
(applied to
6.55
).

∎

Remark 7.15
.

Notice that the value of
\Lambda
when the noise terms are dropped is the smallest eigenvalue (the only eigenvalue with nonnegative eigenvector) of the operator
-[d_{2}\Delta-(\eta+\delta+\sigma)+\frac{\beta\lambda}{\eta+c_{1}\lambda}]
. See also the proof of
7.1
, where this phenomena is illustrated directly, and
7.4
, which gives an intuitive explanation.

Remark 7.16
.

Observe that if our “functional response”
\beta SI/(1+c_{1}S+c_{2}I+c_{3}SI)
was replaced with
\beta SI/(S+I)
, which is the one considered in
[
NNY20
]
, then technically
2
would not hold because
xy/(x+y)
is not continuously differentiable. In other words, we cannot linearize the problem. However, we believe our results should still apply in some sense. For example, consider
[
NNY20
, (2.4)]
. Introduce a parameter
\epsilon\geq 0
and consider

(7.12)

\displaystyle dS(t)

\displaystyle=\Big[A_{1}S(t)+\Lambda-\mu_{1}S(t)-\frac{\alpha S(t)I(t)}{S(t)+I(t)}\Big]dt+S(t)dW_{1}(t)

\displaystyle dI(t)

\displaystyle=\Big[A_{2}I(t)-\mu_{2}I(t)+\frac{\alpha S(t)I(t)}{S(t)+I(t)+\epsilon}\Big]dt+I(t)dW_{2}(t)

The corresponding process
X_{t}=(S_{t},I_{t},\epsilon)
satisfies
1
-
3
,
5
(i)
-
(iii)
, and
6
on the state space
\mathcal{M}=C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{2})_{+}\times[0,1]
. Thus, its set of invariant measures is compact, its empirical occupation measures are almost surely tight, it is Feller, etc. If
\sup_{\epsilon\in(0,\overline{\epsilon})}\Lambda_{\epsilon}<0
, then (
7.12
) is stochastically persistent with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{0}=C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R})_{+}\times\{0\}
for all fixed
\epsilon\in(0,\overline{\epsilon})
. We believe this should imply that it is also persistent for
\epsilon=0
. Such statement requires proving an extra ‘uniform’ tightness of
\mu_{t}
on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
. It would be interesting to further explore this property, because in general a sort of uniform tightness of
\mu_{t}
should also give us a rate of convergence of
\mu_{t}
to
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
(see for example
[
Ben23
, Theorem 4.11, Theorem 4.13]
for a result in the finite-dimensional case).

7.4.
Stochastic Competitive Lotka-Volterra

Consider the population of
m
species
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}
evolving on some domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
which is connected, open, bounded, and has smooth boundary. Assume
a_{ii}:\overline{D}\to(0,\infty)
,
a_{ij}:\overline{D}\to[0,\infty)
,
m_{i}:\overline{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\sigma_{i}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
are all differentiable with bounded Lipschitz derivative and additionally,
\sigma_{i}(0)=0
. Fix constants
d_{i}>0
and suppose that the driving noise
W_{i}(t)
is given by independent cylindrical
Q
-Wiener processes (
Q
is the covariance operator):

W_{i}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{ik}e_{k}w_{ik}(t)\,,

where
w_{ik}(t)
are independent standard (one-dimensional) Brownian motions, the functions
\{e_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
form an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(D)
consisting of eigenfunctions of
\Delta
, and either
N>1
and
\lambda_{ik}\geq 0
satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{ik}^{p}\|e_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}<\infty

for some
p\in[2,2N/(N-2))
, or
N=1
and
\sup_{ik}|\lambda_{ik}|<\infty
.

Remark 7.17
.

If
p=2
then
W_{i}
is a finite-trace Q-Wiener process (it is a Hilbert-Schmidt embedding of a cylindrical Wiener process). If
N=1
and
\lambda_{ik}=1
, then
W_{i}
is a cylindrical Wiener process, so
dW_{i}(t)/dt
represents space-time white noise. Otherwise, we have something in between. See
[
NY21
, Section 8]
for further discussion about the choice of noise, and notice that our assumption is weaker than
[
NY21
, Hypothesis 8.1]
because we do not assume that
\{e_{ik}\}
is equibounded in
L^{\infty}
.

Our SPDE is as follows:

(7.13)

dx_{i}=\Big[d_{i}\Delta x_{i}+x_{i}\Big(m_{i}-a_{ii}x_{i}-\sum_{j\neq i}a_{ij}x_{j}\Big)\Big]dt+\sigma_{i}(x_{i})dW_{i}(t)\,,

where
x_{i}
satisfies Neumann boundary conditions (
dx_{i}/dn=0
on
\partial D
, where
n
is the outward pointing normal vector to
\partial D
).

Then (
7.13
) induces a Markov process on
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})_{+}
, the set of nonnegative continuous
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions on
\overline{D}
(this is a consequence of
6.35
). We investigate when the species can coexist, meaning that the following set is a “repeller”:

\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{M}\mid x_{i}=0\text{ for some }i=1,\dots,m\}\,.

As in the finite-dimensional cases like SDEs and discrete time Markov chains (see
[
HN18
,
BS19
,
HNC21
]
), this question can be answered by considering the “invasion rates”
r_{i}(\mu)
defined below. Intuitively, we imagine that a species
i
is extinct and other species are in a steady state
\mu
. Then,
the
i
th invasion rate measures the average exponential rate of growth of the
i
th population if a small amount of species
i
is introduced into the ecosystem.
The exponential rate of growth can be obtained by formally applying Itô’s formula to the function
\ln{\int_{D}x_{i}}
, where
\int_{D}x_{i}=\|x_{i}\|_{L^{1}}
is the total population of species
i
, and then to obtain
r_{i}(\mu)
we take average according to the measure
\mu
. If the species was not extinct we define the invasion rate to be zero. We arrive at the following definition, which should be compared to
[
HNC21
, (2.3) and Proposition 3.1]
.

Definition 7.18
.

Fix
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
(see
2.13
),
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, and suppose
\mu(\{x_{k}=0\})=1

Let
\tilde{x}
be a process satisfying

\displaystyle d\tilde{x}_{k}

\displaystyle=[d_{k}\Delta\tilde{x}_{k}+\tilde{x}_{k}(m_{k}-\sum_{j\neq k}a_{kj}\tilde{x}_{j})]dt+\sigma_{k}^{\prime}(0)\tilde{x}_{k}dW_{k}(t)

\displaystyle d\tilde{x}_{i}

\displaystyle=[d_{i}\Delta\tilde{x}_{i}+\tilde{x}_{i}(m_{i}-a_{ii}\tilde{x}_{i}-\sum_{j\neq i,k}a_{ij}\tilde{x}_{j})]dt+\sigma_{i}(\tilde{x}_{i})dW_{i}(t)\qquad i\neq k\,.

Define

(7.14)

r_{k}(\mu)\coloneqq\inf_{\nu\in P_{k}(\mu)}\nu\Big(\int_{D}v_{k}\Big(m_{k}-\sum_{j\neq k}a_{kj}\tilde{x}_{j}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{k}^{\prime}(0)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{ki}^{2}\Big(\int_{D}e_{ki}v_{k}\Big)^{2}\Big)\,,

where
P_{k}(\mu)
denotes the set of all invariant measures (see
2.13
)
\nu
for the process
(v_{k},\tilde{x}_{-k})\coloneqq(\tilde{x}_{k}/\|\tilde{x}_{k}\|_{L^{1}},\tilde{x}_{-k})
satisfying
\phi^{*}\nu=\mu
. Here,
\phi
is given by
\phi(v,x_{-k})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},0,x_{k+1},\dots,x_{m})
,
\phi^{*}\nu(A)=\nu(\phi^{-1}(A))
, and
x_{-k}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{m})
.

For a general
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
, by invariance there is
p\in[0,1]
and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
such that
\mu_{1}(\{x_{k}=0\})=1
,
\mu_{2}(\{x_{k}=0\})=0
, and
\mu=p\mu_{1}+(1-p)\mu_{2}
. Then we
we define
r_{k}(\mu)=pr_{k}(\mu_{1})
.

Remark 7.19
.

If
\mu
is ergodic then
\mu(\{x_{k}=0\})\in\{0,1\}
because
\{x_{k}=0\}
is an invariant set for (
7.13
). This is intuitively clear and follows rigorously from
6.31
(ii)
with
R=1
. Indeed, we will prove below that
1
-
3
hold for (
7.13
). Thus, we could also define
r_{k}(\mu)
for ergodic
\mu
first and then in general via ergodic decomposition.

Remark 7.20
.

To provide intuition for
7.18
, note that the expression inside of
\nu()
in (
7.14
) is identical to
\mathcal{L}\ln\|x_{k}\|_{L^{1}}
when
x_{k}=0
, modulo the tildes, provided we identify
v_{k}
with
x_{k}/\|x_{k}\|_{L^{1}}
.

This is a valid identification because writing the equation for
x_{k}/\|x_{k}\|_{L^{1}}
and then setting
x_{k}=0
, yields the same result as if we first linearized the equation for
x_{k}
. Hence, formally when
x_{k}=0
we have
x_{k}/\|x_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=\tilde{x}_{k}/\|\tilde{x}_{k}\|_{L^{1}}
and the equations for
\tilde{x}_{i}
are obtained simply by setting
x_{k}=0
.

Note that we are averaging over
\nu\in P_{k}(\mu)
as opposed to
\mu
. The reason is that before we were looking at an invariant measure
\mu
where
x_{k}
had only one possible value (
0
). However, the expression we need to average contains
v_{k}
, and since
\|x_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=0
the polar decomposition
x_{k}=\|x_{k}\|_{L^{1}}v_{k}
leaves many possible options for
v_{k}
(any
v_{k}\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R})_{+}
with
\|v_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=1
). Thus, we must consider all possible stationary behaviors for
v_{k}
given that the other species are still distributed according to
\mu
. This detail was not encountered in the SDE or discrete-time Markov chain case where
x_{k}/x_{k}
has only one possible value (
1
) since each
x_{k}
lives in the one-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}
as opposed to the infinite-dimensional space
C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R})_{+}
.

Remark 7.21
.

Below we list some common cases for the noise which simplify the formula (
7.14
).
When
\lambda_{ki}=1
(space-time white noise), by Plancherel’s theorem

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{ki}^{2}\Big(\int_{D}e_{ki}v_{k}\Big)^{2}=\|v_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,.

Similarly if
\lambda_{ki}=\lambda_{i}^{\alpha/2}
, where
\lambda_{i}
is the
i
th eigenvalue of the Laplacian (cylindrical Brownian motion on the Sobolev space
H^{\alpha}(D)
), then

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{ki}^{2}\Big(\int_{D}e_{ki}v_{k}\Big)^{2}=\|v_{k}\|_{H^{\alpha}}^{2}\,,

where
H^{\alpha}
is the
\alpha
-Sobolev norm. If the noise terms are standard Brownian motions, meaning
\lambda_{ki}=1
for
i=1
and
\lambda_{ki}=0
otherwise, then
e_{k1}=1
for all
k
and
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{ki}^{2}\Big(\int_{D}e_{ki}v_{k}\Big)^{2}=1\,,

because
\|v\|_{L^{1}}=1
.

Remark 7.22
.

In the SDE case there is no
\inf
in (
7.14
) because
L^{1}(D)\cong\mathbb{R}
when
D
is a singleton (no spatial dependence) which forces
v_{k}=1
. Since we have spatial dependence, the simplex in
L^{1}(D)
is a much richer space and the set
P_{k}(\mu)
is needed. However, as mentioned in the introduction, it may be possible to use a contraction argument as in
[
BLSS25
,
Bus86
,
CF24
]
to show that the
\nu
in (
7.14
) is unique (
P_{k}(\mu)
is singleton), and then

\inf
could be removed.

Theorem 7.23
.

Suppose that
\max_{i=1,\dots,m}r_{i}(\mu)>0
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
. Then coexistence (persistence) occurs in (
7.13
). Specifically,

•

There is an invariant measure
\mu_{+}
for (
7.13
) with
\mu_{+}(\mathcal{M}_{0})=0
.

•

For all
\delta>0
there are
0<b<B<\infty
such that for any nonnegative initial condition
u\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}
,

\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}m(\{t\leq T\mid b\leq\int_{D}x_{i}(t)\leq B\text{ for all }i\})\geq 1-\delta

almost surely.

Remark 7.24
.

It follows from
6.59
that the invasion rates are continuous in
m_{i},a_{ij},\sigma_{i}
, so in fact coexistence still occurs if we slightly perturb these functions (compare to
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.8]
). Also, in the deterministic case (
\sigma_{i}=0
) it may be possible to give more explicit descriptions of the invasion rates, for example in terms of solutions to eigenvalue problems. Specifically, if
\sigma_{k}=0
then any
\nu\in P_{k}(\mu)
(see
7.18
) satisfies
\nu(\{v_{k}=v^{*}\})=1
, where
v^{*}
is the nonnegative eigenvector of a linear PDE for
\tilde{x}_{k}
. Then one could use similar methods to
[
HNC21
, Section 5.5.]
to calculate the invasion rates, at least when
m_{i},a_{ij}
are constants. It would be interesting to see how this aligns with the results in
[
LL22
]
for corresponding deterministic models since, combined with the continuous dependence on
\sigma_{i}
, the results would extend to the case of small noise. We already illustrated such an argument in
7.13
for the SIR epidemic model, so we skip the details here, but we encourage an intersted reader to calculate and/or bound
r_{k}(\mu)
for specific examples.

Proof.

To use
6.58
, we first verify
1
-
4
. Since there is no parameter
\theta
we do not include it in the notation.
1
follows from the Laplacian being self-adjoint (see
6.4
) and
A_{i}=d_{i}\Delta-c
, where
c>0
is large enough. For
2
we have
f_{2}=0
and
(f_{1}(\cdot,x))_{i}=c+m_{i}(\cdot)-a_{ii}(\cdot)x_{i}-\sum_{j\neq i}a_{ij}(\cdot)x_{j}
, so
2
is satisfied for
x_{t}=(x_{i}(t),x_{-i}(t))
for any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
.
3
is satisfied with
\sigma(\cdot,x)=(\sigma_{1}(x_{1}),\dots,\sigma_{m}(x_{m}))
,
\mathcal{U}=(l^{2})^{m}
, and
Hu_{ik}=\lambda_{ik}\tilde{e}_{ik}
, where
\{\{u_{ik}\}_{i=1,\dots,m}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
is an orthonormal basis for
(l^{2})^{m}
and
\tilde{e}_{ik}
is the function in
C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})
with
i
th coordinate equal to
e_{k}
and all other coordinates equal to
0
(indeed,
\{\tilde{e}_{ik}\}
forms an orthonormal basis for
(L^{2}(D))^{m}
).

It remains to verify
4
. First (
6.69
) follows from

\displaystyle\langle Ax+F(x),1\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle xf_{1}(x),1\rangle\leq K-\sum_{i}a_{ii}\langle x_{i}^{2},1\rangle-\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}\langle x_{i}x_{j},1\rangle\leq K-\tilde{c}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq K-\tilde{c}\|x\|_{L^{1}}\,,

where we used
a_{ii}>0
on
\overline{D}
,
x_{i},a_{i,j}\geq 0
, and Young’s inequality.

To check
4
(i)
we use ideas from
7.12
, in particular, recall (
7.7
) (the derivation did not use any conditions specific to that example).
As in (
7.7
), we have

\langle f,g\rangle_{\beta}=C(\beta)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-2\beta-1}e^{-ct}\langle\mathcal{P}_{t}f,g\rangle dt\,,

where
C(\beta)>0
is a constant and
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is the (contraction) semigroup generated by
Ax=(d_{1}\Delta x_{1},\dots,d_{m}\Delta x_{m})
on
C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})
with Neumann boundary conditions (
\mathcal{P}_{t}x
is the solution to
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{P}_{t}x=A\mathcal{P}_{t}x
). As
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is positivity preserving, we conclude that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\beta}
is monotone in each argument (for nonnegative
f,g
). Thus, for every
\epsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle\langle x_{i},F_{i}^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle\geq\langle x_{i},-cx_{i}-m_{i}x_{i}+a_{ii}x_{i}^{2}\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle\geq\langle x_{i},\epsilon^{-1}x_{i}-K\rangle_{\beta,2}

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon^{-1}\|x_{i}\|_{\beta,2}^{2}-K\|x_{i}\|_{\beta,2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{2}\|x_{i}\|_{\beta,2}^{2}-K

for constants
K=K(\epsilon)>0
which are allowed to change from line to line, and therefore
\|x_{i}\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
vanishes over
\langle x_{i},F_{i}^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}
. Summing over
i
proves
4
(i)
.

Finally, we need to reconcile
7.18
with (
6.91
). If
\mu(\{x_{k}=0\})=1
, then
7.18
agrees with (
6.91
) by exactly the same reasoning as why
6.52
is the same as (
6.90
) (see the paragraph right below (
6.90
)).

When
\mu(\{x_{k}=0\})=0
, then
r_{k}(\mu)=0
in
7.18
and
in (
6.91
) we have

r_{k}(\mu)=\mu H=0
if
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
, where we used
4.13
applied in the setting of
6.36
with
x_{t}=(x_{k}(t),x_{-k}(t))
(
V
is as in the proof of
6.55
, in which
1
-
6
are verified, so we may indeed apply
4.13
).

∎

Remark 7.25
.

Our results also apply to predator-prey systems with minor adjustments. For example, if we considered an SPDE version of
[
HNC21
, (5.20)]
, then
4
(i)
technically does not hold because
\langle x,F^{-1}(x)\rangle_{\beta}
contains terms like
\langle x_{3}-x_{1},a_{13}x_{1}x_{3}\rangle_{\beta}
. This can be bypassed by using
\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
instead of
\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|x_{i}\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
. Indeed, in this case
\langle x,F^{-1}(x)\rangle_{\beta}
is replaced by
\langle\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{m}F_{i}^{-}(x)\rangle_{\beta}
, and then terms
a_{13}x_{1}x_{3}
and
-a_{13}x_{1}x_{3}
in
\sum_{i=1}^{m}F_{i}^{-}(x)
cancel (as in the calculation of
\langle F(x),1\rangle
). This also applies to the SDE case; we would
use
(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3})^{2}
as
a Lyapunov function instead of
x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}
in
[
HNC21
, (5.20)]
. We decided, for the sake of brevity and clarity, not to include a third case to
4
, which would have been quite similar to
4
(i)
. We are confident that all of the arguments are still valid with this minor change and if we were to write this paper again we would use
\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
in place of
\|x\|_{\beta,2}^{2}
for
4
(i)
.

Remark 7.26
.

Our results will also apply when
d_{i}
are allowed to be functions on
D
. However, we avoided this technicality because then we would have to use a measure (different in each component, see
Section

7.1
) other than Lebesgue measure on
D
to make the operator
d_{i}\Delta
self-adjoint. Our general results assume each operator
d_{i}\Delta
is self-adjoint with respect to the same measure, but all results in
Section

6
follow without a change if we use spaces like
L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\mu_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\mu_{m})
as opposed to
L^{p}(D,\Sigma,\mu;\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
